Quantization of the Willmore Energy in Riemannian Manifolds
Alexis Michelat, Andrea Mondino

Abstract
We show that the quantization of energy for Willmore spheres into closed Riemannian manifolds holds provided that the Willmore energy and the area are uniformly bounded. The analogous energy quantization result holds for Willmore surfaces of arbitrary genus, under the additional assumptions that the immersion maps weakly converge to a limiting (possibly branched, weak immersion) map from the same surface, and that the conformal structures stay in a compact domain of the moduli space.



Mathematical subject classification :

53C42, 53A30, 49Q10

1
Introduction

Let
m\geq 3
and
(M^{m},h)
be a compact Riemannian manifold. For every smooth immersion
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow M^{m}
, the (conformal) Willmore energy is defined by

\displaystyle W(\vec{\Phi})=\int_{\Sigma}\left(|\vec{H}|^{2}+K_{h}(\vec{\Phi}_{\ast}T\Sigma)\right)d\mathrm{vol}_{g},

where
g=\vec{\Phi}^{\ast}h
is the induced metric by
\vec{\Phi}
on
\Sigma
,
K_{h}(\vec{\Phi}_{\ast}T\Sigma)
is the sectional curvature of the two-plan induced by
\vec{\Phi}
, and
\vec{H}
is the mean curvature
vector, defined by

\displaystyle\vec{H}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}g^{i,j}\,\vec{\mathbb{I}}_{i,j}\,,

where
\vec{\mathbb{I}}_{i,j}
is the second fundamental form. This functional, first introduced in the Euclidean space by Poisson in 1814 (
[
45
]
, see also the work of Sophie Germain
[
11
]
) in the context of non-linear elasticity, was rediscovered by Blaschke and Thomsen
[
5
]
in the 1920’s in the
framework of conformal geometry and by Willmore
[
58
]
in
1965
.

A key property of such a Lagrangian is the conformal invariance.
Furthermore, it has been recently proved in
[
37
]
that the Willmore functional is the unique (up to linear combinations with topological terms)
conformally invariant
integral curvature energy for surfaces (in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, it was already known that
(H^{2}-K_{g})d\mathrm{vol}_{g}
is up to scaling the only
pointwise
conformally invariant
2
-form).

In addition to the aforementioned strong connection with conformal geometry, the Willmore functional in curved ambient spaces has remarkable links with other topics in mathematics and physics. For instance, the Willmore energy is the main term of the Hawking mass
[
13
]
in the framework of general relativity (see for instance
[
23
,
10
,
40
]
), moreover it corresponds to the
main term of the Nambu-Gotō action
in string theory
[
46
]

and the renormalised area functional in the AdS/CFT correspondence

[
1
,
2
]
.

From the point of view of calculus of variations, as well as motivated by the aforementioned connections to physics, it is natural to investigate the existence and the properties of the
Willmore immersions
that are by definition the critical points of the Willmore energy. The standard first variation formulae show that critical points of
W
satisfy the Euler-Lagrange equation

\displaystyle\Delta_{g}^{\perp}\vec{H}-2|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}+\mathscr{A}(\vec{H})+\mathscr{R}_{1}^{\perp}(\vec{H})-2\,\widetilde{K}_{h}\,\vec{H}+2\,\mathscr{R}_{2}(d\vec{\Phi})+(DR)(d\vec{\Phi})=0\,,

(1.1)

where
\mathscr{A}
is the Simons operator, and the other terms are curvature functionals defined by

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}(\vec{w})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}\left(\langle\vec{e}_{i},\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w}\rangle+\langle\vec{e}_{j},\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{w}\rangle\right)\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\vec{e}_{i},\vec{e}_{j})

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{1}^{\perp}(\vec{w})

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{i})\vec{e}_{i}\right)^{\perp}

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{h}

\displaystyle=K_{h}(\vec{\Phi}_{\ast}T\Sigma)

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{2}(d\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\langle R(\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\vec{e}_{i},\vec{e}_{1}),\vec{e}_{2})\vec{e}_{2},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle+\langle R(\vec{e}_{1},\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\vec{e}_{i},\vec{e}_{2}))\vec{e}_{2},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle(DR)(d\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\langle(\nabla_{\vec{v}_{j}}R)(\vec{e}_{1},\vec{e}_{2})\vec{e}_{2},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\vec{v}_{j}\,,

where
(\vec{e}_{1},\vec{e}_{2})
is a orthonormal moving frame of
\vec{\Phi}_{\ast}(T\Sigma)
,
(\vec{v}_{1},\cdots,\vec{v}_{m})
is a local orthonormal frame of
TM^{m}
, and
R
is the Riemann curvature tensor
of the ambient space
(M^{m},h)
.

The literature about Willmore immersions in Riemannian manifolds (other than
\mathbb{R}^{n}
or, equivalently by conformal invariance,
S^{n}
) is relatively recent
and in expansion. The first existence results for Willmore spheres have been obtained in perturbative settings by the second author
[
34
,
35
]
. Under the area-constraint condition, the existence and the geometric properties of Willmore-type spheres have been investigated by Lamm-Metzger-Schulze
[
23
]
, Lamm-Metzger
[
21
,
22
]
, Laurain and the second author

[
24
]
and Eichmair-Körber
[
10
]
. Area-constrained Willmore tori of small area have been recently constructed by Ikoma, Malchiodi and the second author
[
16
,
17
]
. All the aforementioned results are perturbative in nature,
i.e.
either the surfaces have sufficiently small area, or the ambient Riemannian metric is sufficiently close to either the Euclidean or the spherical metrics.

The global problem to study the existence of smooth immersed spheres
minimising quadratic curvature functionals in Riemannian manifolds
was studied by the second author in collaboration with
Kuwert and Schygulla
[
20
]
adapting Simon’s ambient approach
[
53
]
, and by the second author with Rivière
[
38
,
39
]
via a parametric approach, proving
the existence of area-constrained Willmore spheres in homotopy
classes as well as the existence of Willmore spheres under various assumptions and constraints. Also, Chen-Li
[
7
]
proved the existence of stratified weak branched immersions of arbitrary genus minimising quadratic curvature functionals under various constraints (for weak immersions, refer to
[
41
,
57
]
and to
[
50
,
27
]
for works more in relationship with Willmore surfaces).

The main goal of this article is to generalise the quantization result of Bernard-Rivière
[
4
]
to the case for Willmore immersions in Riemannian manifolds. This result should be seen as the first step to generalise Rivière’s min-max theory for Willmore spheres
[
51
]
to immersions with values into closed Riemannian manifolds. This extension is natural since, by conformal invariance of the Willmore energy, the quantization result in Euclidean spaces is equivalent to the energy quantization in the sphere
S^{n}
(for
n\geq 3
) equipped with its standard round metric.

Theorem A
.

Let
(M^{m},h)
be a smooth compact Riemannian manifold
of dimension
m\geq 3
, and let
\{\vec{\Phi}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathrm{Imm}(S^{2},M^{m})
be a sequence of Willmore immersions.
Assume that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\limsup\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}W(\vec{\Phi}_{k})<\infty\\
&\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Area}(\vec{\Phi}_{k})<\infty.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.2)

Then, up to a subsequence, the following energy identity holds

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}W_{(M^{m},h)}(\vec{\Phi}_{k})

\displaystyle=W_{(M^{m},h)}(\vec{\Phi}_{\infty})+\sum_{j=1}^{u}W_{(M^{m},h)}(\vec{\Psi}_{j})+\sum_{s=1}^{p}W_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}(\vec{\eta}_{s})+\sum_{t=1}^{q}\left(W_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}(\vec{\zeta}_{t})-4\pi\theta_{0,t}\right)\,,

(1.3)

where:

(1)

The map
\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}
is a smooth Willmore immersion of
S^{2}
into
(M^{m},h)
, possibly branched at finitely many points
a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}\in S^{2}
.

(2)

For any
j=1,\ldots,u\in\mathbb{N},
the maps
\vec{\Psi}_{j}
are smooth, possibly branched, Willmore immersions of
S^{2}
into
(M^{m},h)
.

(3)

For any
s=1,\ldots,p\in\mathbb{N},\,t=1,\ldots,q\in\mathbb{N},
the maps
\vec{\eta}_{s}:S^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\vec{\zeta}_{t}:S^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
are smooth, possibly branched, Willmore immersions in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\theta_{0,t}=\theta_{0}(\vec{\zeta}_{t},x_{t})\in\mathbb{N}
is the multiplicity of
\vec{\zeta}_{t}
at some point
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

(4)

The map
\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}:S^{2}\to M^{m}
is obtained as follows: there exist a sequence of diffeomorphisms
\{f_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of
S^{2}
such that
\vec{\Phi}_{k}\circ f_{k}
is conformal and

\vec{\Phi}_{k}\circ f_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}\quad\text{ in }C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{2}\setminus\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}\right\}),\quad\forall l\in\mathbb{N}.

Furthermore, it holds

\lim_{k\to\infty}W_{(M^{m},h)}(\vec{\Phi}_{k})=W_{(M^{m},h)}(\vec{\Phi}_{\infty})\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\vec{\Phi}_{k}\circ f_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}\text{ in }C^{l}(\Sigma),\quad\forall l\in\mathbb{N}.

Moreover, if
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Area}(\vec{\Phi}_{k})=0
, then the first two terms in the right hand side of (
1.3
) are not present,
i.e.
using the same notation as above for
\vec{\eta}_{s},\,\vec{\zeta}_{t}
and
\theta_{0,t}
, it holds

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}W_{(M^{m},h)}(\vec{\Phi}_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{p}W_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}(\vec{\eta}_{s})+\sum_{t=1}^{q}\left(W_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}(\vec{\zeta}_{t})-4\pi\theta_{0,t}\right)\,.

Remarks 1.2
.

Let us denote
\vec{\xi}_{k}=\vec{\Phi}_{k}\circ f_{k}
, where
f_{k}
is given by (4) in Theorem
A
.

—

The
Riemannian Willmore bubbles

\vec{\Psi}_{j}:S^{2}\to M^{m}
are obtained as follows: for any
j\in\{1,\ldots,u\}
, there exist a sequence of positive Möbius transformations
\psi^{j}_{k}
of
S^{2}
concentrating at one of
\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}\right\}
such that:

\vec{\xi}_{k}\circ\psi^{j}_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\vec{\Psi}_{j}\quad\text{ in }C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{2}\setminus\big\{a_{1}^{j},\cdots,a^{j}_{N_{j}}\big\}),\quad\forall l\in\mathbb{N},

where
\big\{a_{1}^{j},\cdots,a^{j}_{N_{j}}\big\}
is a finite set of points in
S^{2}
.

—

The
Euclidean Willmore bubbles

\vec{\eta}_{s},\vec{\zeta}_{t}:S^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
are obtained by the following blow up procedure: for any
s\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
(resp. for any
t\in\{1,\ldots,q\}
), there exists a point
\overline{x}^{s}\in M
(resp.
\overline{x}^{t}\in M
), there exist a sequence of positive Möbius transformations
\psi^{s}_{k}
(resp.
\psi^{t}_{k}
) of
S^{2}
concentrating at one of
\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}\right\}
, a sequence of rescalings
\lambda^{s}_{k}\to+\infty
(resp.
\lambda^{t}_{k}\to+\infty
) and inversions
\Xi^{t}_{k}
of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that:

\lambda^{s}_{k}\cdot{\rm Exp}_{\overline{x}^{s}}^{-1}\circ\vec{\xi}_{k}\circ\psi^{s}_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\vec{\eta}_{s}\quad\text{ in }C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{2}\setminus\big\{a_{1}^{s},\cdots,a^{s}_{N_{s}}\big\}),\quad\forall l\in\mathbb{N},

and, respectively,

\Xi^{t}_{k}\circ\lambda^{t}_{k}\cdot{\rm Exp}_{\overline{x}^{t}}^{-1}\circ\vec{\xi}_{k}\circ\psi^{t}_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\vec{\zeta}_{t}\quad\text{ in }C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{2}\setminus\left\{a_{1}^{t},\cdots,a^{t}_{N_{t}}\right\}),\quad\forall l\in\mathbb{N},

where
\big\{a_{1}^{s},\cdots,a^{s}_{N_{s}}\big\},\big\{a_{1}^{t},\cdots,a^{t}_{N_{t}}\big\}\subset S^{2}
are finite sets of points.

Arguing along the lines of the proof of Theorem
A
, one can prove the energy quantization for surfaces of arbitrary genus, under the assumption of
\mathrm{W}^{2,2}
weak convergence to a limit map and a bound on the conformal structures; the reader is referred to Theorem
6.1
for the precise statement.

Remarks 1.3
.

(1)

Since the Gauss curvature is quantized as well
(see (
6.4
), and refer to
[
4
, Lemma V.
1
]
), the quantization of energy stated in Theorem
A
and Theorem
6.1
also holds for a general quadratic curvature functional of the form

\displaystyle F_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(\vec{\Phi})=\lambda_{1}\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{H}|^{2}\,d\mathrm{vol}_{g}+\lambda_{2}\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{h}_{0}|^{2}_{WP\,}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,,

for some
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\vec{h}_{0}
is the Weingarten tensor (see for example the form introduced by Calabi in
[
6
]
) and
|\,\cdot\,|_{WP}
the Weil-Petersson metric. Explicitly, we have in a conformal local chart
\vec{h}_{0}=2\,\pi_{\vec{n}}(\partial_{z}^{2}\vec{\Phi})dz^{2}=2e^{2\lambda}\,\partial_{z}\left(e^{-2\lambda}\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}\right)dz^{2}
, and for two
2
-forms
\alpha=\varphi(z)dz^{2}
and
\beta=\psi(z)dz^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{WP}=e^{-4\lambda}\varphi(z)\overline{\psi(z)}=g^{-2}\otimes\alpha\otimes\overline{\beta}.

Indeed, the quantity

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}K_{g_{k}}d\mathrm{vol}_{g_{k}}

is equal to
2\pi\chi(\Sigma)
in the limit
by Gauss-Bonnet Theorem and the smooth convergence of the conformal structures.

(2)

In
[
4
]
the boundedness of the area is not explicitly assumed because the authors work in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
; but making a stereographic projection and using the conformal invariance of the Willmore energy, their result is equivalent to the quantization of energy for
S^{n}
-valued maps, where
S^{n}
is equipped with its standard round metric. Indeed, for all immersion
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow S^{n}
, the Willmore energy in the sphere is defined by

\displaystyle W_{S^{n}}(\vec{\Phi})=\int_{\Sigma}\left(1+|\vec{H}|^{2}\right)d\mathrm{vol}_{g},

which shows in particular that a uniform bound on the Willmore energy implies a uniform bound on the area.

(3)

Consider the following assumption.

Assumption 1.4
.

The sectional curvature
K_{h}
of the ambient manifold
(M,h)
is bounded below by
\kappa_{0}>0
,
i.e.

there exists
\kappa_{0}>0
such that
K_{h}(P)\geq\kappa_{0}
for any
2
-dimensional non-isotropic tangent plane
P\in\mathscr{G}_{2}(TM)
, where

\displaystyle K_{h}(P)=\frac{\langle R(\vec{v},\vec{w})\vec{w},\vec{v}\rangle}{|\vec{v}|^{2}|\vec{w}|^{2}-\langle\vec{v},\vec{w}\rangle^{2}},

where
P=\vec{v}\wedge\vec{w}
.

The assumption
1.4
implies that
for any immersion
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow(M^{m},h)
, it holds

\displaystyle\mathrm{Area}(\vec{\Phi})\leq\frac{1}{\kappa_{0}}\int_{\Sigma}K_{h}(\vec{\Phi}_{\ast}T\Sigma)\,d\mathrm{vol}_{g}\leq\frac{1}{\kappa_{0}}\int_{\Sigma}\left(|\vec{H}|^{2}+K_{h}(\vec{\Phi}_{\ast}T\Sigma)\right)d\mathrm{vol}_{g}\leq\frac{1}{\kappa_{0}}W(\vec{\Phi}),

which implies in particular that the bound on the area follows
once a Willmore energy bound is in place. In particular, the theorem will hold for any small enough (in the
C^{2}
topology) perturbation of the round metric on
S^{n}

without the assumption on the uniform boundedness of the area
.

(4)

Given a closed manifold
M^{m}
, for a generic Riemannian metric
h
(see
[
42
]
), one can control the area of an immersion
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow(M^{m},h)
by its
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-curvature energy (
[
3
]
; see also
[
36
]
)

F_{1,1}(\vec{\Phi})=\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{\mathbb{I}}|^{2}\,d\mathrm{vol}_{g}.

Therefore, the area bound in the assumption (
1.2
) can be dropped for a generic metric on a closed ambient manifold, when considering the quantization of energy for a functional
F_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}
, with
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}>0
.

Some ideas of the proofs

Related literature on energy quantization and general strategy

The main results Theorem
A
and Theorem
6.1
shall be read in the context of other bubble-neck decomposition and energy quantization results, previously obtained in the literature. One can mention
[
52
,
56
,
18
,
9
,
44
,
30
,
47
]

in the setting of harmonic maps and other conformally invariant variational problems.
A fundamental difference between the aforementioned energies and the Willmore functional is that, in the former, the corresponding Euler Lagrange equations are of second order, while the latter is a fourth-order problem.

As already mentioned, the first quantization result for the Willmore energy was obtained by Bernard-Rivière
[
4
]
for Willmore surfaces with bounded conformal structures and immersed in Euclidean ambient spaces. A first generalisation of
[
4
]
was established by Laurain-Rivière
[
26
]
in the case of Willmore immersions with degenerating conformal classes, still with values into
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The crux of the proof of the quantization of energy is to obtain the
no-neck energy
property, once a suitable decomposition of the domain is performed. The idea is not restricted to the Willmore energy and applies to any quadratic energy (the Dirichlet energy
[
30
]
, the Ginzburg-Landau energy
[
29
,
28
]
, the Euclidean Willmore energy
[
4
,
26
]
, horizontal
1/2
-harmonic maps
[
31
]
, etc). To fix ideas, consider an immersion
\vec{u}:(\Sigma,g)\rightarrow(M^{m},h)\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
between Riemannian manifolds (where we assume without loss of generality that
(M^{m},h)
is isometrically embedded into
\mathbb{R}^{N}
) and its Dirichlet energy given by

\displaystyle E(\vec{u})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}|d\vec{u}|_{g}^{2}\,d\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,.

A neck region is conformally equivalent to an annulus
\Omega=B_{R}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)
.We say that the
no-neck energy
property holds provided that for any neck-region
\Omega_{k}(1)=B_{R_{k}}\setminus\overline{B}_{r_{k}}(0)\subset\mathbb{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}|\nabla\vec{u}_{k}|^{2}dx=0\,,

(1.4)

where, for all
0<\alpha\leq 1
,
\Omega_{k}(\alpha)=B_{\alpha R_{k}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\alpha^{-1}r_{k}}(0)
.
The idea of the proof is to use Lorentz spaces (see the Appendix
7
for more details) and the duality between
\mathrm{L}^{2,1}
and
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}
, where
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}
is the weak
\mathrm{L}^{2}
space and
\mathrm{L}^{2,1}
its pre-dual which can be explicitly characterised. The duality implies in particular that for any measured space
(X,\mu)
and any measurable maps
\vec{u},\vec{v}:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
, it holds

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega}\langle\vec{u},\vec{v}\rangle\,d\mu\right|\leq\left\|\vec{u}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}\left\|\vec{v}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega)}.

(1.5)

The idea of the quantization is to first show that, for some
\alpha_{0}>0
independent of
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a uniform bound

\displaystyle\left\|d\vec{u}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}))}\leq C

(1.6)

holds.
Then, if one can prove a
weak
quantization of energy,
i.e.
that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left\|\nabla\vec{u}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}=0,

(1.7)

then the duality inequality (
1.5
), (
1.6
) and (
1.7
) show that the energy quantization in (
1.4
) holds. Indeed:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}|\nabla\vec{u}_{k}|^{2}dx\leq\left\|\nabla\vec{u}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\left\|\nabla\vec{u}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\leq C\left\|\nabla\vec{u}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}.

This approach was first developed by Lin-Rivière in the context of the Ginzburg-Landau functional
[
29
,
28
]
and for harmonic maps
[
30
]
. It was used more recently for Willmore immersions with values into
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by Bernard-Rivière
[
4
]
and Laurain-Rivière
[
26
]
.

Difficulties and novelties of this paper

Although we build on Bernard-Rivière’s work (
[
4
]
), new technical difficulties arise in the Riemannian setting. One key point is the need to introduce new Lorentz spaces that were not previously used in this context to our knowledge.

A fundamental ingredient in the proof of the energy quantization for Willmore immersions in Euclidean spaces
[
4
]
is the introduction of conservation laws by Rivière
[
48
]
: these permit to rewrite the fourth-order Willmore equation into a system of second-order Jacobian-type equations which can be handled with tools from integrability by compensation.

The first technical difficulty compared to
[
4
]
is that we do not get the existence of
such an exact system of conservation laws for any
critical immersion.
Indeed, it is necessary to assume that the area in small enough to get the existence
of a perturbed system of conservation laws (see
[
38
, Lemma A.1 and Lemma A.2]
), and Lemmas
3.6
and
3.15
in this paper.
The perturbation is caused by the ambient curvature, and makes the system to be complex-valued of non-pure Jacobians (rather than real-valued of pure Jacobians as in the Euclidean setting). This is why, in order to obtain the existence of the perturbed system of conservation laws in the neck region, we assume that the area is bounded and we prove that the area in neck-regions (and bubble regions too) is quantized, and therefore arbitrarily small.

Another technical point is to obtain a suitable
\varepsilon
-regularity result for Willmore immersions with values into
curved ambient spaces. We prove in Theorem
4.9
that there exists
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(M^{m},h)>0
with the following property: provided that
\vec{\Phi}:B(0,1)\rightarrow M^{m}
is a weak Willmore immersion, the estimate

\displaystyle\mathrm{Area}(\vec{\Phi}(B(0,1)))+\int_{B(0,1)}|\nabla\vec{n}|^{2}dx\leq\varepsilon_{0}

implies that
\vec{\Phi}\in C^{\infty}(B(0,1))
and, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
C_{k}<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{k}\vec{n}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B(0,\frac{1}{2}))}\leq C_{k}\left\|\nabla\vec{n}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))}.

Notice that by the previous Remark
1.3
(
3
)., the bound on area is superfluous for a generic metric on
M^{m}
,
or in case of ambient metric with positive sectional curvature.

As mentioned above, a key technical difficulty in this paper is that the conservation laws are complex-valued and the systems are non-pure Jacobians. This makes all the estimates more complicated: the additional terms coming from the non-vanishing Christoffel symbols force us to obtain
a priori
estimates on the functions appearing in the system, contrary to the Euclidean case where one can proceed directly from the existence and obtain the
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}
estimate
a posteriori
.

The main technical difficulty
is to obtain a pointwise
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}
bound
(see Theorem
3.16
). In order to prove it, we will introduce a generalised Lorentz (or Orlicz-Lorentz)
space modelled on
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}
and named
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\log^{\beta}}
(where
0\leq\beta\leq 1
and
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\log^{0}}=\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}
) in the analysis.
The reader is referred to Appendix
7
for more details on these Banach function spaces.

Another important step in the proof is to show that for holomorphic maps (the same proof works more generally for harmonic maps), the standard
\varepsilon
-regularity and scaling considerations giving that a
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}
bound implies locally a
\mathrm{W}^{1,1}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2,1}
estimate, hold
more generally when one has a
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\log^{\beta}}
bound (see Lemma
3.10
).
Since the more classical improvement
from
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}
to
\mathrm{W}^{1,1}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2,1}
for harmonic maps had several applications, it is natural to expect that the aforementioned sharpened improvement obtained in Lemma
3.10
will be useful also in other settings.

. The first author is supported by the Early Postdoc Mobility
Variational Methods in Geometric Analysis
P
2
EZP
2
_
191893
.

2
Notation and preliminaries

Throughout the paper,
(M^{m},h)
is a compact Riemannian manifold without boundary and
\Sigma
is a closed Riemann surface.
Given a smooth immersion
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\to(M^{m},h)
, we endow
\Sigma
with the pull-back metric
g=\vec{\Phi}^{*}h
. An important role will be played by the conformal structure associated to the metric
g
(for conformal structures on compact Riemann surfaces see for instance
[
19
]
). In particular we will assume that, given a sequence of immersions
\vec{\Phi}_{k}:\Sigma\to(M^{m},h)
, the conformal structures associated to the pull-back metrics
g_{k}=\vec{\Phi}_{k}^{*}h
are contained in a compact region of the moduli space. This assumption prevents the degeneration of the Riemann surface in the domain (see
[
4
,
26
]
).

Without loss of generality, we can assume that the smooth immersion
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\to(M^{m},h)
is a conformal parametrisation,
i.e.
we can choose local coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2})
on
\Sigma
such that
g_{i,j}=e^{2\lambda}\delta_{i,j}
. The real valued function
\lambda
will be called
conformal factor
. Sometimes, taking advantage of the complex structure of a Riemann surface,
it will be useful to switch the complex notation
z=x_{1}+i\,x_{2}
.

Thanks to the Nash isometric embedding theorem, we can assume without loss of generality that
M^{m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and that
h=\iota^{\ast}g_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, where
\iota:M^{m}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the
Nash embedding. Since
M^{m}
is a compact manifold, we have in particular

\displaystyle\left\|\vec{\mathbb{I}}_{M^{m}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(M^{m})}\leq C_{0}<\infty,

where
\vec{\mathbb{I}}_{M^{m}}
is the second fundamental form of
\iota
.
Given a smooth immersion
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow(M^{m},h)
of the
2
-dimensional surface
\Sigma
, we define the generalised Gauss map (see Hoffman-Osserman
[
15
]
)
\vec{n}_{\vec{\Phi}}:\Sigma\rightarrow\Lambda^{m-2}TM^{m}
by

\displaystyle\vec{n}_{\vec{\Phi}}=\star_{h}\frac{\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}}{|\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}|}

where
z=x_{1}+i\,x_{2}
are arbitrary local coordinates on
\Sigma
, and
\star_{h}:\Lambda^{2}TM^{m}\rightarrow\Lambda^{m-2}TN^{m}
is the linear Hodge operator associated to the metric
h
. We claim that the following formula holds:

\displaystyle\vec{n}_{\iota\circ\vec{\Phi}}=\iota_{\ast}(\vec{n}_{\vec{\Phi}})\wedge(\vec{n}_{\iota})\circ\vec{\Phi}.

(2.1)

Indeed, we have locally

\vec{n}_{\iota}=\vec{v}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\vec{v}_{n-m},

and

\vec{n}_{\vec{\Phi}}=\vec{n}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\vec{n}_{m-2}
. If
\vec{e}_{i}=e^{-\lambda}\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}
in a conformal chart, we deduce by definition that
(\vec{e}_{1},\vec{e}_{2},\vec{n}_{1},\cdots\vec{n}_{m-2})
is an orthonormal basis of
TM^{m}
, which implies that
(\iota_{\ast}(\vec{e}_{1}),\iota_{\ast}(\vec{e}_{2}),\iota_{\ast}(\vec{n}_{1}),\cdots,\iota_{\ast}(\vec{n}_{m-2}),\vec{v}_{1},\cdots,\vec{v}_{n-m})
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and we deduce the claim (
2.1
).

Even if the main Theorem
A
concerns smooth immersions, some of the intermediate results that we will establish will hold more generally for weak conformal immersions. A weak conformal immersion of the unit ball
B(0,1)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is map
\vec{\Phi}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}\cap\mathrm{W}^{2,2}(B(0,1),M^{m})
such that the a.e. well defined pullback metric
g=\vec{\Phi}^{*}h
is conformal to the Euclidean metric on
B(0,1)
,
i.e.

g_{i,j}=e^{2\lambda}\delta_{i,j}
for some a.e. well defined function
\lambda
.
Observe that the space of weak (conformal) immersions corresponds to the energy space for the Willmore functional
W
, thus it provides a natural functional analytic framework for the analysis & calculus of variations of such an energy functional. Indeed the space of weak immersions with bounded area and Willmore energy satisfy useful pre-compactness properties. Let us recall the following pre-compactness result from
[
38
]
, after
[
50
,
KuwertLi
]
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
\vec{\Phi}_{k}:S^{2}\hookrightarrow(M^{m},h)
be a sequence of weak immersions of
S^{2}
into the closed
m
-dimensional Riemannian manifold
(M^{m},h)
and assume that the uniform area and Willmore bounds (
1.2
) hold.
Then, up to pre-composing with suitable bi-Lipschitz diffeomorphisms of
S^{2}
, one can assume that
\vec{\Phi}_{k}
are conformally parametrised. Moreover,

(1)

Either
,
\mathrm{diam}\,(\vec{\Phi}_{k}(S^{2}))\to 0
and thus there exists a point
\overline{x}\in M
such that, up to a subsequence,
\vec{\Phi}_{k}(S^{2})\to\overline{x}
in Hausdorff distance sense;

(2)

Or
, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a positive Möbius transformation
f_{k}
of
S^{2}
such that, called

\vec{\xi}_{k}=\vec{\Phi}_{k}\circ f_{k}

the reparametrised immersion and

\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}=\log|\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\xi}_{k}|=\log|\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\xi}_{k}|

the new conformal factor, the following holds
(
up to a subsequence
)
:

(i)

There exists a finite set of points
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}\}
such that for any compact subset
K\subset S^{2}\setminus\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}\}

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left\|\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(K)}<\infty\,.

(ii)

There exists a conformal weak immersion
\vec{\xi}_{\infty}:S^{2}\hookrightarrow(M^{m},h)
, possibly branched at
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}\}
, such that

\vec{\xi}_{k}\rightharpoonup\vec{\xi}_{\infty}\,\text{ weakly in }\mathrm{W}^{2,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{2}\setminus\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}\})\,.

(2.2)

Moreover,

W(\vec{\xi}_{\infty})\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}W(\vec{\xi}_{k})\,.

(iii)

Furthermore,
W(\vec{\xi}_{\infty})=\lim_{k\to\infty}W(\vec{\xi}_{k})
if and only if one can choose
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}\}=\emptyset
in the above claims.

Remark 2.2
.

Thanks to Simon’s monotonicity formula
[
53
]
, the first case in Theorem
2.1
is equivalent to
\mathrm{Area}(\vec{\Phi}_{k}(S^{2}))\to 0
.

As a consequence of the
\varepsilon
-regularity Theorem
4.9
that we will prove later in the paper, if
\vec{\Phi}_{k}
are Willmore spheres, then (
2.2
) can be improved to a
C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{2}\setminus\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}\})
convergence for every
l\in\mathbb{N}
.
The goal of the paper is to perform a fine analysis of
\vec{\xi}_{k}
, including the conformal factor, area, and Willmore energy, around the points
a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}
, under the assumption that
\vec{\xi}_{k}
are Willmore immersions. In order to simplify the notation, throughout the paper we will assume that the
\vec{\Phi}_{k}
’s are already conformally parametrised and in the good gauge satisfying
\displaystyle\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left\|\lambda_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(K)}<\infty
, for every compact subset
K\subset S^{2}\setminus\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}\right\}
.

3
\mathrm{L}^{2,1}
estimates on the mean curvature in the neck region

The first part of the proof of the main theorem is to establish
\mathrm{L}^{2,1}
estimates on the mean curvature in the neck region (see Theorem
3.2
). To this aim, in Subsection

3.1
we prove: a no-neck area property (in (
3.20
); see
[
39
]
), a Harnack-type inequality for the conformal factors (in (
3.21
); see
[
4
]
), an
\mathrm{L}^{p}
quantization result for the conformal parameters for some
p>2
(see (
3.25
)) and, finally, uniform
\mathrm{L}^{p}
estimates for the conformal parameters for some
p>2
(see (
3.26
)).

These results are of independent interest as they hold in general for weak immersions, regardless whether they satisfy the Willmore equation or not.

The heart of the proof of Theorem
3.2
will be to establish refined estimates on the approximate conservation laws
[
38
]
satisfied by Willmore immersions. This will be achieved in Subsection
3.2
.

The crux will be to remove a
\log|z|
term in one of the key estimates (more precisely, in (
3.54
)). This will take almost all of Subsection
3.3
and will be the most innovative and technical part of the paper, requiring the introduction of apparently new Lorentz-type function spaces. Let us stress that such a
\log|z|
term is due to the curved ambient space and therefore was not present in the proof of the energy quantization for Willmore surfaces in Euclidean spaces
[
4
]
.

We next pass to some preliminary considerations.

Thanks to the hypothesis (
1.2
) of the theorem, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\mathrm{Area}(\vec{\Phi}_{k}(\Sigma))+W(\vec{\Phi}_{k})\right)<\infty\,.

(3.1)

Combining (
2.1
) with the triangle inequality,
the Gauss equations and the conformal invariance of the Dirichlet energy,
we get:

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}|d\vec{n}_{\iota\circ\vec{\Phi}}|^{2}_{g}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}\leq 2\int_{\Sigma}|d\vec{n}_{\vec{\Phi}}|^{2}_{g}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}+2\int_{\Sigma}|d(\vec{n}_{\iota}\circ\vec{\Phi})|_{g}^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}=2\int_{\Sigma}|d\vec{n}_{\vec{\Phi}}|^{2}_{g}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}+2\int_{\vec{\Phi}(\Sigma)}|\vec{\mathbb{I}}_{M^{m}}|^{2}_{h}d\mathrm{vol}_{h}

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{\Sigma}|d\vec{n}_{\vec{\Phi}}|^{2}_{g}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}+2C_{0}^{2}\,\mathrm{Area}(\vec{\Phi})=2\int_{\Sigma}\left(4|\vec{H}|^{2}-2K_{g}+2\,K_{h}(\vec{\Phi}_{\ast}T\Sigma)\right)d\mathrm{vol}_{g}+2C_{0}^{2}\,\mathrm{Area}(\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle\leq 8\,W(\vec{\Phi})+2C_{0}^{2}\mathrm{Area}(\vec{\Phi})+4\left\|K_{h}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(M^{m})}-8\pi\,\chi(\Sigma)\,.

(3.2)

Since
M^{m}
is a closed manifold, we deduce that the sectional curvature
K_{h}
of the smooth metric
h
on
M^{m}
is bounded.
Therefore,
the combination of (
3.1
) and (
3
) yields:

\displaystyle\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\Sigma}|d\vec{n}_{\iota\circ\vec{\Phi}_{k}}|^{2}_{g}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}\leq(8+2C_{0}^{2})\Lambda+4\left\|K_{h}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(M^{m})}-8\pi\,\chi(\Sigma)=\Lambda(h)<\infty\,.

(3.3)

This allows us to apply the bubble-neck decomposition of Bernard-Rivière
[
4
, Proposition III.
1
]
, and the other theorems of
[
4
, Sections III and IV]
, since they do not use the Euler-Lagrange equation of Willmore surfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and work for any sequence of smooth immersions of bounded Willmore energy.

Lemma 3.1

(Bernard-Rivière, Lemma V.
1
of
[
4
]
)
.

There exist constants
\varepsilon_{0}(n),C_{0}(n)>0
with the following property. If
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}(n)
,
0<4r<R<\infty
,
\Omega=B_{R}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)
, and
\vec{\Phi}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a conformal weak immersion satisfying the conditions

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \left\|\nabla\vec{n}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega)}&\leq\varepsilon\\
\int_{\partial B_{r}(0)}|\nabla\vec{n}|d\mathscr{H}^{1}&\leq\varepsilon\\
\int_{B_{R}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{R}{2}}(0)}|\nabla\vec{n}|^{2}dx+\int_{B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}|\nabla\vec{n}|^{2}dx&\leq\varepsilon,\end{aligned}\right.

(3.4)

then

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega}K_{g}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right|\leq C_{0}(n)\,\varepsilon,

where
g=\vec{\Phi}^{\ast}g_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.

By the Theorema Egregium of Gauss, since
\iota:(M^{m},h)\rightarrow(\iota(M^{m}),g_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
is an isometry, we deduce that we can apply this result to
\vec{\Phi}_{k}
. Hence, if
\vec{\Phi}_{k}
is parametrising a neck-region
\Omega=B_{R}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)
and satisfies the hypothesis of Lemma
3.1
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega}K_{g_{k}}d\mathrm{vol}_{g_{k}}\right|\leq C_{0}(n)\varepsilon.

Then, the other results of
[
4
, Section V]
where no Euler-Lagrange equation is used can be applied identically to
\{\iota\circ\vec{\Phi}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. The Liouville equation

\displaystyle-\Delta\lambda_{k}=e^{2\lambda_{k}}K_{g_{k}}

implies by the Adams-Morrey embedding (see
[
49
]
) that

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left\|\nabla\lambda_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,1))}\leq C.

(3.5)

Now, we will describe the differences from
[
4
, Section VI]
onwards.

In
[
4
, Section VI]
, the conservative form of the Willmore equation for immersions in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
discovered in
[
48
]
plays a fundamental role. In order to extend such analysis to the curved ambient setting, we will use the Euler-Lagrange equation in conservative form obtained in
[
38
]
for immersions into Riemannian manifolds. However, this requires to prove that the area in neck regions is small enough (in fact, we need a slightly stronger statement which will follow from this bound thanks to a Harnack inequality; see Lemma
3.6
).

The next theorem is the main result of this section.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
\left\{r_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}},\left\{R_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,\infty)
be such that
r_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
and
R_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}R\in(0,\infty)
. Let
(M^{m},h)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a closed Riemannian manifold that we assume isometrically embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For any
0<\alpha\leq 1
, define the subset
\Omega_{k}(\alpha)=B_{\alpha R_{k}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\alpha^{-1}r_{k}}(0)\subset B(0,R_{k})
. There exists constants
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(n,h),\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{0}(n,h)>0
with the following property. Let
\{\vec{\Phi}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C^{\infty}(B(0,R_{k}),M^{m})
be a sequence of Willmore disks satisfying:

\displaystyle\Lambda=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\left\|\nabla\lambda_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,R_{k}))}+\mathrm{Area}(\vec{\Phi}_{k}(B(0,R_{k})))+\int_{B(0,R_{k})}|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}dx\right)<\infty

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in\left[r_{k},\frac{R_{k}}{2}\right]}\int_{B_{2s}\setminus\overline{B}_{s}(0)}|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}dx\leq\varepsilon_{0}\,.

Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|e^{\lambda_{k}}\vec{H}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}))}\leq C_{0}(n,h,\Lambda).

The rest of the section will be devoted to the proof of Theorem
3.2
. This will require to estabish several results of independent interest.

3.1
\mathrm{L}^{p}
bounds and quantization for the conformal parameters

The goal of this section is four-fold: we prove a no-neck area property (see (
3.20
)), establish a Harnack-type inequality for the conformal factors (see (
3.21
)), we prove an
\mathrm{L}^{p}
quantization result for the conformal parameters for some
p>2
(see (
3.25
)), and establish uniform
\mathrm{L}^{p}
estimates for the conformal parameters for some
p>2
(see (
3.26
)).

Let us first recall the following lemma from
[
33
]
, slightly generalising
[
4
, Lemma IV.
1
]
.

Theorem 3.3
.

There exists a positive real number
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}(n)
with the following property. Let
0<2^{6}r<R<\infty
be fixed radii and
\vec{\Phi}:\Omega=B_{R}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a weak immersion of finite total curvature such that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\vec{n}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega)}\leq\varepsilon_{1}(n).

(3.6)

For all
\left(\dfrac{r}{R}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}<\alpha<1
, define
\Omega({\alpha})=B_{\alpha R}\setminus\overline{B}_{\alpha^{-1}r}(0)
. Then there exists a universal constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(n)
and
d\in\mathbb{R}

(
depending on
r,R,\vec{\Phi}
but not on
\alpha
)
such that for all
\left(\dfrac{r}{R}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}<\alpha<\dfrac{1}{4}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla(\lambda-d\log|z|)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega(\alpha))}\leq C_{1}\left(\sqrt{\alpha}\left\|\nabla\lambda\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega)}+\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\vec{n}|^{2}dx\right)

(3.7)

and for all
r\leq\rho<R
, we have

\displaystyle\left|d-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial B_{\rho}}\partial_{\nu}\lambda\,d\mathscr{H}^{1}\right|\leq C_{1}\left(\int_{B_{\max\left\{\rho,2r\right\}}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}|\nabla\vec{n}|^{2}dx+\frac{1}{\log\left(\frac{R}{\rho}\right)}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\vec{n}|^{2}dx\right)\,.

(3.8)

In particular, there exists a universal constant
C_{1}^{\prime}=C^{\prime}_{1}(n)
with the following property: for all
\left(\dfrac{r}{R}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}<\alpha<\dfrac{1}{4}
, there exists
A_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\lambda-d\log|z|-A_{\alpha}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Omega({\alpha}))}\leq C_{1}^{\prime}\left(\sqrt{\alpha}\left\|\nabla\lambda\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega)}+\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\vec{n}|^{2}dx\right).

(3.9)

Applying Theorem
3.3
to
\{\vec{\Phi}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
(that we see from now as a map
\vec{\Phi}_{k}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\vec{\Phi}_{k}(\Sigma)\subset M^{m}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
), we deduce that in a neck region
\Omega_{k}(\alpha)=B(0,\alpha R_{k})\setminus\overline{B}(0,\alpha^{-1}r_{k})
(where
\displaystyle\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}R_{k}<\infty
), there exists large enough
d_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
and
A_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\lambda_{k}-d_{k}\,\log|z|-A_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\leq C_{1}^{\prime}\left(\sqrt{\alpha}\left\|\nabla\lambda_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}+\int_{\Omega_{k}(1)}|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}dx\right)\leq C_{1}^{\prime\prime}<\infty

thanks to (
3.3
) and (
3.5
). We deduce that:

\displaystyle e^{-2C_{1}^{\prime\prime}}e^{2A_{k}}|z|^{2d_{k}}\leq e^{2\lambda_{k}}\leq e^{2C_{1}^{\prime\prime}}e^{2A_{k}}|z|^{2d_{k}},\quad\text{for all $z\in\Omega_{k}(\alpha)$}.

(3.10)

Thanks to (
3.3
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}e^{2A_{k}}|z|^{2d_{k}}|dz|^{2}\leq\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}e^{2C_{1}^{\prime\prime}}\mathrm{Area}(\vec{\Phi}_{k})\leq e^{2C_{1}^{\prime\prime}}\Lambda<\infty.

(3.11)

Now, by the
\varepsilon
-regularity Theorem
4.9
to be proven below,
we deduce that
\vec{\Phi}_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}
in
C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(B(0,1)\setminus\left\{0\right\})
for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}:B(0,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a branched immersion having at most a branch point at
0
. Therefore, by
[
48
]
(see also
[
4
]
), there exist an integer
\theta_{0}\geq 1
and
\vec{A}_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
such that

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}=\vec{A}_{0}z^{\theta_{0}-1}+o(|z|^{\theta_{0}-1})\,.

We also let
\beta_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle e^{2\lambda_{\infty}}=2|\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}|^{2}=2|\vec{A}_{0}|^{2}|z|^{2\theta_{0}-2}(1+o(1))=\beta_{0}^{2}|z|^{2\theta_{0}-2}(1+o(1)).

Now, applying (
3.8
) to
d_{k}
and
\rho=\alpha\frac{R}{2}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left|d_{k}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial B_{\alpha\frac{R}{2}}}\partial_{\nu}\lambda_{k}\,d\mathscr{H}^{1}\right|\leq\Gamma_{2}<\infty.

By the strong convergence, it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial B_{\alpha\frac{R}{2}}}\partial_{\nu}\lambda_{k}\,d\mathscr{H}^{1}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial B_{\alpha\frac{R}{2}}}\partial_{\nu}\lambda_{\infty}d\mathscr{H}^{1}=\theta_{0}-1+O(\alpha R)

and we deduce that
\left\{d_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}
is a bounded sequence. Therefore, we can assume up to a subsequence that
d_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}d\in\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

d>-1
.

Proof.

Define

\displaystyle\underline{A}

\displaystyle=\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}A_{k}\in\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{-\infty,\infty\right\}

\displaystyle\overline{A}

\displaystyle=\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}A_{k}\in\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{-\infty,\infty\right\}.

By the strong convergence, assuming without loss of generality that
R_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}R
for some
R>0
, we deduce that for all
|z|=\alpha R
, we have

\displaystyle e^{-2C_{1}^{\prime\prime}}e^{2\underline{A}}|z|^{2d}\leq\beta_{0}^{2}|z|^{2\theta_{0}-2}(1+o(1))\leq e^{2C_{1}^{\prime\prime}}e^{{A}}|z|^{2d}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\infty&<\underline{A}\leq\overline{A}<\infty\\
e^{2\overline{A}}&\geq\beta_{0}^{2}e^{-2C_{1}^{\prime\prime}}(\alpha R)^{2\theta_{0}-2(d+1)}(1+o(1))\geq\frac{2}{3}\beta_{0}^{2}e^{-2C_{1}^{\prime\prime}}(\alpha R)^{2\theta_{0}-2(d+1)}\\
e^{2\overline{A}}&\leq\frac{3}{2}\beta_{0}^{2}e^{2C_{1}^{\prime\prime}}(\alpha R)^{2\theta_{0}-2(d+1)}.\end{aligned}\right.

Now, if
d_{k}\neq-1
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}e^{2A_{k}}|z|^{2d_{k}}|dz|^{2}=2\pi e^{2A_{k}}\int_{\alpha^{-1}r_{k}}^{\alpha R_{k}}r^{2d_{k}+1}dz=\frac{\pi}{d_{k}+1}e^{2A_{k}}\left((\alpha R_{k})^{2d_{k}+2}-(\alpha^{-1}r_{k})^{2d_{k}+2}\right)

(3.12)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\pi}{2(d_{k}+1)}\beta_{0}^{2}e^{-2C_{1}^{\prime\prime}}(\alpha R)^{2\theta_{0}}\left(\left(\frac{R}{R_{k}}\right)^{-2(d+1)}(\alpha R_{k})^{2(d_{k}-d)}-(\alpha R)^{-2(d_{k}+1)}(\alpha^{-1}r_{k})^{2d_{k}+2}\right).

If
d<-1
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{2(d_{k}+1)}\beta_{0}^{2}e^{-2C_{1}^{\prime\prime}}(\alpha R)^{2\theta_{0}}\left(\left(\frac{R}{R_{k}}\right)^{-2(d+1)}(\alpha R_{k})^{2(d_{k}-d)}-(\alpha R)^{-2(d_{k}+1)}(\alpha^{-1}r_{k})^{2d_{k}+2}\right)\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\infty,

which contradicts (
3.11
). Therefore, we have
d\geq-1
. Now, notice that provided that
d_{k}=-1
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}e^{2A_{k}}|z|^{2d_{k}}|dz|^{2}=2\pi e^{2A_{k}}\log\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}R_{k}}{r_{k}}\right)\geq\pi\beta_{0}^{2}e^{-2C_{1}^{\prime\prime}}(\alpha R)^{2\theta_{0}-2(d+1)}\log\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}R_{k}}{r_{k}}\right)\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\infty.

Therefore, in the limiting case
d=-1
, we can assume that
d_{k}\neq-1
for
k
large enough. Now, notice that since
R_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}R
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\frac{R}{R_{k}}\right)^{-2(d+1)}(\alpha R_{k})^{2(d_{k}-d)}=1+o(1)

\displaystyle(\alpha R)^{-2(d_{k}+1)}(\alpha^{-1}r_{k})^{2d_{k}+2}=(1+o(1))r_{k}^{2d_{k}+2},

which implies that

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(\frac{1}{d_{k}+1}\left(1-r_{k}^{2d_{k}+2}\right)\right)<\infty.

(3.13)

First assume that
d_{k}<-1
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
large enough. Writing
d_{k}=-1-\varepsilon_{k}
, we get than there exists
C>0
such that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
large enough

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d_{k}+1}\left(1-r_{k}^{2d_{k}+2}\right)=\frac{1}{-\varepsilon_{k}}\left(1-\frac{1}{r_{k}^{2\varepsilon_{k}}}\right)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\left(\frac{1}{r_{k}^{2k}}-1\right)\leq C.

Therefore, this implies that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r_{k}^{2\varepsilon_{k}}}\leq 1+C\varepsilon_{k}\,.

Taking the logarithm,

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon_{k}\log\left(\frac{1}{r_{k}}\right)\leq\log\left(1+C\varepsilon_{k}\right)\leq C\varepsilon_{k}

and finally

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{1}{r_{k}}\right)\leq\frac{C}{2}

which is absurd since
r_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
. Coming back to the
identity (
3.12
),
we deduce that for
k
large enough if
\delta>0
is such that
d>-1+\delta

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}e^{2\lambda_{k}}|dz|^{2}\leq\frac{4\pi}{\delta}\beta_{0}^{2}e^{2C_{1}^{\prime\prime}}(\alpha R)^{2\theta_{0}}\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0.

Then, provided that
d_{k}=-1+\varepsilon_{k}
, (
3.13
) implies that there exists some
C<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\left(1-r_{k}^{2\varepsilon_{k}}\right)=\frac{1}{d_{k}+1}\left(1-r_{k}^{2d_{k}+2}\right)\leq C,

which yields

\displaystyle\log(r_{k})\geq\frac{\log(1-C\varepsilon_{k})}{2\varepsilon_{k}}\geq-C,

for
k
large enough, using the inequality
\log(1-x)\geq-2x
for
0\leq x\leq\dfrac{1}{2}
. Since
r_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
, we have
\log(r_{k})\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}-\infty
and we obtain a contradiction. We conclude that
d>-1
.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

Once the quantization of energy is established, it will imply
a posteriori
that (see
[
33
]
)

d_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\theta_{0}-1\geq 0\,.

(3.14)

In fact,

[
33
, Theorem A]

holds for an arbitrary immersion with values into
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and yields that
d_{k}=\theta_{0}-1\geq 0
. However,
in the present setting of curved ambient space, we could not get (
3.14
)
a priori
(as it happens in the case of a flat ambient space). The origin of such a difficulty lies in the non-vanishing curvature that perturbs the system of conservation laws associated to the Willmore equation
[
38
]
.

Let us also observe that the fact that a no-neck energy property implies the asymptotic integrality of
d_{k}
suggests that an hypothesis ensuring that
d_{k}>-1+\varepsilon
(for
k
large enough) is necessary.

Now, since by
[
4
, Proposition III.
1
]
neck-regions are disjoint and finite unions of such annuli, if
\Omega_{k}(\alpha)
is the whole neck-region associated to a concentration point
a_{i}
(where
1\leq i\leq N
, and
N
is the number of concentration points), we have
(see also
[
39
]
):

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}e^{2\lambda_{k}}|dz|^{2}=0\,.

(3.15)

Consider a typical bubble region

B(i,j,\alpha,k)=B_{\alpha^{-1}r_{k}^{i,j}}(x_{k}^{i,j})\setminus\bigcup_{j^{\prime}\in I^{i,j}}B_{\alpha r_{k}^{i,j}}(x_{k}^{i,j^{\prime}})

(3.16)

from the bubble-neck decomposition
[
4
, Proposition III.
1
]
. We refer to
[
4
]
for the precise statement and relevant definitions. For our purpose here it is sufficient to recall that

(1)

B_{r_{k}^{i,j}}(x_{k}^{i,j})
corresponds to a bubble for
\vec{\Phi}_{k}
;

(2)

the set of indices
I^{i,j}
corresponds to the bubbles contained in
B_{r_{k}^{i,j}}(x_{k}^{i,j})
; in this sense, the role of (
3.16
) is to isolate a single bubble.

(3)

the total number of bubbles is bounded:
\displaystyle\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{card}\bigcup_{i,j}I^{i,j}<\infty
;

(4)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}x^{i,j}_{k}\to a_{i}
, for every
i,j
.

(5)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}r^{i,j}_{k}\to 0
, for every
i,j
.

From
[
4
, (VIII.
10
)]
, a uniform Harnack inequality holds
: for all
0<\alpha<1
, there exists
C_{\alpha}>1
such that

\sup_{B(i,j,\alpha,k)}e^{2\lambda_{k}}\leq C_{\alpha}\,\inf_{B(i,j,\alpha,k)}e^{2\lambda_{k}},\quad\text{ for all $k\in\mathbb{N}$ large enough}.

(3.17)

Therefore, the estimate (
3.10
) implies that there exists
C_{\alpha}^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle e^{2\lambda_{k}(z)}\leq C_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\alpha^{-1}r_{k}^{i,j})^{2d_{k}}\leq C_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}\left(r_{k}^{i,j}\right)^{2d_{k}}\,,\quad\text{ for all $z\in B(i,j,\alpha,k)$}\,.

(3.18)

Since
d_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow}{\longrightarrow}d>-1
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{B(i,j,\alpha,k)}e^{2\lambda_{k}}|dz|^{2}\leq\pi C_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}\left(r_{k}^{i,j}\right)^{2d_{k}+2}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0\,,\quad\text{ for every fixed $\alpha\in(0,1)$}.

(3.19)

\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{B(0,\alpha R_{k})}e^{2\lambda_{k}}|dz|^{2}=0\,.

(3.20)

We also deduce that there exists
A\in\mathbb{R}
such that, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
large enough, it holds:

\displaystyle e^{-A}|z|^{d_{k}}\leq e^{\lambda_{k}(z)}\leq e^{A}|z|^{d_{k}},\quad\text{ for all $z\in\Omega_{k}(1/2)$}.

(3.21)

For the next developments, we need to sharpen the above estimates to an
\mathrm{L}^{2,1}
bound for the conformal parameter and an
\mathrm{L}^{p}
quantization result for it.

Let
d\in\mathbb{R}
and
f:B(0,R)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
be such that, for all
z\in B(0,R)
, it holds
f(z)=|z|^{d}
. If
d\leq-1
, since
\left\|\,\cdot\,\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(X)}\geq 2\sqrt{2}\left\|\,\cdot\,\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(X)}
, we have:

\displaystyle\left\||z|^{d}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,R))}=\infty.

Recalling that for all measured space
(X,\mu)
, for all
1<p<\infty
, it holds (see for example
[
33
, Appedix 3.7.1]
)

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,1}(X)}=\frac{p^{2}}{p-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu\left(X\cap\left\{x:|f(x)|>t\right\}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}dt,

we have:

\displaystyle\left\||z|^{d}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,R))}

\displaystyle=4\int_{0}^{1}\mu(B(0,R))^{\frac{1}{2}}dt=4\sqrt{\pi}R\,,\quad\text{for $d=0$},

\displaystyle\left\||z|^{d}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,R))}

\displaystyle=4\int_{0}^{{R^{d}}}\mu(B(0,R))^{\frac{1}{2}}dt+4\int_{R^{d}}^{\infty}\mu(B(0,t^{\frac{1}{d}}))^{\frac{1}{2}}dt

\displaystyle=4\sqrt{\pi}R^{1+d}+4\sqrt{\pi}\frac{-d}{1+d}R^{1+d}=\frac{4\sqrt{\pi}}{1+d}R^{1+d}\,,\quad\text{for $-1<d<0$},

\displaystyle\left\||z|^{d}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,R))}

\displaystyle=4\int_{0}^{R^{d}}\mu(B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,t^{\frac{1}{d}}))^{\frac{1}{2}}dt=4\sqrt{\pi}R^{1+d}\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{1-s^{\frac{2}{d}}}ds

\displaystyle\leq 4\sqrt{\pi}R^{1+d}\,,\quad\text{for $d>0$}.

Combining the last estimates with (
3.21
), we deduce that, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
large enough, it holds

\displaystyle\left\|e^{\lambda_{k}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\leq 4\sqrt{\pi}e^{A}\max\left\{1,\frac{1}{1+d_{k}}\right\}(\alpha R_{k})^{1+d_{k}}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}4\sqrt{\pi}e^{A}\max\left\{1,\frac{1}{1+d}\right\}(\alpha R)^{1+d}.

(3.22)

By (
3.18
), we have

\displaystyle\left\|e^{\lambda_{k}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(i,j,\alpha,k))}\leq 4\sqrt{\pi}\sqrt{C_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}}\left(r_{k}^{i,j}\right)^{1+d_{k}}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

(3.23)

The combination of (
3.22
) and (
3.23
) yields

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left\|e^{\lambda_{k}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,\alpha R_{k}))}=0.

(3.24)

Later on, we will need the following improvement of the quantization (
3.24
). Since
d_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}d>-1
, we deduce that there exists
0<\varepsilon<1
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
k\geq N
, we have
d_{k}\geq-1+\varepsilon
. In particular, this implies that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}e^{p\lambda_{k}(z)}|dz|^{2}\leq e^{pA}\int_{B(0,\alpha R_{k})}|z|^{pd_{k}}|dz|^{2}=2\pi e^{pA}\frac{(\alpha R_{k})^{2+pd_{k}}}{2+pd_{k}}\,,\quad\text{ for all $p<\dfrac{2}{1-\varepsilon}$}.

Using the Harnack inequality in bubble domains (
3.17
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left\|e^{\lambda_{k}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(B(0,\alpha R_{k}))}=0\,,\quad\text{for all $p<\frac{2}{1-\varepsilon}$}.

(3.25)

Indeed, we have
2+pd_{k}>2+p(-1+\varepsilon)>0
if and only if
p<\dfrac{2}{1-\varepsilon}
. In particular, we deduce that

\displaystyle e^{\lambda_{k}}\quad\text{is bounded in }\;\,\mathrm{L}^{p}\left(B\left(0,R_{k}/2\right)\right)\;\,\text{for all}\;\,p<\dfrac{2}{1-\varepsilon}.

(3.26)

3.2
Refined estimates on the approximate conservation laws

From now on,
\{\vec{\Phi}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
will be a sequence of smooth Willmore immersions satisfying the assumptions of the main Theorem
A
.
In the next lemma, we generalise
[
38
, Lemma A.1]
, by relaxing the
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
control to an
\mathrm{L}^{2,1}
control.

Lemma 3.6
.

There exists constants
\varepsilon_{2}(m),C_{2}(m)>0
with the following property.
For all
j,k\in\left\{1,\cdots,m\right\}
, let
\gamma_{j}^{k}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,2}\cap C^{0}(\mathbb{C})
be such that
\mathrm{supp}\,(\gamma_{j}^{k})\subset B(0,2)
and
\left\|\gamma_{j}^{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\mathbb{C})}\leq\varepsilon_{0}
. For all
\vec{U}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{C})
, define

\displaystyle\left(\nabla_{z}\vec{U}\right)_{j}

\displaystyle=\partial_{z}\vec{U}_{j}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\gamma_{j}^{k}\vec{U}_{k}\qquad\text{in}\;\,\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{C}),\;\,\text{where}\;\,1\leq j\leq m.

Then for all
\vec{Y}\in\left(\dot{\mathrm{H}}^{-1}+\mathrm{L}^{1}\right)(\mathbb{C})

, there exists a unique
\vec{U}\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,1))
satisfying

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \nabla_{z}\vec{U}&=\vec{Y}\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(B(0,1))\\
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{U})&=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,1).\end{aligned}\right.

Furthermore, we have the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\vec{U}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,1))}\leq C_{2}\left\|\vec{Y}\right\|_{\dot{\mathrm{H}}^{-1}+\mathrm{L}^{1}(B(0,1))}.

Proof.

As in
[
38
]
, we use a fix point argument. For all
\vec{U}\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})
, define

\displaystyle T(\vec{U})=\left(-\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}}\ast\left(\vec{Y}_{j}-\sum_{k=1}^{m}\gamma_{j}^{k}\vec{U}_{k}\right)\right)_{1\leq j\leq m}.

By the Young inequality for weak
\mathrm{L}^{p}
spaces (that follows from the classical Young inequality by interpolation) and the
\mathrm{L}^{2,1}/\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}
duality, we have for some
\Gamma_{0}<\infty

\displaystyle\left\|T(\vec{U})+\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}}\ast\vec{Y}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}\Gamma_{0}\left\|\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\left\|\gamma_{j}^{k}\vec{U}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{C})}\leq\frac{\Gamma_{0}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}\left\|\gamma_{j}^{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\mathbb{C})}\left\|\vec{U}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\Gamma_{0}m}{\sqrt{\pi}}\varepsilon_{0}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left\|\vec{U}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\leq\frac{\Gamma_{0}m^{2}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\varepsilon_{0}\left\|\vec{U}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}.

(3.27)

Choose

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2\Gamma_{0}m^{2}}.

Now, exactly as in
[
38
]
, we get the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}}\ast\vec{Y}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\leq\Gamma_{1}(m)\left\|\vec{Y}\right\|_{\dot{\mathrm{H}}^{-1}+\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{C})},

(3.28)

where for all
u:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\dot{\mathrm{H}}^{-1}+\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m})}=\inf\left\{\left\|u_{1}\right\|_{\mathrm{\dot{H}}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{m})}+\left\|u_{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m})}:u=u_{1}+u_{2}\right\}.

Therefore, (
3.2
) and (
3.28
) implies that for all
\vec{U}\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|T(\vec{U})\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\|\vec{U}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}+\Gamma_{1}(n)\left\|\vec{Y}\right\|_{\dot{\mathrm{H}}^{-1}+\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{C})}\,.

(3.29)

By (
3.2
), for all
\vec{U}_{1},\vec{U}_{2}\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|T(\vec{U}_{1})-T(\vec{U}_{2})\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\|\vec{U}_{1}-\vec{U}_{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}.

(3.30)

Therefore, (
3.29
) and (
3.30
)
prove that
T:\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})
is a contraction, and therefore admits a fixed point by Banach contraction mapping Theorem. The estimate follows from (
3.28
).

∎

Now, let
f_{k}:\mathbb{C}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{m}
be the linear map such that for all
X\in C^{\infty}(B(0,R_{k}),\mathbb{C}^{m})
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{z}\vec{X}=\partial_{z}\vec{X}+f_{k}(\vec{X})=\partial_{z}\vec{X}+\left(\sum_{l=1}^{m}\gamma_{j,k}^{l}\vec{X}_{l}\right)_{1\leq j\leq m},

where,
denoting with
(\vec{\Phi}_{k}^{1},\ldots,\vec{\Phi}_{k}^{m})
the components of
\vec{\Phi}_{k}
in the local coordinates of
M^{m}
, we set:

\displaystyle\gamma_{j,k}^{l}=\sum_{q=1}^{m}\Gamma_{j,q}^{l}\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}^{q},

where
\Gamma_{j,q}^{l}
are the Christoffel symbols of the ambient Riemannian manifold
(M^{m},h)
. We now fix some
\varepsilon_{3}(m)\leq\varepsilon_{2}(m)
to be determined later. By the estimate (
3.24
), we deduce that there exists
\alpha_{0}>0
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{1\leq j,l\leq m}\left\|\gamma_{j,k}^{l}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))}\leq\varepsilon_{3}(m)\leq\varepsilon_{2}(m)\,,\quad\text{ for all $k\geq N$}.

(3.31)

Recalling the
{\mathrm{L}}^{2,1}/{\mathrm{L}}^{2,\infty}
duality, we have that for all
X\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,R_{k}),\mathbb{C}^{m})
and for all
r\leq\alpha_{0}R_{k}
the following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\left\|f_{k}(\vec{X})\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(B(0,r))}\leq\sum_{j,l=1}^{m}\left\|\gamma_{j}^{l}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,r))}\left\|\vec{X}_{l}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,r))}\leq\varepsilon_{3}(m)m^{2}\left\|\vec{X}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,r))}.

(3.32)

We also have the pointwise estimate

\displaystyle|f_{k}(\vec{X})(z)|\leq C_{1}(h)\,e^{\lambda_{k}(z)}|\vec{X}(z)|\,,

(3.33)

where
C_{1}(h)>0
depends only on
h
.
Let
{\vec{Y}_{k}}:\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{m}
be defined by

\vec{Y}_{k}=i\left(\nabla_{z}\vec{H}_{k}-3\,\nabla^{\perp}_{z}\vec{H}_{k}-i\,\star_{h}\left(\nabla_{z}\vec{n}_{k}\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\right)\right)\,.

(3.34)

Notice that
{\vec{Y}_{k}}
is smooth, so in particular it is an element of
\mathrm{H}^{-1}+\mathrm{L}^{1}
. Thus, the extension
{\vec{Y}_{k}}
to the whole
\mathbb{C}
by setting
{\vec{Y}_{k}}\equiv 0
on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0})
is an element of
\dot{\mathrm{H}}^{-1}+\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
. Making use of the
\mathrm{L}^{2,1}
estimate (
3.31
), we are in position to apply Lemma
3.6
and deduce that there exists
\vec{L}_{k}\in{\mathrm{L}}^{2,\infty}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}),\mathbb{C})
such that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \nabla_{z}\vec{L}_{k}&=\vec{Y}_{k}\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k})\\
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{L}_{k})&=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}).\end{aligned}\right.

(3.35)

Remark 3.7
.

The boundary condition for
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{L}_{k})
in (
3.35
) is obtained in the exact same way as in Lemma A.
2
in
[
38
]
since
\vec{\Phi}_{k}
is smooth (see Lemma
3.15
for more details). However, the
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}
estimate obtained here by simply applying Lemma
3.6
will depend on
k
and for technical reasons we need to obtain a function
\vec{L}_{k}
controlled in
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}
independently of
k
. Indeed, without
a priori
estimates, since the boundary condition of
\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{L}_{k})
cannot be prescribed, we would not be able to get a
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}
control on
\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{L}_{k})
(in
[
4
]
, at a crucial step, the authors use the fact that the equation in
\nabla\vec{L}
holds up to a constant, which allows to assume that some mean of
\vec{L}_{k}
vanishes; however, if the Christoffel symbols do not vanish, the equation is not invariant by translation). This is due to the fact that in general, one cannot prescribe the full boundary condition in a
\overline{\partial}
equation.

From the
\varepsilon
-regularity Theorem
4.9

we know that there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
k
and
\alpha_{0}
such that

\displaystyle e^{\lambda_{k}(z)}|\vec{Y}_{k}(z)|\leq Ce^{-\lambda_{k}(|z|)}\frac{\delta(|z|)}{|z|}\leq\frac{C_{0}}{|z|^{2+d_{k}}}.

(3.36)

For technical reasons, we will have to perform a disjunction of cases depending on the value of
d_{k}
.
The analysis in the first case,
d_{k}\leq 0
, will take several pages; the other cases will be discussed after (
3.96
).

Analysis of case 1:
d_{k}\leq 0

If
d_{k}\leq 0
, then we have
a fortiori

\displaystyle|\vec{Y}_{k}(z)|\leq\frac{C_{0}}{|z|^{2}}.

(3.37)

First define
\vec{U}_{k}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
by

\displaystyle\vec{U}_{k}(z)=\frac{1}{\overline{z}^{2}}\left(-\frac{1}{\pi\overline{\zeta}}\ast\left(\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)\right)\right)(z)=-\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{z-\zeta}}|d\zeta|^{2}=\frac{1}{2\pi i\overline{z}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta-z}}d\overline{\zeta}\wedge d\zeta.

(3.38)

Lemma 3.8
.

There exists constants
C_{0},C_{1}>0
and
a_{k,0}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, with
\displaystyle\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}|a_{k,0}|<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\left|\vec{U}_{k}(z)-\frac{a_{k,0}}{\overline{z}^{2}}\right|\leq C_{1}\frac{\log\left(\frac{R}{|z|}\right)}{|z|}+\frac{8C_{0}}{|z|},\quad\text{for all }z\in\mathbb{C}\,.

(3.39)

Proof.

Since
\overline{z}^{-2}
is anti-holomorphic, we have:

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\vec{U}_{k}(z)=\frac{1}{\overline{z}^{2}}\left(\delta_{z}\ast\left(\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)\right)\right)=\frac{1}{\overline{z}^{2}}\cdot\overline{z}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(z)=\vec{Y}_{k}(z),

(3.40)

where
\delta_{z}
is the Dirac mass in
z\in\mathbb{C}
.
Write for simplicity
r=2r_{k}
and
R=\frac{R_{k}}{2}
.

Fix some
z\in B(0,R)
. First, if
0<2|z|\leq r
, we have

\displaystyle\vec{U}_{k}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i\overline{z}^{2}}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta-z}}d\overline{\zeta}\wedge d\zeta

(3.41)

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta-z}}d\overline{\zeta}\wedge d\zeta=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\zeta^{l+1}}|d\zeta|^{2}\right)z^{l}=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}a_{k,l}z^{l}\,.

(3.42)

For
l=0
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B(0,2|z|)}}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\zeta^{l+1}}\frac{|d\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta|}\right|\leq C_{0}\int_{B(0,R)}\frac{|d\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta|}=2\pi C_{0}R,

(3.43)

and for
l=1
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{\overline{\zeta^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}}{\overline{\zeta-z}}|d\zeta|^{2}\right|\leq 2\pi\int_{2|z|}^{R}\frac{dt}{t}=2\pi\log\left(\frac{R}{2|z|}\right)\,.

(3.44)

Therefore, by (
3.41
), (
3.42
), (
3.43
) and (
3.44
), we deduce that there exists a universal constant
C_{1}
such that

\displaystyle\left|\overline{z}^{2}\vec{U}_{k}(z)-a_{k,0}-\sum_{l=2}^{\infty}a_{k,l}z^{l}\right|\leq C_{1}|z|\log\left(\frac{R}{|z|}\right),

(3.45)

and
a_{k,0}
is uniformly bounded for
k\geq N
.

Furthermore, we have for all
l\geq 2

\displaystyle\left|\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\zeta^{l+1}}|d\zeta|^{2}\right|\leq C_{0}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{|d\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta|^{l-1}}\leq 2\pi C_{0}\int_{2|z|}^{R}\frac{dt}{t^{l}}=\frac{2\pi C_{0}}{l-1}\left(\frac{1}{(2|z|)^{l-1}}-\frac{1}{R^{l-1}}\right),

(3.46)

which implies that

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{l=2}^{\infty}a_{k,l}z^{l}\right|\leq 2C_{0}\sum_{l=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(2|z|)^{l-1}}\times|z|^{l}=2C_{0}|z|.

(3.47)

Therefore, by (
3.46
) and (
3.47
) we get

\displaystyle\left|\vec{U}_{k}(z)-\frac{a_{k,0}}{\overline{z}^{2}}\right|\leq C_{1}\frac{\log\left(\frac{R}{|z|}\right)}{|z|}+\frac{2C_{0}}{|z|}.

(3.48)

Now, assume that
2|z|>r
. Then we have

\displaystyle\overline{z}^{2}\vec{U}_{k}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{B(0,2|z|)\setminus\overline{B}(0,r)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta-z}}d\overline{\zeta}\wedge d\zeta+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta-z}}d\overline{\zeta}\wedge d\zeta=\vec{u}_{1}(z)+\vec{u}_{2}(z).

(3.49)

We first easily estimate

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{B(0,2|z|)\setminus\overline{B(0,r)}}\frac{\overline{\zeta^{2}}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta-z}}d\overline{\zeta}\wedge d\zeta\right|\leq\frac{C_{0}}{\pi}\int_{B(0,2|z|)}\frac{|d\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta-z|}\leq\frac{C_{0}}{\pi}\int_{B(z,3|z|)}\frac{|d\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta-z|}=6C_{0}R|z|

(3.50)

and the previous argument shows that (notice that this is the same constant
a_{k,0}
)

\displaystyle\left|\vec{u}_{2}(z)-a_{k,0}\right|\leq C_{1}|z|{\log\left(\frac{R}{|z|}\right)}+2C_{0}|z|.

(3.51)

Finally, by (
3.49
), (
3.50
) and (
3.51
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\left|\vec{U}_{k}(z)-\frac{a_{k,0}}{\overline{z}^{2}}\right|\leq C_{1}\frac{\log\left(\frac{R}{|z|}\right)}{|z|}+\frac{8C_{0}}{|z|},

(3.52)

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Next, define
\vec{V}_{k}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
by:

\displaystyle\vec{V}_{k}(z)=\vec{U}_{k}(z)-\frac{a_{k,0}}{\overline{z}^{2}}\,,\quad\text{for all }z\in\mathbb{C}.

(3.53)

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\vec{V}_{k}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
be defined in (
3.53
). Then there exists
C_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle\left|\vec{V}_{k}(z)\right|\leq\frac{C_{2}}{|z|}\left({\log\left(\frac{R}{|z|}\right)}+1\right).

(3.54)

Moreover,
\nabla\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})
which shows in particular that
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,R_{k}))
. Furthermore, by the Sobolev embedding, it holds:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})\in\bigcap_{p<\infty}\mathrm{L}^{p}(B(0,R_{k})).

Proof.

From Lemma
3.8
, we get that
\partial_{z}\vec{V}_{k}=\vec{Y}_{k}
on
\mathbb{C}
and that there exists
C_{2}>0
such that:

\displaystyle\left|\vec{V}_{k}(z)\right|\leq\frac{C_{2}}{|z|}\left({\log\left(\frac{R}{|z|}\right)}+1\right),\quad\text{for all $z\in\mathbb{C}$}.

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\Delta\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{V}_{k}\right)=4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{Y}_{k}\right)=4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\nabla_{\overline{z}}\vec{Y}_{k}(z)\right)-4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\overline{f_{k}}(\vec{Y}_{k})\right)\quad\text{ on $\Omega_{k}(1/2)$ },

while
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})
is harmonic on
B(0,\alpha_{0}^{-1}r_{k})
.
Recall that by
[
38
, Lemma 3.2 and Theorem 3.1]
, the following identities hold:

\displaystyle\vec{Y}_{k}=i\left(\nabla_{z}\vec{H}_{k}-3\,\nabla_{z}^{\perp}\vec{H}_{k}-i\star_{h}(\nabla_{z}\vec{n}_{k}\wedge\vec{H}_{k})\right)=-2i\left(\nabla_{z}^{\perp}\vec{H}_{k}+\langle\vec{H}_{k},\vec{H}_{k,0}\rangle\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle 4e^{-2\lambda}\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\nabla_{\overline{z}}\left(\nabla_{z}^{\perp}\vec{H}_{k}+\langle\vec{H}_{k},\vec{H}_{0,k}\rangle\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\right)\right)=\Delta_{g}^{\perp}\vec{H}_{k}-2|\vec{H}_{k}|^{2}\vec{H}+\mathscr{A}(\vec{H}_{k})+8\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\langle R(\vec{e}_{\overline{z}},\vec{e}_{z})\vec{e}_{z},\vec{H}_{k}\rangle\vec{e}_{\overline{z}}\right).

Therefore:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\,(\nabla_{\overline{z}}\vec{Y}_{k})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Im}\,\left(-2i\nabla_{\overline{z}}\left(\nabla_{z}^{\perp}\vec{H}_{k}+\langle\vec{H}_{k},\vec{H}_{k,0}\rangle\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\right)\right)=-2\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\nabla_{\overline{z}}\left(\nabla_{z}^{\perp}\vec{H}_{k}+\langle\vec{H}_{k},\vec{H}_{k,0}\rangle\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}e^{2\lambda_{k}}\left(\Delta_{g_{k}}^{\perp}\vec{H}_{k}-2|\vec{H}_{k}|^{2}\vec{H}+\mathscr{A}(\vec{H}_{k})\right)-4e^{2\lambda_{k}}\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\langle R(\vec{e}_{\overline{z}},\vec{e}_{z})\vec{e}_{z},\vec{H}_{k}\rangle\vec{e}_{\overline{z}}\right).

Using that
\vec{\Phi}_{k}
is Willmore
and using (
1.1
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\,(\nabla_{\overline{z}}\vec{Y}_{k})=\frac{1}{2}e^{2\lambda_{k}}\left(\mathscr{R}_{1}^{\perp}(\vec{H}_{k})-2\,\widetilde{K}_{h}\,\vec{H}_{k}+2\,\mathscr{R}_{2}(d\vec{\Phi}_{k})+(DR)(d\vec{\Phi}_{k})-8\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\langle R(\vec{e}_{\overline{z}},\vec{e}_{z})\vec{e}_{z},\vec{H}_{k}\rangle\vec{e}_{\overline{z}}\right)\right).

(3.55)

Therefore, by (
3.26
) and Hölder’s inequality, we get:

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Im}\,(\nabla_{\overline{z}}\vec{Y}_{k})|\leq Ce^{2\lambda_{k}(z)}\left(1+|\vec{H}_{k}|\right)\in\bigcap_{p<\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}}\mathrm{L}^{p}(B(0,R_{k})).

(3.56)

However, from (
3.37
) we have

\displaystyle\left|\overline{f_{k}}(\vec{Y}_{k})\right|\leq Ce^{\lambda_{k}}|\vec{Y}_{k}|\leq C^{\prime}\frac{\delta(|z|)}{|z|}\leq\frac{C^{\prime\prime}}{|z|^{2}},

which does not suffice as one cannot obtain elliptic estimates from a
\mathrm{L}^{1,\infty}
bound on the Laplacian.
In order to circumvent this problem, we will argue differently. Recall that for any vector-field
\vec{X}
, we defined

\displaystyle\nabla_{z}\vec{X}=\partial_{z}\vec{X}+f_{k}(\vec{X})=\partial_{z}\vec{X}+\left(\sum_{l=1}^{m}\gamma_{j,k}^{l}\vec{X}_{l}\right)_{1\leq j\leq m},

where

\displaystyle\gamma_{j,k}^{l}=\sum_{q=1}^{m}\Gamma_{j,q}^{l}\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}^{q}.

Recalling that
\vec{Y}_{k}=i\left(\nabla_{z}\vec{H}_{k}-3\,\nabla^{\perp}_{z}\vec{H}_{k}-i\,\star_{h}\left(\nabla_{z}\vec{n}_{k}\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\right)\right)
, we get

\displaystyle\vec{Y}_{k}

\displaystyle=i\left(\partial_{z}\vec{H}_{k}-3\pi_{\vec{n}_{k}}(\partial_{z}\vec{H}_{k})-2f_{k}(\vec{H}_{k})-i\star_{h}(\partial_{z}\vec{n}_{k}\wedge\vec{H}_{k})-i\star_{h}\left(G_{k}(\vec{n}_{k})\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=i\left(-2\,\partial_{z}\vec{H}_{k}+3\left(\partial_{z}\pi_{\vec{n}_{k}}\right)\vec{H}_{k}-2f_{k}(\vec{H}_{k})-i\star_{h}(\partial_{z}\vec{n}_{k}\wedge\vec{H}_{k})-i\star_{h}\left(G_{k}(\vec{n}_{k})\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\right)\right).

Since
e^{\lambda_{k}}
, and
e^{\lambda_{k}}\vec{H}_{k}
and
|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|
are bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,R_{k}))
by hypothesis, we deduce that there exists a constant
C>0
such that:

\displaystyle\left|\overline{f_{k}}(\vec{Y}_{k}+2i\,\partial_{z}\vec{H}_{k})\right|\leq C\left(|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|e^{\lambda_{k}}|\vec{H}_{k}|+e^{\lambda_{k}}|\vec{H}_{k}|+|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|e^{\lambda_{k}}|\vec{H}_{k}|+e^{2\lambda_{k}}|\vec{H}_{k}|\right)\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(B(0,R_{k})).

(3.57)

It follows that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\overline{f_{k}}(-2i\,\partial_{z}\vec{H}_{k})\right)_{j}=\mathrm{Im}\,\left(-2i\sum_{q=1}^{m}\Gamma_{j,q}^{l}\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{\Phi}_{k}^{q}\partial_{z}\vec{H}_{k,l}\right)=-2\,\sum_{q=1}^{m}\Gamma_{j,q}^{l}\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{\Phi}_{k}^{q}\partial_{z}\vec{H}_{k,l}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{q=1}^{m}\Gamma_{j,q}^{l}\langle\nabla\vec{\Phi}_{k}^{q},\nabla\vec{H}_{k,l}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\dive\left(\sum_{q=1}^{m}\Gamma_{j,q}^{l}\nabla\vec{\Phi}_{k}^{q}\,\vec{H}_{k,l}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{q=1}^{m}\langle\nabla\Gamma_{j,q}^{l},\nabla\vec{\Phi}_{k}^{q}\rangle\vec{H}_{k,l}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{q=1}^{m}\Gamma_{j,q}^{l}\Delta\vec{\Phi}_{k}^{q}\,\vec{H}_{k,l}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\dive\left(\sum_{q=1}^{m}\Gamma_{j,q}^{l}\nabla\vec{\Phi}_{k}^{q}\,\vec{H}_{k,l}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{q=1}^{m}\langle\nabla\Gamma_{j,q}^{l},\nabla\vec{\Phi}_{k}^{q}\rangle\vec{H}_{k,l}+\sum_{q=1}^{m}\Gamma_{j,q}^{l}e^{2\lambda_{k}}\vec{H}_{k,q}\vec{H}_{k,l}.

Since
e^{\lambda_{k}}\vec{H}_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,R_{k}))
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\overline{f_{k}}(-2i\,\partial_{z}\vec{H}_{k})\right)\in\mathrm{H}^{-1}+\mathrm{L}^{1}(B(0,R_{k})).

(3.58)

Therefore, by (
3.56
), (
3.57
) and (
3.58
), we finally deduce that

\displaystyle\Delta\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V_{k}})\in\mathrm{H}^{-1}+\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{C}).

Standard elliptic estimates imply that
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(\mathbb{C})
. Indeed, by the preceding estimates, we have a decomposition

\displaystyle\Delta\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})=\dive(\vec{X}_{k})+\vec{Z}_{k},

where
\vec{X}_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
\vec{Z}_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
. Therefore, making the decomposition
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})=\vec{u}_{k}+\vec{v}_{k}
, where

\displaystyle\Delta\vec{u}_{k}=\dive(\vec{X}_{k})\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta\vec{v}_{k}=\vec{Z}_{k},

we obtain:

\displaystyle\vec{v}_{k}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\Delta\vec{v}_{k}(\zeta)\log|z-\zeta|\,|d\zeta|^{2}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\vec{Z}_{k}(\zeta)\log|z-\zeta|\,|d\zeta|^{2}.

Clearly, it holds

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\vec{v}_{k}(z)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\vec{Z}_{k}(\zeta)}{z-\zeta}|d\zeta|^{2},

which, by Young inequality, yields:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\vec{v}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\leq C_{Y}\left\|\frac{1}{z}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\left\|\vec{Z}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{C})}\leq C.

Likewise, we have in the distributional sense

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\vec{u}_{k}(z)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\dive(\vec{X}_{k})(\zeta)}{z-\zeta}|\zeta|^{2}.

Now, since the Fourier transform of
\dfrac{1}{z}
is
\dfrac{c_{0}}{\overline{\xi}}
for some constant
c_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}(\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{X})(\xi)=-i\xi_{j}\mathscr{F}(\vec{X})(\xi)\,,\quad\text{ for all $j\in\left\{1,2\right\}$}.

Therefore, by the Parseval identity, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\nabla\vec{u}_{k}|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=4\int_{\mathbb{C}}\left|\partial_{z}\vec{u}_{k}\right|^{2}|dz|^{2}=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\left|\frac{1}{z}\ast\dive(\vec{X}_{k})\right|^{2}|dz|^{2}=\frac{c_{0}^{2}}{16\pi^{4}}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{1}{|\xi|^{2}}\left|-i\xi_{1}\vec{X}_{k,1}-i\xi_{2}\vec{X}_{k,2}\right|^{2}|d\xi|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{0}^{2}}{8\pi^{4}}\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\vec{X}_{k}|^{2}dx.

Finally, we deduce that
\nabla\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})
which shows in particular that
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,R_{k}))
. Furthermore, by the Sobolev embedding, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})\in\bigcap_{p<\infty}\mathrm{L}^{p}(B(0,R_{k})),

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

The next step will be to remove the
\log|z|
term in the estimate (
3.54
).

3.3
Removal of the logarithmic singularity in the estimate of
\vec{V}_{k}

Removing the
\log|z|
term in the inequality (
3.54
) is the most technical part of the proof of Theorem
3.2
. To this aim, we will introduce and use some (apparently new) Lorentz-type functional space. Let us stress out that this difficulty is due to the curved ambient space and therefore was not present in the proof of the energy quantization for Willmore surfaces in Euclidean spaces
[
4
]
.

Let us first make the decomposition
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})=\vec{u}_{k}+\vec{v}_{k}+\vec{w}_{k}
, where

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta\vec{u}_{k}&=4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\nabla_{\overline{z}}\vec{Y}_{k}\right)\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,{R_{k}}/{2})\\
\vec{u}_{k}&=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,{R_{k}}/{2})\,,\end{aligned}\right.

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta\vec{v}_{k}&=-4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\overline{f_{k}}(\vec{Y}_{k})\right)\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,{R_{k}}/{2})\\
\vec{v}_{k}&=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,{R_{k}}/{2})\,,\end{aligned}\right.

and

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta\vec{w}_{k}&=0\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,{R_{k}}/{2})\\
\vec{w}_{k}&=\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,{R_{k}}/{2}).\end{aligned}\right.

By the bound (
3.56
), we have
\Delta\vec{u}_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{C})
for all
p<\dfrac{1}{1-\varepsilon}
which implies by Calderón-Zygmund estimates that
\vec{u}_{k}\in\mathrm{W}^{2,p}(B(0,R_{k}/2))
for all
p<\dfrac{1}{1-\varepsilon}
. By standard elliptic regularity (
[
14
]
, Chapter
3
,
3.3
), we also get
\vec{w}_{k}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,R_{k}/2))
.
Regarding
\vec{v}_{k}
, it holds

\displaystyle\partial_{\overline{z}}\left(z^{2}\partial_{z}\vec{v}_{k}-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\zeta^{2}\mathrm{Im}\,(\overline{f_{k}}(\vec{Y}_{k})(\zeta))}{\zeta-z}|d\zeta|^{2}\right)=0,

and
\vec{v}_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})
, which implies that there exists a holomorphic function
h_{k}
such that

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\vec{v}_{k}=\frac{h_{k}(z)}{z^{2}}+\frac{1}{\pi z^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\zeta^{2}\mathrm{Im}\,(\overline{f_{k}}(\vec{Y}_{k})(\zeta)}{\zeta-z}|d\zeta|^{2}.

By the estimates in the proof of Lemmas
3.8
and
3.9

, we deduce that there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{\pi z^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\zeta^{2}\mathrm{Im}\,(\overline{f_{k}}(\vec{Y}_{k})(\zeta)}{\zeta-z}|d\zeta|^{2}-\frac{a_{0,k}}{z^{2}}\right|\leq C\frac{\log\left(\frac{R_{k}}{|z|}\right)}{|z|}.

(3.59)

Taking the expansion
h_{k}(z)=b_{0,k}+O(|z|)
, from
\nabla\vec{v}_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})
we deduce that

\displaystyle b_{0,k}=-a_{0,k},

(3.60)

and that

\psi_{k}(z)=\dfrac{h_{k}(z)-b_{0,k}}{z^{2}}\in\mathrm{L}^{p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{C})\,,\quad\text{for all $p<2$}.

(3.61)

More precisely, the estimate (
3.59
) shows that there exists a function
\psi_{1,k}
and a constant
C_{k}>0
such that

\displaystyle|\psi_{k}(z)-\psi_{1,k}(z)|\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}\left(B\left(0,\frac{R_{k}}{2}\right)\right)

(3.62)

and

\displaystyle|\psi_{1,k}(z)|\leq\frac{C_{k}}{|z|}\log\left(\frac{R_{k}}{|z|}\right).

(3.63)

Therefore, we deduce that
\psi_{k}
admits the Laurent expansion

\displaystyle\psi_{k}(z)=\sum_{n\geq-1}^{\infty}a_{k,n}z^{n}.

(3.64)

Next, we will show that

\psi_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,\alpha R_{k}))\,.

(3.65)

The proof is quite involved and will make use of some (apparently new) Lorentz-type function spaces.
