For a more systematic discussion of generalised Lorentz spaces, the reader is refereed to the Appendix
7
.

First, write for simplicity
R=\dfrac{R_{k}}{2}
, and for all
\alpha>0
, let
\varphi_{\alpha}:(0,R)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be defined by

\displaystyle\varphi_{\alpha}(t)=\frac{t}{\log^{\alpha}\left(\frac{R}{t}\right)}.

Let
W:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
be the Lambert function, which satisfies for all
x\geq 0

\displaystyle W(x)e^{W(x)}=x

(3.66)

One easily checks that

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha}(t)=Re^{-\alpha W\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\left(\frac{R}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)}

is the inverse of
\varphi_{\alpha}
.
Notice that for
\alpha=1
, the identiy (
3.66
) gives

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(t)=tW\left(\frac{R}{t}\right).

Explicit computations give

\displaystyle\varphi_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t)=\frac{1}{\log^{\alpha}\left(\frac{R}{t}\right)}+\frac{\alpha}{\log^{\alpha+1}\left(\frac{R}{t}\right)}>0\,,\quad\varphi_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}(t)=\frac{\alpha}{t\log^{\alpha+1}\left(\frac{R}{t}\right)}+\frac{\alpha(\alpha+1)}{t\log^{\alpha+2}(t)}>0\,,

which show that
\varphi_{\alpha}
is convex and strictly increasing. We deduce that for all
t>0
, it holds

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{2}\left(B(0,R)\cap\left\{x:\frac{\log^{\alpha}\left(\frac{R}{|x|}\right)}{|x|}>t\right\}\right)=\mathscr{L}^{2}\left(B(0,R)\cap\left\{x:\varphi_{\alpha}(|x|)<\dfrac{1}{t}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}^{2}\left(B(0,R)\cap\left\{x:|x|<\psi_{\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)\right\}\right)=\pi\min\left\{R^{2},\psi_{\alpha}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)\right\}=\pi\min\left\{R^{2},R^{2}e^{-2\alpha W\left(\alpha^{-1}(Rt)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\pi R^{2}e^{-2\alpha W\left(\alpha^{-1}(Rt)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)}\,.

The following asymptotic expansions hold:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} W(t)&=t+O(t^{2})\qquad&&\text{when}\;\,t\rightarrow 0\\
W(t)&=\log(t)-\log\log(t)+o(1)\qquad&&\text{when}\;\,t\rightarrow\infty\,.\end{aligned}\right.

Therefore, when
t\rightarrow 0
, we deduce that

\displaystyle e^{-2\alpha W\left(\alpha^{-1}(Rt)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)}=e^{-2(Rt)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}+O(t^{\frac{2}{\alpha}})}\underset{t\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}1\,,

so that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow 0+}t^{2}\mathscr{L}^{2}\left(B(0,R)\cap\left\{x:\frac{\log^{\alpha}|x|}{|x|}>t\right\}\right)=0\,.

However, when
t\rightarrow\infty
, we have

\displaystyle e^{-2\alpha\,W\left(\alpha^{-1}(Rt)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)}=e^{-2\alpha\left(\log\left(\alpha^{-1}(Rt)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)-\log\log\left(\alpha^{-1}(Rt)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)+o(1)\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{2(\alpha\log(\alpha)-\log(R))+o(1)}}{t^{2}}\log^{2\alpha}\left(\alpha^{-1}\left(Rt\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)=\frac{e^{A+o(1)}}{t^{2}}\left(\log^{2\alpha}(t)+O(1)\right).

for some
A\in\mathbb{R}
. This implies that

\displaystyle t^{2}\mathscr{L}^{2}\left(B(0,R)\cap\left\{x:\frac{\log^{\alpha}|x|}{|x|}>t\right\}\right)=\frac{e^{A+o(1)}}{t^{2}}\left(\log^{2\alpha}(t)+O(1)\right)\underset{t\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\infty.

Note that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}\left(\frac{t}{\log^{\alpha}(t)}\right)^{2}\mathscr{L}^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\cap\left\{x:\frac{\log^{\alpha}|x|}{|x|}>t\right\}\right)\leq C<\infty.

This suggests that
u=u_{\alpha}=1/\varphi_{\alpha}(|\,\cdot\,|)
belongs to a Lorentz space. To determine its weight, we first compute the function
u_{\ast}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
defined by

\displaystyle u_{\ast}(t)=\inf\left\{s>0:\mathscr{L}^{2}\left(B(0,R)\cap\left\{x:|u(x)|>s\right\}\right)\leq t\right\}.

For
t\leq\pi R^{2}
, it holds

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{2}\left(B(0,R)\cap\left\{x:|u(x)|>s\right\}\right)=\pi\psi^{2}\left(\frac{1}{s}\right)\leq t\Longleftrightarrow\frac{1}{s}\leq\varphi\left(\sqrt{\frac{t}{\pi}}\right)\Longleftrightarrow s\geq u\left(\sqrt{\frac{t}{\pi}}\right)

while
u_{\ast}(t)=0
, for
t\geq\pi R^{2}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle u_{\ast}(t)=u\left(\sqrt{\frac{t}{\pi}}\right)=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}{\log^{\alpha}\left(R\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}\right)},\,\quad\text{for $t\leq\pi R^{2}$}

and

\displaystyle u_{\ast}(t)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}\log^{\alpha}\left(R\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}\right)\qquad&&\text{for}\;\,t<\pi R^{2}\\
&0\qquad&&\text{for}\;\,t\geq\pi R^{2}\end{aligned}\right.

so that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}u_{\ast}(s)ds

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\pi}\left[2\sqrt{s}\log^{\alpha}\left(R\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{s}}\right)\right]_{0}^{t}+{2\pi\alpha R}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\log^{\alpha-1}\left(R\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{s}}\right)}{\sqrt{s}}ds

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\pi t}\log^{\alpha}\left(R\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}\right)+2\pi\alpha R\int_{0}^{t}\log^{\alpha-1}\left(R\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{s}}\right)\frac{ds}{\sqrt{s}},

which, for
\alpha=1
, shows that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}u_{\ast}(s)ds=2\sqrt{\pi t}\log\left(R\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}\right)+4\pi R\sqrt{t}.

First assume that
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
and notice that, for
s=\pi R^{2}v
, it holds:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}u_{\ast}(s)ds=\pi R^{2}\int_{0}^{\frac{t}{\pi R^{2}}}\frac{1}{R\sqrt{v}}\log^{\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{v}\right)du=\pi R\int_{0}^{\frac{t}{\pi R^{2}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{v}}\log^{\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{v}\right)dv.

We introduce the integrals
I(\alpha,r)
for
r\leq 1
defined by

\displaystyle I(\alpha,r)=\int_{0}^{r}\log^{\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)\frac{dt}{\sqrt{t}}.

It holds

\displaystyle I(\alpha,r)

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{r}\log^{\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)+2\alpha\int_{0}^{r}\log^{\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)\frac{dt}{\sqrt{t}}=2\sqrt{r}\log^{\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)+2\alpha\,I(\alpha-1,r)

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{r}\log^{\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)+4\alpha\sqrt{r}\log^{\alpha-1}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)+4\alpha(\alpha-1)I(\alpha-2,r).

Therefore, we deduce that provided that
\alpha=n+\beta
with
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
0\leq\beta<1
, we have

\displaystyle I(\alpha,r)=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}2^{k+1}\prod_{l=0}^{k-1}(n-l+\beta)\sqrt{r}\log^{n-l+\beta}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)+2^{n}\prod_{k=0}^{n}(n-k+\beta)\int_{0}^{r}\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)\frac{dt}{\sqrt{t}}.

Now, we will estimate the last integral by the method of stationary phase. A change of variable
x=\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)
shows that, for
p=\frac{1}{\beta}>1
, it holds

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)\frac{dt}{\sqrt{t}}=\int_{\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)}^{\infty}px^{p}e^{-\frac{x^{p}}{2}}dx

\displaystyle=\left[-2x\,e^{-\frac{x^{p}}{2}}\right]_{\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)}^{\infty}+2\int_{\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{x^{p}}{2}}dx

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{r}\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)+2\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)\int_{1}^{\infty}e^{-\log\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)\frac{x^{p}}{2}}dx,

where we performed a linear change of variable in the last integral. Now, if
F:(0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined by

\displaystyle F(t)=\int_{1}^{\infty}e^{-t\frac{x^{p}}{2}}dx,

(3.67)

then we can directly apply the method of stationary phase since
p>1
(it will be clear that it fails for
p=1
since in this case, we have
F(t)=2/t
). Indeed, if
\varphi:[1,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined by
\varphi(x)=-\frac{x^{p}}{2}
, then
\varphi
is strictly decreasing and
\varphi^{\prime\prime}(1)=-\frac{p(p-1)}{2}<0
, so the method of stationary phase (or rather Laplace’s method) implies that

\displaystyle F(t)\underset{t\rightarrow\infty}{\sim}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{-\varphi^{\prime\prime}(1)}}\frac{e^{-\frac{t}{2}}}{\sqrt{t}}=\frac{4\pi}{p(p-1)}\frac{e^{-\frac{t}{2}}}{\sqrt{t}}.

Applying it to
t=\log\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)
, we deduce that

\displaystyle 2\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)\int_{1}^{\infty}e^{-\log\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)\frac{x^{p}}{2}}dx\underset{r\rightarrow 0}{\sim}2\beta\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)\sqrt{\frac{4\pi}{1-\beta}}\sqrt{\frac{r}{\log\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)}}=2\beta\sqrt{\frac{4\pi}{1-\beta}}\sqrt{r}\log^{\beta-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right).

Therefore, we deduce that for
\alpha>0
, we have

\displaystyle I(\alpha,r)=2\sqrt{r}\log^{\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)}}\right).

In particular, choosing the function

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\alpha}(t)=\sqrt{t}\left(1+\log_{+}^{\alpha}\left(R\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}\right)\right),

(3.68)

we deduce that
u_{\alpha}\in M(\Lambda_{\alpha})=\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\log^{\alpha}}(B(0,R))
, where

\displaystyle M(\Lambda_{\alpha})=\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(B(0,R))\cap\left\{f:\|f\|_{M(\Lambda_{\alpha})}=\sup_{t>0}\left(\frac{1}{\Lambda_{\alpha}(t)}\int_{0}^{t}f_{\ast}(s)ds\right)<\infty\right\}.

Likewise, we define the space
N(\Lambda_{\alpha})=\mathrm{L}^{2,1}_{\log^{\alpha}}(B(0,R))
by

\displaystyle N(\Lambda_{\alpha})=\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(B(0,R))\cap\left\{f:\|f\|_{N(\Lambda_{\alpha})}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\Lambda_{\alpha}\left(\mathscr{L}^{2}\left(B(0,R)\cap\left\{x:|f(x)|>t\right\}\right)\right)dt<\infty\right\}.

Furthermore, notice that
\Lambda_{\beta}:(0,\infty)\rightarrow(0,\infty)
is non-zero, concave (for
\beta\leq 1
), and that
\Lambda_{\beta}
extends continuously to
0
and that
\Lambda_{\beta}(0)=0
. Therefore, we can apply the classical results of Steigerwalt and White to deduce
(
[
55
, Theorem
4.1
and Theorem
4.4
]
, see also Theorem
7.4
in the appendix) that
N(\Lambda_{\beta})^{\ast}=M(\Lambda_{\beta})
, and that for all
(f,g)\in N(\Lambda_{\beta})\times M(\Lambda_{\beta})
, the product
fg\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(B(0,R))
with

\displaystyle\left|\int_{B(0,R)}fg\,d\mathscr{L}^{2}\right|\leq\|f\|_{N(\Lambda_{\beta})}\|g\|_{M(\Lambda_{\beta})}.

To see that
\Lambda_{\beta}
is concave, we can assume that
t\leq\pi R^{2}
without loss of generality, so that up to a scaling
\Lambda_{\beta}
is concave if and only if

\displaystyle\psi(t)=\sqrt{t}\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)

is concave on
(0,1)
. We compute

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{t}}\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)-\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{t}}\log^{\beta-1}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4t^{\frac{3}{2}}}\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)-\frac{\beta}{2t^{\frac{3}{2}}}\log^{\beta-1}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)+\frac{\beta}{2t^{\frac{3}{2}}}\log^{\beta-1}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)+\frac{\beta(\beta-1)}{t^{\frac{3}{2}}}\log^{\beta-2}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4t^{\frac{3}{2}}}\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)-\frac{\beta(1-\beta)}{t^{\frac{3}{2}}}\log^{\beta-2}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)<0

for all
0<t<1
and
0\leq\beta\leq 1
. Now, fix
0<\beta\leq 1
, let
x\in B(0,\frac{R}{2})
and
0<r<\frac{R}{4}
. By the co-area formula, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x)}|u(x)|dx=\int_{r}^{2r}\left(\rho\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(x)}|u|d\mathscr{H}^{1}\right)\frac{d\rho}{\rho}\leq\log(2)\inf_{r<\rho<2r}\left(\rho\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(x)}|u|d\mathscr{H}^{1}\right).

Therefore, there exists
\rho\in(r,2r)
such that

\displaystyle\rho\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(x)}|u|d\mathscr{H}^{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\log(2)}\int_{B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x)}|u(x)|dx.

Now, we will show that if
u
is a holomorphic function on
B(0,R)
, then a
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\log^{\beta}}
control implies a
\mathrm{W}^{1,1}
control on
B(0,\alpha R)
for all
\alpha<1
. Since it seems not standard to us, we give a full proof of this claim (in fact, we are not aware of a previous study of such spaces in the past literature).
Using the
\mathrm{L}^{2,1}_{\log^{\beta}}/\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\log^{\beta}}
duality
(see Theorem
7.4
in the appendix, or
[
55
, Theorem
4.4
]
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x)}|u(x)|dx\leq\|1\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{log}^{\beta}}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0))}\|u\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\mathrm{log}^{\beta}}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0))}.

Notice that

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(t)=\mathscr{L}^{2}\left(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x)\cap\left\{x:1>t\right\}\right)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &3\pi r^{2}\qquad&&\text{if}\;\,t<1\\
&0\qquad&&\text{if}\;\,t\geq 1.\end{aligned}\right.

We have by definition

\displaystyle\|1\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{log}}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x))}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\lambda_{1}(t)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(1+\log_{+}^{\beta}\left(R\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{\lambda_{1}(t)}}\right)\right)dt=\sqrt{3\pi}r\left(1+\log_{+}^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{R}{r}\right)\right).

Finally, we deduce that

\displaystyle\rho\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(x)}|u|d\mathscr{H}^{1}\leq\frac{\sqrt{3\pi}}{\log(2)}r\left(1+\log_{+}^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{R}{r}\right)\right)\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log^{\beta}}^{2,\infty}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x))},

and that,
a fortiori
, for all
x\in B(0,\frac{R}{2})
and
0<r\leq\frac{R}{2}
such that
B(x,2r)\subset B(0,R)
, there exists
\rho\in[r,2r]
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(x)}|u|d\mathscr{H}^{1}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{3\pi}}{\log(2)}\left(1+\log_{+}^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{R}{r}\right)\right)\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log^{\beta}}^{2,\infty}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x))}.

(3.69)

Thanks to this result, we will now be able to show a variant of a lemma appearing in
[
26
]
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
u:B(0,R)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a holomorphic function and fix some
0\leq\alpha<1
,
0\leq\beta\leq 1
. Assume that
u\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\log^{\beta}}(B(0,R))
. Then
u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,\alpha R))
and there exists a universal constant
\Gamma_{0}

(
independent of
\alpha
and
\beta
)
such that

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,\alpha R))}\leq\Gamma_{0}\frac{\alpha}{(1-\alpha)}\left(1+\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)\right)\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log^{\beta}}^{2,\infty}(B(0,R))}.

Proof.

Write

\displaystyle u(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n}.

Notice that
u=\partial_{z}v
, where

\displaystyle v(z)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}b_{n}z^{n}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{a_{n-1}}{n}z^{n}.

(3.70)

First, using the estimate (
3.69
) applied to
\nabla v
at a point
z\in\partial B(0,\alpha R)
with
r=\frac{1}{2}(1-\alpha)R
, we deduce by the mean-value formula that for some
\rho\in[\frac{1}{2}(1-\alpha)R,(1-\alpha)R]
, we have

\displaystyle|\nabla v(z)|=|2\,\partial_{z}v(z)|=\left|\frac{1}{\pi\rho}\int_{\partial B(z,\rho)}\partial_{\zeta}v(\zeta)d\zeta\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\log(2)}\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\frac{1}{(1-\alpha)R}\left(1+\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)\right)\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log^{\beta}}^{2,\infty}(B(0,R))}

\displaystyle=\frac{8}{\log(2)}\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\frac{1}{(1-\alpha)R}\left(1+\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)\right)\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log^{\beta}}^{2,\infty}(B(0,R))},

(3.71)

where we used
|\nabla v|^{2}=4|\partial_{z}v|^{2}
by the holomorphy of
v
. Therefore, via integration by parts and using that both
u
and
\overline{u}
are harmonic, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,\alpha R)}|u(z)|^{2}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{B(0,\alpha R)}|\nabla v|^{2}dx=\frac{1}{2}\int_{B(0,\alpha R)}\dive\left(\overline{v}\nabla v\right)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial B(0,\alpha R)}\overline{v}\,{\partial_{\nu}{v}}\,d\mathscr{H}^{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial B(0,\alpha R)}\overline{(v-v_{\alpha R})}\partial_{\nu}v\,d\mathscr{H}^{1},

(3.72)

where for all
0<\rho<R
, we set

\displaystyle v_{\rho}={\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.57222pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.91281pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.30664pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.13547pt}}\!\int}_{\mkern-10.0mu\partial B_{\rho}(0)}v\,d\mathscr{H}^{1}.

Thanks to the
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
bound (
3.3
) and the Sobolev embedding
\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}(S^{1})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{1}(B(0,1))
, there exists a uniform constant
C_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,\alpha R))}^{2}=\left|\int_{\alpha R}\overline{(v-{v}_{\partial B_{\alpha R}})}\partial_{\nu}v\,d\mathscr{H}^{1}\right|\leq\left\|v-v_{\alpha R}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(\partial B_{\alpha R}(0))}\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\partial B_{\alpha R(0)})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\alpha R\left\|v\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial B_{\alpha R}(0))}\times\frac{8}{\log(2)}\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\frac{1}{(1-\alpha)R}\left(1+\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)\right)\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log^{\beta}}^{2,\infty}(B(0,R))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8C_{0}}{\log(2)}\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\frac{\alpha}{(1-\alpha)}\left(1+\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)\right)\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,\alpha R))}\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log^{\beta}}^{2,\infty}(B(0,R))}.

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,\alpha R))}\leq\frac{4C_{0}}{\log(2)}\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\frac{\alpha}{(1-\alpha)}\left(1+\log^{\beta}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)\right)\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log^{\beta}}^{2,\infty}(B(0,R))}

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We will also need the following lemma.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
R>0
and
u:B(0,R)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by a holomorphic function such that
u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,R))
. Then, for all
0<\alpha<1
, the following estimates hold:

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,\alpha R))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\alpha}{1-\alpha^{2}}\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,R))}

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(B(0,\alpha R))}

\displaystyle\leq 4\sqrt{\pi}\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(1-\alpha^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,R))}.

Proof.

Let
\left\{a_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be such that

\displaystyle u(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n}.

Taking complex coordinates, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,R)}|u(z)|^{2}|dz|^{2}=2\pi\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{R}|a_{n}|^{2}\rho^{2n+1}d\rho=\pi\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{|a_{n}|^{2}}{n+1}R^{2(n+1)}.

A direct computation shows that for all
n\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\left\||z|^{n}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,R))}=4\sqrt{\pi}R^{n+1}.

Therefore, we deduce by the triangle inequality and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,\alpha R))}

\displaystyle\leq 4\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|a_{n}|(\alpha R)^{n+1}\leq 4\sqrt{\pi}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)\alpha^{2(n+1)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{|a_{n}|^{2}}{n+1}R^{2(n+1)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\alpha}{1-\alpha^{2}}\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,R))}.

Then, we compute

\displaystyle\partial_{z}u(z)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}na_{n}z^{n-1}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)a_{n+1}z^{n}.

Using that

\displaystyle\left\||z|^{n}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(B(0,R))}=\frac{2\pi}{n+2}R^{n+2},\quad\text{for all $n\geq 0$}\,,

(3.73)

we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(B(0,\alpha R))}

\displaystyle=\left\|\partial_{z}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(B(0,\alpha R))}\leq 2\pi\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(n+1)}{(n+2)}|a_{n+1}|(\alpha R)^{n+2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(n+1)^{2}}{n+2}\alpha^{2(n+2)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{|a_{n+1}|^{2}}{n+2}R^{2(n+2)}\right)\leq 2\sqrt{\pi}\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(1-\alpha^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,R))}

where we used the following identities valid for
|a|<1

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(n+1)^{2}}{n+2}a^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)a^{n}-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{n+1}{n+2}a^{n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)a^{n}-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a^{n}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{a^{n}}{n+2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-a^{2})}-\frac{1}{1-a}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{a^{n}}{n+2},

and

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{a^{n}}{n+2}=\frac{1}{a^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{a^{n+2}}{n+2}=\frac{1}{a^{2}}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{a^{n+1}}{n+1}-a\right)=\frac{1}{a^{2}}\left(-\log(1-a)-a\right)

which shows that

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(n+1)^{2}}{n+2}a^{n+2}=\frac{a^{3}}{(1-a)^{2}}-\log(1-a)-a\leq\frac{a^{3}}{(1-a)^{2}}+\frac{a^{2}}{2(1-a)}=\frac{a^{2}+a^{3}}{2(1-a)^{3}}.

∎

Back to estimating
\psi_{k}
defined in (
3.61
)

Thanks to Lemmas
3.10
and
3.11
, recalling the estimates (
3.62
), (
3.63
) and (
3.64
), we deduce that
\psi_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\log}(B(0,R_{k}))
. Since
\psi_{k}
has a pole of order at most
1
at zero, this implies that

\displaystyle\psi_{k}(z)-\frac{a_{-1,k}}{z}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,1}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2,1}\left(B(0,{R_{k}}/{4})\right).

Notice that
a_{-1,k}
is bounded for
k\geq N
. Indeed,

\Psi_{k}(z)=z\psi_{k}(z)
is holomorphic, so the mean-value formula and (
3.69
) applied to
r=\frac{R_{k}}{2}
, yield that there exists
\rho\in[\frac{R}{2},R]
such that

\displaystyle|a_{-1,k}|

\displaystyle=|u(0)|=\frac{1}{2\pi\rho}\left|\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(0)}\Psi_{k}(z)dz\right|\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(0)}|\psi_{k}|d\mathscr{H}^{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{2\sqrt{3\pi}}{\log(2)}\left(1+\log\left(\frac{4}{\sqrt{3}}\right)\right)\left\|\psi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log}^{2,\infty}(B(0,R_{k}))}.

A pointwise estimate on
\vec{V}_{k}

Using variants of Lemmas
3.10
and
3.11
for harmonic functions (see
[
26
]
and
[
33
]
for more details), we also deduce that
\vec{w}_{k}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,1}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,R_{k}/4))
. We obtain a decomposition on
B(0,R_{k}/4)

\displaystyle\nabla\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{V}_{k}\right)=h_{1,k}(z)+h_{2,k}(z),

(3.74)

where

\displaystyle|h_{1,k}(z)|\leq C\frac{\log\left(\frac{R_{k}}{|z|}\right)}{|z|}

and
h_{2,k}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,1}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,R_{k}/4))
. More precisely, there exists a constant
C_{0}>0
independent of
k
such that for all
k\geq N
,

\displaystyle\left\|h_{2,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,R_{k}/4))}+\left\|\nabla h_{2,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(B(0,R_{k}/4))}\leq C_{0}.

Now, identifying
\mathbb{C}
with
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we can rewrite the equation for
\partial_{z}\vec{V}_{k}
as

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{x}\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{V}_{k})+\partial_{y}\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})&=2\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{Y}_{k}\right)\\
-\partial_{y}\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{V}_{k})+\partial_{x}\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})&=2\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{Y}_{k}\right)\,,\end{aligned}\right.

(3.75)

or, equivalently,

\displaystyle\nabla\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{V}_{k})-\nabla^{\perp}\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V_{k}})=2\,\overline{\vec{Y}_{k}}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{x}\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{V}_{k}\right)=\partial_{y}\vec{H}_{k}-3\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}\langle\partial_{y}\vec{H}_{k},\vec{n}_{k,j}\rangle\vec{n}_{k,j}+\star_{h}\left(\partial_{y}\vec{n}_{k}\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\right)-\partial_{y}\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})\\
&\partial_{y}\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{L_{k}})=-\partial_{x}\vec{H}_{k}+3\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}\langle\partial_{x}\vec{H}_{k},\vec{n}_{k,j}\rangle\vec{n}_{k,j}-\star_{h}\left(\partial_{x}\vec{n}_{k}\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\right)+\partial_{x}\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k}).\end{aligned}\right.

(3.76)

The identity (
3.76
) implies that

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{V}_{k})

\displaystyle=\cos(\theta)\partial_{x}\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{V}_{k})+\sin(\theta)\partial_{y}\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{V}_{k})=\left(\cos(\theta)\partial_{y}\vec{H}_{k}-\sin(\theta)\partial_{x}\vec{H}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle-3\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}\langle\cos(\theta)\partial_{y}\vec{H}_{k}-\sin(\theta)\partial_{x}\vec{H}_{k},\vec{n}_{k,j}\rangle\vec{n}_{k,j}+\star_{h}\left((\cos(\theta)\partial_{y}\vec{n}_{k}-\sin(\theta)\partial_{x}\vec{n}_{k})\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle-\cos(\theta)\partial_{y}\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})+\sin(\theta)\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{V}_{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}\vec{H}_{k}-3\pi_{\vec{n}_{k}}\left(\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}\vec{H}_{k}\right)+\star_{h}\left(\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}\vec{n}_{k}\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\right)-\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{V}_{k}\right),

(3.77)

since for any smooth function
u:B(0,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
a,b\in\mathbb{C}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}u=-\sin(\theta)\partial_{x}u+\cos(\theta)\partial_{y}u\,.

Now, recall the notation

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{V}_{k})_{\rho}={\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.57222pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.91281pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.30664pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.13547pt}}\!\int}_{\mkern-10.0mu\partial B(0,\rho)}\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{V}_{k}\right)d\mathscr{H}^{1}=\frac{1}{2\pi\rho}\int_{\partial B(0,\rho)}\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{V}_{k}\right)d\mathscr{H}^{1}\,,\quad\text{ for all $\alpha_{0}^{-1}r_{k}\leq\rho\leq\alpha_{0}R_{k}$}.

Since
\pi_{\vec{n}_{k}}(\vec{H}_{k})=\vec{H}_{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\pi_{\vec{n}_{k}}\left(\partial_{\theta}\vec{H}_{k}\right)=\partial_{\theta}\left(\pi_{\vec{n}_{k}}(\vec{H}_{k})\right)-\left(\partial_{\theta}\pi_{\vec{n}_{k}}\right)(\vec{H}_{k})=\partial_{\theta}\vec{H}_{k}-\left(\partial_{\theta}(\pi_{\vec{n}_{k}})(\vec{H}_{k})\right).

(3.78)

Therefore, by (
3.76
), (
3
) and (
3.78
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\rho}\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{V}_{k})_{\rho}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\partial_{\rho}\vec{V}_{k}(\rho,\theta)d\theta=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bigg(\frac{3}{\rho}(\partial_{\theta}\pi_{\vec{n}_{k}})\left(\vec{H}_{k}\right)+\star_{h}\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\partial_{\theta}\vec{n}_{k}\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\right)\bigg)d\theta.

(3.79)

Arguing as in the proof of
[
4
, Lemma VI.1]
using the
\varepsilon
-regularity Theorem
4.9
(adapted from Rivière’s original result
[
48
]
), we deduce that there exists
\varepsilon_{4}(n),C_{4}(n)>0
such that the following holds: if

\displaystyle\sup_{r_{k}<s<R_{k}}\int_{B_{2s}\setminus\overline{B}_{s}(0)}|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}dx\leq\varepsilon_{4}(n)

then

\displaystyle|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}(z)|^{2}\leq C_{4}^{2}(n)\frac{1}{|z|^{2}}\int_{B_{2|z|}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{|z|}{2}}}|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}dx\leq\frac{C_{4}^{2}(n)\varepsilon_{3}(n)}{|z|^{2}},\quad\text{for all $2r_{k}\leq|z|\leq\frac{R_{k}}{2}$}\,.

(3.80)

This motivates us to introduce the function
\delta_{k}:(0,R_{k})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined as

\displaystyle\delta_{k}(\rho)=\left(\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\int_{B_{2\rho}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{\rho}{2}}(0)}|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{\sqrt{15\pi}}{2}\left({\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.57222pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.91281pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.30664pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.13547pt}}\!\int}_{\mkern-10.0muB_{2\rho}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{\rho}{2}}(0)}|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We have in particular

\displaystyle\rho\,\delta_{k}(\rho)\leq\left(\int_{\Omega_{k}(1)}|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\sqrt{\Lambda(h)}.

(3.81)

Using Fubini’s theorem, we deduce that, for all
0<r<R<\frac{R_{k}}{2}
, it holds

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{R}\delta^{2}(\rho)\,\rho\,d\rho

\displaystyle=\int_{r}^{R}\left(\int_{B_{2R}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{r}{2}}(0)}|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\frac{\rho}{2}<|x|<2\rho\right\}}dx\right)\frac{d\rho}{\rho}=\int_{B_{2R}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{r}{2}}(0)}|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}\left(\int_{\frac{|x|}{2}}^{2|x|}\frac{d\rho}{\rho}\right)dx

\displaystyle=\log(4)\int_{B_{2R}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{r}{2}}(0)}|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}dx.

(3.82)

This implies by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{R}\delta_{k}(\rho)\,\rho\,d\rho\leq\left(\int_{r}^{R}\rho\,d\rho\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{r}^{R}\delta^{2}(\rho)\,\rho\,d\rho\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq R_{k}\left(\int_{B_{R}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{r}{2}}(0)}|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(3.83)

Furthermore, since
\vec{H}_{k}
satisfies the Euler-Lagrange equation

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{g_{k}}\vec{H}_{k}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{g_{k}}^{\ast}\left(\nabla_{g_{k}}\vec{H}_{k}-3\,\nabla_{g_{k}}^{\perp}\vec{H}_{k}+\star_{h}\left(\left(\ast\nabla_{g_{k}}\vec{n}_{k}\right)\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=-\mathscr{R}_{1}^{\perp}(\vec{H}_{k})+2\,\widetilde{K}_{h}\,\vec{H}_{k}-2\,\mathscr{R}_{2}(d\vec{\Phi}_{k})-(DR)(d\vec{\Phi}_{k})-\mathscr{R}_{3}^{\perp}(\vec{H}_{k}),

which is uniformly elliptic in all dyadic annuli in
\Omega_{k}(1)
thanks to the hypothesis and the Harnack equation on the conformal parameter (Lemma
3.3
, (
3.9
)), we deduce by standard elliptic regularity (
[
12
]
, Theorem
3.9
) that there exists a constant
\Gamma_{0}(n)
such that

\displaystyle r\left\|\nabla\vec{H}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\partial B(0,r))}\leq\Gamma_{0}(n)\left(\left\|\vec{H}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B_{\frac{4}{3}r}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{3}{4}r}(0))}+r^{2}\left\|\mathscr{L}_{g}\vec{H}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B_{\frac{4}{3}r}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{3}{4}r}(0))}\right).

(3.84)

Remark 3.12
.

In fact, one rather applies elliptic regularity to the operator
\mathscr{L}_{\vec{n}}
introduced below in (
4.4
), and to the equation (
4
). One checks that using this equation yields the same estimate for
\nabla\vec{H}_{k}
.

With the
\varepsilon
-regularity Theorem
4.9
inspired from Rivière’s one (
[
48
]
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|e^{\lambda_{k}}\vec{H}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B_{\frac{4}{3}r}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{3}{4}r}(0))}\leq\Gamma_{1}(n)\left\|e^{\lambda_{k}}\vec{H}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{r}{2}}(0))}.

(3.85)

Now, since

\displaystyle e^{-A}|z|^{d_{k}}\leq e^{\lambda_{k}(z)}\leq e^{A}|z|^{d_{k}},

we deduce that for all
0<\beta<1
and
r_{k}\leq\beta r<\beta^{-1}r<R_{k}
, we have for all
\beta r\leq|z|\leq\beta^{-1}r

\displaystyle e^{-A}\alpha^{|d_{k}|}r^{d_{k}}\leq e^{\lambda_{k}(z)}\leq e^{A}\alpha^{-|d_{k}|}r^{d_{k}}.

And since
d_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}d
, we deduce that there exists
B\in\mathbb{R}
independent of
k
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{r}{2}}(0)}e^{\lambda_{k}(z)}\leq e^{B}\inf_{z\in B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{r}{2}}(0)}e^{\lambda_{k}(z)}\,,\quad\text{for all $2r_{k}<r<\dfrac{R_{k}}{2}$}.

(3.86)

The combination of (
3.85
) and (
3.86
) gives

\displaystyle\left\|\vec{H}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B_{\frac{4}{3}r}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{3r}{4}}(0))}\leq e^{B}e^{-\lambda_{k}(r)}\Gamma_{1}(n)\left\|e^{\lambda_{k}}\vec{H}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{r}{2}}(0))}\leq e^{B}e^{-\lambda_{k}(r)}\Gamma_{1}(n)\delta(r).

(3.87)

Likewise, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathscr{L}_{g}\vec{H}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B_{\frac{4}{3}r}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{3}{4}r}(0))}

\displaystyle\leq C(h)\left(1+\left\|\vec{H}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B_{\frac{4}{3}r}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{3r}{4}}(0))}\right)\leq C(h)\left(1+e^{B}e^{-\lambda_{k}(r)}\Gamma_{1}(n)\delta(r)\right).

(3.88)

Since
r\delta(r)\leq\sqrt{\Lambda(h)}
, we finally deduce by (
3.84
), (
3.87
) and (
3.88
) that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} e^{\lambda_{k}(|z|)}|\vec{H}_{k}(z)|&\leq|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}(z)|\leq C_{4}(n)\delta(|z|)\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,z\in\Omega_{k}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\\
e^{\lambda_{k}(|z|)}|\nabla\vec{H}_{k}(z)|&\leq C_{5}(n,h)\frac{\delta(|z|)}{|z|}\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,z\in\Omega_{k}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\,.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.89)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d}{d\rho}\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{V}_{k})_{\rho}\right|\leq Ce^{-\lambda_{k}(\rho)}\delta^{2}(\rho).

This estimate implies that

\displaystyle\int_{2r_{k}}^{\frac{R_{k}}{2}}e^{\lambda_{k}(\rho)}\left|\frac{d}{d\rho}\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{V}_{k})_{\rho}\right|\,\rho\,d\rho\leq C\int_{\Omega_{k}(1)}|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}dx.

(3.90)

Define the function

\displaystyle a:(0,R_{k}/2)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}\,,\quad a(t)=\left|\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{V}_{k}\right)_{t}\right|.

As
|a^{\prime}(t)|=\left|\frac{d}{dt}\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{V}_{k}\right)_{t}\right|,
the combination of (
3.21
) and (
3.90
) gives that

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{R}t^{1+d_{k}}|a^{\prime}(t)|dt\leq C.

Since
d_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}d>-1
, from the identity

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{R}t^{1+d_{k}}a^{\prime}(t)dt=R^{1+d_{k}}a(R)-r^{1+d_{k}}a(r)-(1+d_{k})\int_{r}^{R}t^{d_{k}}a(t)dt\,,

(3.91)

we deduce that

\displaystyle r^{1+d_{k}}a(r)\leq\left(\frac{R_{k}}{2}\right)^{1+d_{k}}a\left(\frac{R_{k}}{2}\right)+C.

The estimate

\displaystyle|V_{k}(z)|\leq C_{2}\frac{\log\left(\frac{R_{k}}{|z|}\right)}{|z|}

implies that

\displaystyle\left(\frac{R_{k}}{2}\right)^{1+d_{k}}a\left(\frac{R_{k}}{2}\right)\leq C_{2}R_{k}^{d_{k}}\left|\log(R_{k})\right|,

which is bounded independently of
k\geq N
since
d_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}d>-1
and
R_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}R>0
. Finally, we deduce that for all
2r_{k}<|z|<\frac{R_{k}}{2}
, it holds

\displaystyle|z|^{d_{k}}\left|\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{V}_{k})_{|z|}\right|\leq\frac{C}{|z|}.

(3.92)

Lemma 3.13
.

For all
r>0
and
u\in\mathrm{W}^{1,1}(\partial B(0,r),\mathbb{R}^{n})
, it holds

\displaystyle\left\|u-u_{r}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\partial B(0,r))}\leq n\int_{\partial B(0,r)}|\nabla u|d\mathscr{H}^{1}.

(3.93)

Proof.

By scaling invariance, we can assume that
r=1
, which permits to see
u
as a
2\pi
-periodic function
u=(u_{1},\cdots,u_{n}):[0,2\pi]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
. By the intermediate values theorem, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, there exists
a_{i}\in[0,2\pi]
such that

\displaystyle u_{i}(a_{i})={\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.57222pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.91281pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.30664pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.13547pt}}\!\int}_{\mkern-10.0mu0}^{2\pi}u_{i}\,d\mathscr{L}^{1}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}u_{i}(\theta)d\theta.

Therefore, we have for all
1\leq i\leq n
and for all
\theta\in[0,2\pi]

\displaystyle\left|u_{i}(\theta)-{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.57222pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.91281pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.30664pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.13547pt}}\!\int}_{\mkern-10.0mu0}^{2\pi}u_{i}\,d\mathscr{L}^{1}\right|=|u_{i}(\theta)-u_{i}(a_{i})|=\left|\int_{a_{i}}^{\theta}u_{i}^{\prime}(t)dt\right|\leq\int_{0}^{2\pi}|u_{i}^{\prime}(t)|dt,

Therefore, by the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\left|u(\theta)-{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.57222pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.91281pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.30664pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.13547pt}}\!\int}_{\mkern-10.0mu0}^{2\pi}u\,d\mathscr{L}^{1}\right|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|u_{i}(\theta)-{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.57222pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.91281pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.30664pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.13547pt}}\!\int}_{\mkern-10.0mu0}^{2\pi}u_{i}^{\prime}d\mathscr{L}^{1}\right|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|u_{i}^{\prime}(t)|dt\leq n\int_{0}^{2\pi}|u^{\prime}(t)|dt,\quad\text{for all $\theta\in[0,2\pi]$}.

Coming back to the initial inequality, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|u-u_{r}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\partial B(0,r))}\leq n\int_{\partial B(0,r)}\left|\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}u\right|d\mathscr{H}^{1}\leq n\int_{\partial B(0,r)}|\nabla u|d\mathscr{H}^{1}

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Using the estimates
|\vec{Y}_{k}|\leq\dfrac{Ce^{-\lambda_{k}(|z|)}}{|z|^{2}}
,
|\nabla\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})|\leq\dfrac{C}{|z|}
and (
3.75
), we deduce that for all
z\in\Omega_{k}(\frac{1}{4})
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{V}_{k}(z)\right)-\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{V_{k}}\right)_{|z|}\right|

\displaystyle\leq n\int_{\partial B(0,|z|)}|\nabla\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{V}_{k})|d\mathscr{H}^{1}=n\int_{\partial B(0,|z|)}\left|2\overline{\vec{Y}_{k}}+\nabla^{\perp}\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})\right|d\mathscr{H}^{1}

\displaystyle\leq n\int_{\partial B(0,|z|)}\left(2|\vec{Y}_{k}|+|\nabla\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})|\right)d\mathscr{H}^{1}\leq\frac{4\pi nC}{|z|^{1+d_{k}}}+2\pi nC_{5}\log\left(\frac{R_{k}}{|z|}\right)

\displaystyle+n\int_{\partial B(0,|z|)}|h_{2}|d\mathscr{H}^{1},

where
h_{2}
is bounded in
\mathrm{W}^{1,1}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,R_{k}/4))
. In particular, by trace theory, we have
h_{2}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\partial B(0,|z|))
and for some universal constant independent of
|z|
, we have by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

\displaystyle\left\|h_{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(\partial B(0,|z|))}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{2}\left(\frac{1}{R_{k}}\left\|h_{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(B(0,R_{k}/4))}+\left\|\nabla h_{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(B(0,R_{k}/4))}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{4}\left\|h_{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,R_{k}/4))}+\left\|\nabla h_{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(B(0,R_{k}/4))}\right).

Therefore, we obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{V}_{k}(z))-\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{V}_{k}\right)_{|z|}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\pi nC}{|z|^{1+d_{k}}}+2\pi nC_{5}\log\left(\frac{R_{k}}{|z|}\right)+C.

Finally,
using that
d_{k}>-1+\varepsilon
and recalling the Harnack inequality (
3.21
), we deduce that

\displaystyle e^{\lambda_{k}(z)}|\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{V}_{k})(z)|\leq\frac{C}{|z|}+2\pi nC_{5}|z|^{-1+\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{R_{k}}{|z|}\right)+C|z|^{-1+\varepsilon}\leq\frac{C}{|z|}.

(3.94)

The previous estimate on
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})
(see (
3.74
)) coupled with the same argument on averages implies that

\displaystyle e^{\lambda_{k}(z)}|\vec{V}_{k}(z)|\leq\frac{C}{|z|},\quad\text{for all $z\in\Omega_{k}(1/4)$,}

(3.95)

which,
recalling the Harnack inequality (
3.21
) for the conformal parameters, in turn gives,

\displaystyle|z|^{d_{k}}|\vec{V}_{k}(z)|\leq\frac{C}{|z|}\,,\quad\text{for all $z\in\Omega_{k}(1/4)$.}

(3.96)

Analysis of the other cases

Now, assume that
d_{k}>0
. We will distinguish the case
d_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
and
d_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}d>0
.

Case 2:

d_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
. Define as above

\displaystyle\vec{U}_{k}(z)=\frac{1}{\overline{z}^{2}}\left(-\frac{1}{\pi\overline{\zeta}}\star\left(\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)\right)\right)=-\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{z-\zeta}}|d\zeta|^{2}.

Notice that
\partial_{z}\vec{U}_{k}=\vec{Y}_{k}
. Fix some
z\in B(0,R)
, where we recall that
r=2r_{k}
and
R=\dfrac{R_{k}}{2}
.

For
0<2|z|\leq r
, it holds

\displaystyle\vec{U}_{k}(z)=-\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}^{2}}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta-z}}|d\zeta|^{2}=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta^{l+1}}}|d\zeta|^{2}\right)z^{l}=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}a_{k,l}\overline{z}^{l}.

Likewise, we have

\displaystyle|a_{k,0}|\leq 2\pi C_{0}R\,.

Then, we estimate for
l=1

\displaystyle\left|\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta}^{l+1}}|d\zeta|^{2}\right|\leq C_{0}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{|d\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta|^{2+d_{k}}}=\frac{2\pi C_{0}}{d_{k}}\left(\frac{1}{(2|z|)^{d_{k}}}-\frac{1}{R^{d_{k}}}\right).

For
l\geq 2
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta}^{l+1}}|d\zeta|^{2}\right|\leq C_{0}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B(0,2|z|)}}\frac{|d\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta|^{l+1+d_{k}}}=\frac{2\pi C_{0}}{l-1+d_{k}}\left(\frac{1}{(2|z|)^{l-1}}-\frac{1}{R^{l-1+d_{k}}}\right).

Therefore, it holds

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{l=2}^{\infty}a_{k,l}z^{l}\right|\leq 2C_{0}|z|.

We deduce that

\displaystyle\left|\vec{U}_{k}(z)-\frac{a_{k,0}}{\overline{z}^{2}}\right|\leq\frac{2C_{0}}{|z|}\frac{1}{d_{k}}\left(\frac{1}{(2|z|)^{d_{k}}}-\frac{1}{R^{d_{k}}}\right)+\frac{2C_{0}}{|z|}.

Recalling the definition (
3.53
) of
\vec{V}_{k}(z)=\vec{U}_{k}(z)-\frac{a_{k,0}}{\overline{z}^{2}}
, we deduce that for all
\displaystyle|z|\leq\frac{r}{2}\leq\frac{R}{4}
, it holds

\displaystyle|\vec{V}_{k}(z)|\leq\frac{2C_{0}R^{-d_{k}}}{|z|}\frac{1}{d_{k}}\left(\left(\frac{R}{2|z|}\right)^{d_{k}}-1\right)+\frac{2C_{0}}{|z|}\leq\frac{4C_{0}}{|z|}\left(1+\frac{1}{d_{k}}\left(\left(\frac{R}{2|z|}\right)^{d_{k}}-1\right)\right)

(3.97)

for
k
large enough. Notice that for all
z\neq 0
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d_{k}}\left(\left(\frac{R}{2|z|}\right)^{d_{k}}-1\right)=\frac{1}{d_{k}}\left(e^{d_{k}\log\left(\frac{R}{2|z|}\right)}-1\right)=\log\left(\frac{R}{2|z|}\right)+O(d_{k})\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\log\left(\frac{R}{|z|}\right)\,,

which suggest at the light of the previous discussion that the function above in the right-hand side of (
3.97
) belongs to a Lorentz space. Although one can effectively prove such an estimate by introducing a sequence of Lorentz spaces
“converging”
towards
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\log}(B(0,R))
and generalise Lemma
3.10
(see the Appendix
7.2
), we will not need of this fact.

Assume that
2|z|>r
. It holds

\displaystyle\overline{z}^{2}\vec{U}_{k}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{B(0,2|z|)\setminus\overline{B}(0,r)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta-z}}d\overline{\zeta}\wedge d\zeta+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta-z}}d\overline{\zeta}\wedge d\zeta.

We first estimate directly

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{B(0,2|z|)\setminus\overline{B}(0,r)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta-z}}d\overline{\zeta}\wedge d\zeta\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{0}}{\pi}\int_{B(0,2|z|)}\frac{d\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta|^{d_{k}}|z-\zeta|}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{0}}{\pi}\int_{B(0,\frac{|z|}{2})}\frac{|d\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta|^{d_{k}}|z-\zeta|}+\frac{C_{0}}{\pi}\int_{B(0,2|z|)\setminus\overline{B}(0,\frac{|z|}{2})}\frac{|d\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta|^{d_{k}}|z-\zeta|}.

(3.98)

For all
\zeta\in B(0,\frac{|z|}{2})
, we have by the triangle inequality

\displaystyle|z-\zeta|\leq|z|-|\zeta|\geq\frac{|z|}{2},

which implies that

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,\frac{2}{|z|})}\frac{|d\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta|^{d_{k}}|z-\zeta|}\leq\frac{2}{|z|}\int_{B(0,\frac{|z|}{2})}\frac{|d\zeta|^{2}}{|z|^{d_{k}}}=\frac{4\pi}{|z|}\int_{0}^{\frac{|z|}{2}}r^{1-d_{k}}dr=\frac{4\pi}{2-d_{k}}|z|^{1-d_{k}},

(3.99)

while

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,2|z|)\setminus\overline{B}(0,\frac{|z|}{2})}\frac{|d\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta|^{d_{k}}|z-\zeta|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{d_{k}}}{|z|^{d_{k}}}\int_{B(0,2|z|)\setminus\overline{B}(0,\frac{|z|}{2})}\frac{|d\zeta}{|z-\zeta|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{d_{k}}}{|z|^{d_{k}}}\int_{B(z,3|z|)}\frac{|d\zeta|^{2}}{|z-\zeta|}=2^{d_{k}+1}\cdot 3\pi|z|^{1-d_{k}}.

(3.100)

Since
d_{k}<1
, the combination of (
3
), (
3.99
), (
3
) and (
3.101
) gives that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{B(0,2|z|)\setminus\overline{B}(0,r)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta-z}}d\overline{\zeta}\wedge d\zeta\right|\leq{16C_{0}}|z|^{1-d_{k}}.

(3.101)

The previous argument shows that (for the same constant
a_{k,0}
)

\displaystyle\left|-\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}^{2}}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta-z}}|d\zeta|^{2}-\frac{a_{k,0}}{\overline{z}^{2}}\right|\leq\frac{4C_{0}}{|z|}\left(1+\frac{1}{d_{k}}\left(\left(\frac{R}{2|z|}\right)^{d_{k}}-1\right)\right),

and

\displaystyle\left|\vec{U}_{k}(z)-\frac{a_{k,0}}{\overline{z}^{2}}\right|\leq\frac{4C_{0}}{|z|}\left(1+\frac{1}{d_{k}}\left(\left(\frac{R}{2|z|}\right)^{d_{k}}-1\right)\right)+\frac{16C_{0}}{|z|^{1+d_{k}}}.

Finally, we get that for all
z\in B(0,\frac{R}{2})
the following estimate holds:

\displaystyle|\vec{V}_{k}(z)|\leq\frac{4C_{0}}{|z|}\left(1+\frac{1}{d_{k}}\left(\left(\frac{R}{2|z|}\right)^{d_{k}}-1\right)\right)+\frac{16C_{0}}{|z|^{1+d_{k}}}.

(3.102)

Case 3:

0<d_{k}\leq 1
and
d_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}d\in(0,1)
. The estimate (
3.102
) gives that

\displaystyle|z|^{d_{k}}|\vec{V}_{k}(z)|\leq\frac{4C_{0}}{|z|}\left(|z|^{d_{k}}+\frac{1}{d_{k}}\left(\left(R^{d_{k}}-1\right)\right)\right)+\frac{16C_{0}}{|z|}\leq\frac{C}{|z|}

which shows that our needed estimate holds uniformly in
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Case 4:

d_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}d\geq 1
. By the previous cases, we can also assume that
d_{k}\geq 1
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
too. Let
a\in\mathbb{N}
such that
a\leq d_{k}<a+1
, and define

\displaystyle\vec{U}_{k}(z)=\frac{1}{\overline{z}^{a+2}}\left(-\frac{1}{\pi\overline{\zeta}}\ast\left(\overline{\zeta}^{a+2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)\right)\right)=-\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}^{a+2}}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{a+2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{z-\zeta}}|d\zeta|^{2}.

Observe that
\partial_{z}\vec{U}_{k}=\vec{Y}_{k}
. Fix some
z\in B(0,R)
, where we recall that
r=2r_{k}
and
R=\dfrac{R_{k}}{2}
.

For
0<2|z|\leq r
, it holds

\displaystyle\vec{U}_{k}(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}^{a+2}}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{a+2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta-z}}|d\zeta|^{2}=\frac{1}{\overline{z}^{a+2}}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\overline{\zeta}^{a-l+1}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)|d\zeta|^{2}\right)\overline{z}^{l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}a_{k,l}\overline{z}^{l-a-2}.

First, we have

\displaystyle|a_{k,0}|\leq\frac{C_{0}}{\pi}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}|\zeta|^{a-d_{k}-1}|d\zeta|^{2}=\frac{C_{0}}{a+1-d_{k}}\left(R^{a+1-d_{k}}-(2|z|)^{a+1-d_{k}}\right).

Then, for
l=1
:

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\overline{\zeta}^{a-l+1}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)|d\zeta|^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{0}}{\pi}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{|d\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta|^{2+d_{k}-a}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{aligned} &2C_{0}\log\left(\frac{R}{2|z|}\right)\qquad&&\text{if}\;\,d_{k}=a\\
&\frac{2C_{0}}{d_{k}-a}\left(\frac{1}{(2|z|)^{d_{k}-a}}-\frac{1}{R^{d_{k}-a}}\right)\qquad&&\text{if}\;\,d_{k}>a.\end{aligned}\right.

For
l\geq 2
, we have:

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\zeta^{a-l+1}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)|d\zeta|^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{0}}{\pi}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{|d\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta|^{d_{k}+l+1-a}}

\displaystyle\hskip-50.00008pt=\frac{2C_{0}}{d_{k}+l-a-1}\left(\frac{1}{(2|z|)^{d_{k}+l-a-1}}-\frac{1}{R^{d_{k}+l-a-1}}\right)\leq\frac{2C_{0}}{l-1}\frac{1}{(2|z|)^{d_{k}-a-1}}\frac{1}{(2|z|)^{l}}\,.

Therefore

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{l=2}^{\infty}a_{k,l}z^{l}\right|\leq 2C_{0}(2|z|)^{a+1-d_{k}}\sum_{l=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l-1}\frac{1}{2^{l}}=C_{0}(2|z|)^{a+1-d_{k}}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l+1}\frac{1}{2^{l+1}}=\log(2)C_{0}(2|z|)^{a+1-d_{k}}.

We deduce that for
d_{k}=a
, it holds

\displaystyle\left|\vec{U}_{k}(z)-\frac{a_{k,0}}{\overline{z}^{a+2}}\right|\leq\frac{4C_{0}}{|z|^{d_{k}+1}}+2C_{0}\frac{1}{|z|^{d_{k}+1}}\log\left(\frac{R}{2|z|}\right),

(3.103)

while for
d_{k}>a
,

\displaystyle\left|\vec{U}_{k}(z)-\frac{a_{k,0}}{\overline{z}^{a+2}}\right|\leq\frac{4C_{0}}{|z|^{1+d_{k}}}+\frac{2C_{0}}{d_{k}-a}\frac{1}{|z|^{a+1}}\left(\frac{1}{|z|^{d_{k}-a}}-\left(\frac{2}{R}\right)^{d_{k}-a}\right).

(3.104)

Now, if
2|z|>r
, we can make the same decomposition

\displaystyle\overline{z}^{a+2}\vec{U}_{k}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{B(0,2|z|)\setminus\overline{B}(0,r)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{a+2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta-z}}d\overline{\zeta}\wedge d\zeta+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{a+2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta-z}}d\overline{\zeta}\wedge d\zeta.

The second integral is estimated as above and we get

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{B(0,R)\setminus\overline{B}(0,2|z|)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta-z}}d\overline{\zeta}\wedge d\zeta-\frac{a_{k,0}}{\overline{z}^{a+2}}\right|\leq\frac{4C_{0}}{|z|^{1+d_{k}}}+\frac{2C_{0}}{d_{k}-a}\frac{1}{|z|^{a+1}}\left(\frac{1}{|z|^{d_{k}-a}}-\left(\frac{2}{R}\right)^{d_{k}-a}\right).

(3.105)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{B(0,2|z|)\setminus\overline{B}(0,r)}\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{a+2}\vec{Y}_{k}(\zeta)}{\overline{\zeta-z}}|d\zeta|^{2}\right|\leq\frac{C_{0}}{\pi}\int_{B(0,2|z|)\setminus\overline{B(0,r)}}\frac{|d\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta|^{d_{k}-a}|\zeta-z|}\leq 16C_{0}|z|^{a+1-d_{k}}

using the same proof given the estimate (
3.101
).
Finally, we deduce that

\displaystyle|\vec{V}_{k}(z)|\leq\frac{4C_{0}}{|z|}\left(1+\frac{1}{d_{k}}\left(\left(\frac{R}{2|z|}\right)^{d_{k}}-1\right)\right)+\frac{16C_{0}}{|z|^{1+d_{k}}}.

(3.106)

Sub-case 1:

d_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}d
with
a<d\leq a+1
. Then the estimate (
3.106
) immediately implies the bound

\displaystyle|z|^{d_{k}}|\vec{V}_{k}(z)|\leq\frac{C}{|z|},\quad\text{ for all $z\in\Omega_{k}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)$.}

Sub-case 2:

d_{k}=a
. Then we can apply the same argument on averages as in the case
a=-1
and the rest of the proof is unchanged (notice that the proof of the estimate on
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})
is independent of the multiplicity
d_{k}
).

Sub-case 3:

d_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}a
and
d_{k}>a
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
(notice that it covers all cases by the previous case). Then we apply the same averaging argument as before the statement of Lemma
3.13
and find

\displaystyle r^{1+d_{k}}a(r)\leq\left(\frac{R_{k}}{2}\right)^{1+d_{k}}a\left(\frac{R_{k}}{2}\right)+C,

which shows by (
3.104
) that

\displaystyle r^{1+d_{k}}a(r)\leq 4C_{0}+C.

Notice that if the estimate had been replaced by an estimate on
\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0})
for all
0<\alpha_{0}<\dfrac{1}{2}
, we would have had instead

\displaystyle r^{1+d_{k}}a(r)\leq 4C_{0}+\frac{2C_{0}}{d_{k}-a}\frac{1}{(\alpha_{0}R_{k})^{a+1}}\left(\frac{1}{(\alpha_{0}R_{k})^{d_{k}-a}}-\left(\frac{2}{R_{k}}\right)^{d_{k}-a}\right).

Since
d_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}a
and
R_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}R\in(0,\infty)
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\alpha_{0}R_{k})^{d_{k}-a}}=e^{-(d_{k}-a)\log(\alpha_{0}R_{k})}=1-(d_{k}-a)\log(\alpha_{0}R_{k})+O\left((d_{k}-a)^{2}\right)

which gives that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d_{k}-a}\left(\frac{1}{(\alpha_{0}R_{k})^{d_{k}-a}}-\left(\frac{2}{R_{k}}\right)^{d_{k}-a}\right)\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\log\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha_{0}}\right),

showing that

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\in\rightarrow\infty}\left(4C_{0}+\frac{2C_{0}}{d_{k}-a}\frac{1}{(\alpha_{0}R_{k})^{a+1}}\left(\frac{1}{(\alpha_{0}R_{k})^{d_{k}-a}}-\left(\frac{2}{R_{k}}\right)^{d_{k}-a}\right)\right)=4C_{0}+\frac{2C_{0}}{(\alpha_{0}R)^{a+1}}\log\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha_{0}}\right)<\infty.

In the next proposition, we summarise the point-wise estimates on
\vec{V}_{k}
proved in this section.

Proposition 3.14
.

Under the hypothesis of Theorem
3.2
, there exists
C=C(m,h,\Lambda)>0
and
\alpha_{0}>0
such that

\vec{V}_{k}
defined in (
3.53
) satisfies the pointwise estimates

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} e^{\lambda_{k}(z)}|\vec{V}_{k}(z)|&\leq\frac{C}{|z|},\quad\text{for all $z\in\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0})$}\,,\\
|z|^{d_{k}}\,|\vec{V}_{k}(z)|&\leq\frac{C}{|z|},\quad\text{for all $z\in\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0})$}\,.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.107)

Estimates on
\vec{W}_{k}
and
\vec{L}_{k}

First, will construct a function
\vec{U}_{k}
on
B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k})
, for
\alpha_{0}>0
, such that

\nabla_{z}\vec{U}_{k}=\partial_{z}\vec{U}_{k}+\vec{f}_{k}(\vec{U}_{k})=\partial_{z}\vec{V}_{k}\,,\quad\text{on $\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0})$}.

(3.108)

Note this is equivalent to

\displaystyle\vec{U}_{k}=\vec{V}_{k}-\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}}\ast f_{k}(\vec{U}_{k})\,,\quad\text{on $\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0})$}.

This will be achieved by a fixed point argument similar to the one of
[
38
, Lemma A.
1
]
.

First, extend by
0
the restriction of
\vec{V}_{k}
to
B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k})
, and using a smooth non-negative cut-off
\eta
such that
\eta=1
on
B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k})
and
\mathrm{supp}\,(\eta)\subset B(0,2\alpha_{0}R_{k})
, we can assume that
\mathrm{supp}\,(\gamma_{j}^{l})\subset B(0,2\alpha_{0}R_{k})
for all
1\leq j,l\leq m
. Then, define
\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}:\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}(z)=\lambda_{k}(z)\eta(z)-(1-\eta(z))\log(1+|z|^{2}).

Consider the normed space

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}(\mathbb{C})=\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{C})\cap\left\{u:e^{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}u\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})\right\}\,,\quad\text{for all $k\in\mathbb{N}$},

equipped with the norm

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}=\left\|e^{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}.

One checks immediately that
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}(\mathbb{C})
is a Banach space (for example, by the series criterion using that
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})
is a Banach space). Now, by the previous estimate (
3.107
), we deduce that
\vec{V}_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{(2,\infty)}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}(\mathbb{C})
.

We introduce the operator
T:\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{L}^{(2,\infty)}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}(\mathbb{C})
, defined by

T(\vec{U})=\vec{V}_{k}-\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}}\ast f_{k}(\vec{U}).

Using the Young inequality for convolution, for all
2<p<\infty
, if
q<2
is such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{q}-1,

using the support assumption on
\gamma_{j}^{l}
and that
|f_{k}(\vec{U}_{k})|\leq C_{0}e^{\lambda_{k}}|\vec{U}_{k}|
,
we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}}\ast f_{k}(\vec{U})\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\leq C(p)\left\|\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\left\|f_{k}(\vec{U}_{k})\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{2p}{p+2},\infty}(\mathbb{C})}=2\sqrt{\pi}C(p)\left\|f_{k}(\vec{U}_{k})\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{2p}{p+2}}(B(0,2\alpha_{0}R_{k}))}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{\pi}C(p)\left\|1\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{p+2}{p}}(B(0,2\alpha_{0}R_{k}))}\left\|f_{k}(\vec{U}_{k})\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,2\alpha_{0}R_{k}))}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{\pi}C_{0}C(p)\pi^{\frac{p}{p+2}}(2\alpha_{0}R_{k})^{\frac{2p}{p+2}}\left\|\vec{U}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}.

(3.109)

Since
e^{\lambda_{k}}\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(B(0,R_{k}))
for all
p<\dfrac{2}{1-\varepsilon}
, using Hölder’s inequality we deduce that for all
2<p<\dfrac{2}{1-\varepsilon}
it holds

\displaystyle\left\|T(\vec{U})-\vec{V}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}=\left\|e^{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}\left(T(\vec{U})-\vec{V}_{k}\right)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\leq C(p)\left\|e^{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\left\|T(\vec{U})-\vec{V}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{p}{p-2},\infty}(\mathbb{C})}

\displaystyle\leq C(p)\left(C_{\alpha_{0}}\left\|r_{k}^{d_{k}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(B(0,\alpha_{0}^{-1}r_{k}))}+e^{A}\left\||z|^{d_{k}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(B(0,2\alpha_{0}R_{k}))}+\left\|\frac{1}{1+|z|^{2}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{C})}\right)\left\|\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}}\ast f_{k}(\vec{U})\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{p}{p-2},\infty}(\mathbb{C})}

\displaystyle\leq C(p)\left(C_{\alpha_{0}}(2\pi)^{\frac{1}{p}}r_{k}^{\frac{2}{p}+d_{k}}+(2\pi)^{\frac{1}{p}}\frac{(2\alpha_{0}R_{k})^{\frac{2}{p}+d_{k}}}{(2+pd_{k})^{\frac{1}{p}}}+\sqrt{\pi}\right)

\displaystyle\times 2\sqrt{\pi}C_{0}C\left(\frac{p}{p-2}\right)\pi^{\frac{p-2}{3p-4}}(2\alpha_{0}R_{k})^{\frac{2p-4}{3p-4}}\left\|\vec{U}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}.

In particular, choosing
p=2+\varepsilon<\frac{2}{1-\varepsilon}
, we obtain that there exists a constant
C(\varepsilon)>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\|T(\vec{U})-\vec{V}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\leq C(\varepsilon)\alpha_{0}^{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}\left\|\vec{U}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}.

Therefore, taking
\alpha_{0}=\left(\frac{1}{2C(\varepsilon)}\right)^{\frac{4}{\varepsilon}}
, we get that

\displaystyle\left\|T(\vec{U})-\vec{V}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\|\vec{U}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}.

Since
\vec{V}_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}(\mathbb{C})
, we conclude that
T:\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}(\mathbb{C})
is a contraction which implies in particular that
T
admits a unique fixed point
\vec{U}_{k}
.

The function
\vec{U}_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\widetilde{\lambda_{k}}}(\mathbb{C})
satisfies

\displaystyle\vec{U}_{k}=\vec{V}_{k}-\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}}\ast f_{k}(\vec{U}_{k}).

(3.110)

Since
f_{k}(\vec{U}_{k})\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))
, the previous estimate (
3
) shows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}}\ast f_{k}(\vec{U}_{k})\in\bigcap_{p<\infty}\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{C}).

Since
e^{\lambda_{k}}\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))
for all
p<\dfrac{2}{1-\varepsilon}
, we deduce by Hölder’s inequality that

\displaystyle e^{\lambda_{k}}\,\left(\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}}\ast f_{k}(\vec{U}_{k})\right)\in\bigcap_{p<\frac{2}{1-\varepsilon}}\mathrm{L}^{p}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k})).

Now, recalling the pointwise estimate (
3.107
), we deduce that there exists
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))
for all
p<\frac{2}{1-\varepsilon}
such that

\displaystyle e^{\lambda_{k}}|\vec{U}_{k}|\leq\frac{C}{|z|}+f(z)\qquad\text{for all}\;\,z\in\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}).

Then, using an estimate presented in the proof of
[
38
, Lemma A.
2
]
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\left(\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}}\ast f_{k}(\vec{U}_{k})\right)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\leq C\left\|f_{k}(\vec{U}_{k})\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\leq C.

Since
\nabla\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})
, by (
3.110
) we conclude that

\displaystyle\nabla\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{U}_{k})=\nabla\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{k})-\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\nabla\left(\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}}\ast f_{k}(\vec{U}_{k})\right)\right)\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C}).

Now, we can apply the exact same proof as Lemma A.
1
(see also Lemma
3.15
for more details) to obtain by a similar contraction argument as above the existence of
\vec{W}_{k}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(\mathbb{C})
such that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \nabla_{z}\vec{W}_{k}&=0\qquad\text{in}\;\,B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k})\\
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{W}_{k})&=\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{U}_{k})\qquad\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}).\end{aligned}\right.

Finally, defining
\vec{L}_{k}=\vec{U}_{k}-\vec{W}_{k}:B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &e^{\lambda_{k}}\vec{L}_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))\\
&\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{L}_{k})\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))\end{aligned}\right.

and

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\nabla_{z}\vec{L}_{k}=\vec{Y}_{k}\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k})\\
&\Delta\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{L}_{k}\right)=4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\nabla_{\overline{z}}\vec{Y}_{k}\right)-4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(f_{k}(\vec{Y}_{k})\right)-4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}\left(f_{k}(\vec{U}_{k})\right)\right)\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k})\\
&\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{L}_{k})=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}).\end{aligned}\right.

Furthermore, there exists

l_{k}
bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{p}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))
for all
p<\dfrac{2}{1-\varepsilon}
such that

\displaystyle e^{\lambda_{k}(z)}|\vec{L}_{k}(z)|\leq\frac{C}{|z|}l_{k}(z),\quad\text{on }\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0})\,.

(3.111)

Now, we introduce a refinement of
[
38
, Lemma A.
2
]
.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
2<p<\infty
and
1<q<\infty
be fixed real numbers. There exists constants
\varepsilon_{3}(n,p,q),C_{3}(n,p,q)>0
with the following property.
For all
j,k\in\left\{1,\cdots,n\right\}
, let
\gamma_{j}^{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{C})\cap\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(\mathbb{C})
be such that
\mathrm{supp}\,(\gamma_{j}^{k})\subset B(0,2)
and
\left\|\gamma_{j}^{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\mathbb{C})}\leq\varepsilon_{0}
. For all
\vec{U}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{C})
, define

\displaystyle\left(\nabla_{z}\vec{U}\right)_{j}

\displaystyle=\partial_{z}\vec{U}_{j}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\gamma_{j}^{k}\vec{U}_{k}\qquad\text{in}\;\,\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{C}),\;\,\text{where}\;\,1\leq j\leq n.

Then for all
\vec{Y}\in\left(\mathrm{L}^{1}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}\right)(\mathbb{C})
,
if
\left\|\gamma_{j}^{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(B(0,2))}\leq\varepsilon_{3}
there exists a unique
\vec{U}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,1))
such that
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{U})\in\mathrm{W}^{2,q}(B(0,1))
satisfying

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \nabla_{z}\vec{U}&=\vec{Y}\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(B(0,1))\\
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{U})&=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,1).\end{aligned}\right.

Furthermore, we have the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\vec{U}\right\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,1))}\leq C_{3}\left(\left\|\vec{Y}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{C})}+\left\|\vec{Y}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\right)\,.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
3.6
, the same argument shows that if
T
is defined analogously, we have for all
\vec{U}\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})
the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|T(\vec{U})\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\leq C\left\|\vec{Y}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{C})}

and that for all
\vec{U}_{1},\vec{U}_{2}\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})
if
\varepsilon_{3}\leq\delta_{0}
is small enough, we have

\displaystyle\left\|T(\vec{U}_{1})-T(\vec{U}_{2})\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\leq\frac{1}{4}\left\|\vec{U}_{1}-\vec{U}_{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}.

By the same argument as
[
38
]
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla T(\vec{U})_{j}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{2}\left\|\vec{Y}_{j}-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\gamma_{k}^{k}\vec{U}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\leq\Gamma_{2}\left\|\vec{Y}_{j}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Gamma_{2}\left\|\gamma_{j}^{k}\vec{U}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,2))}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{2}\left\|\vec{Y}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Gamma_{2}\left\|\gamma_{j}^{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(B(0,2))}\left\|\vec{U}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{2p}{p-2}}(B(0,2))}.

By the Sobolev embedding if
q<2
, we have
\mathrm{W}^{1,q}(B(0,2))\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{q^{\ast}}(B(0,2))
, where

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q^{\ast}}=\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{2}=\frac{2-q}{2q}.

Since we want to impose
q^{\ast}=\dfrac{2p}{p-2}
where
p>2
, this implies that
q=p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{p-1}

Therefore, we deduce that there exits a universal constant
\Gamma_{4}
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\vec{U}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{2p}{p-2}}(B(0,2))}\leq\Gamma_{4}\left(\left\|\vec{U}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{p}{p-1}}(B(0,2))}+\left\|\nabla\vec{U}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{p}{p-1}}(B(0,2))}\right).

Now, let
r>1
such that
rp^{\prime}=2
,
i.e.

r=\dfrac{2}{p^{\prime}}=\dfrac{2(p-1)}{p}
. By the
\mathrm{L}^{\frac{2(p-1)}{p-2},1}/\mathrm{L}^{\frac{2(p-1)}{p},\infty}
duality, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|\vec{U}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{p}{p-1}}(B(0,2))}\leq\left\|1\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{2(p-1)}{p},1}(B(0,2))}\left\|\vec{U}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,2))}=2^{\frac{3p-4}{p-1}}\frac{(p-1)^{2}}{p(p-2)}\pi^{\frac{p-2}{2(p-1)}}\left\|\vec{U}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,2))},

and likewise

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\vec{U}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{p}{p-1}}(B(0,2))}\leq 2^{\frac{3p-4}{p-1}}\frac{(p-1)^{2}}{p(p-2)}\pi^{\frac{p-2}{2(p-1)}}\pi^{\frac{p}{2(p-2)}}\left\|\nabla\vec{U}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,2))}.

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla T(\vec{U})\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{2}n\left\|\vec{Y}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}

\displaystyle+\Gamma_{2}n^{2}2^{\frac{3p-4}{p-1}}\frac{(p-1)^{2}}{p(p-2)}\pi^{\frac{p-2}{2(p-1)}}\varepsilon_{4}\left(\left\|\vec{U}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,2))}+\left\|\nabla\vec{U}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,2))}\right),

and for all
\vec{U}_{1},\vec{U}_{2}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(\mathbb{C})
, the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla T(\vec{U}_{1})-\nabla T(\vec{U}_{2})\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}\leq\Gamma_{2}n^{2}2^{\frac{3p-4}{p-1}}\frac{(p-1)^{2}}{p(p-2)}\pi^{\frac{p-2}{2(p-1)}}\pi^{\frac{p}{2(p-2)}}\varepsilon_{4}\left\|\vec{U}_{1}-\vec{U}_{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(\mathbb{C})}.

Therefore, taking

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{4}=\min\left\{\delta_{0},\frac{1}{4}\left(\Gamma_{2}n^{2}2^{\frac{3p-4}{p-1}}\frac{(p-1)^{2}}{p(p-2)}\pi^{\frac{p-2}{2(p-1)}}\right)^{-1}\right\},

we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|T(\vec{U}_{1})-T(\vec{U}_{2})\right\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(\mathbb{C})}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\|\vec{U}_{1}-\vec{U}_{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(\mathbb{C})}.

Therefore,
T
has a unique fixed point that we denote
\vec{U}_{0}
. Define by
f:\mathbb{C}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
the linear map such that for all
\vec{X}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C}^{n})
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{z}\vec{X}=\partial_{z}\vec{X}+f(\vec{X}).

Now, to get the boundary condition, define the operator
S:\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,1),\mathbb{C}^{n})\rightarrow\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,1),\mathbb{C}^{n})
such that for all
\vec{U}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,1),\mathbb{C}^{n})
,
\vec{V}=S(\vec{U})
is the unique solution of the equation

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{z}\vec{V}&=-f(\vec{U})\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,1)\\
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V})&=\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{U}_{0})\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,1).\end{aligned}\right.

(3.112)

Since we only prescribe the imaginary part of
\vec{V}
, this system admits a unique solution. First, by the Cauchy formula, if

\displaystyle\vec{V}_{0}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{f(\vec{U})}{\overline{\zeta-z}}d{\zeta}\wedge d\overline{\zeta},

then we have
\partial_{z}\vec{V}_{0}=-f(\vec{U})
and
\vec{V}_{0}=0
on
\partial B(0,1)
. Furthermore, by the previous convolution estimates, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\vec{V}_{0}\right\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,1))}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\|\vec{U}\right\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,1))}.

(3.113)

Now, first solve in
\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,1),\mathbb{R}^{n})
the equation

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta\vec{V}_{1}&=0\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,1)\\
\vec{V}_{1}&=\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{U}_{0})\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,1).\end{aligned}\right.

Then we have by Calderón-Zygmund estimate
\vec{V}_{1}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,1))
. If
\vec{V}_{2}
is the harmonic conjugate of
\vec{V}_{1}
, then by interpolation theory, we also deduce that
\vec{V}_{2}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,1))
and that for some universal constant
C
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\vec{V}_{1}\right\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,1))}+\left\|\vec{V}_{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,1))}\leq C\left\|\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{U}_{0})\right\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,1))}.

By construction, the function
\vec{V}_{3}=\vec{V}_{1}+i\vec{V}_{2}=i(\vec{V}_{2}-i\vec{V}_{1})
is holomorphic, which implies that
\vec{V}_{4}=i\overline{\vec{V}_{3}}=\vec{V}_{2}+i\vec{V}_{1}
is anti-holomorphic,
i.e.

\partial_{z}\vec{V}_{4}=0
. Furthermore, we have by construction
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{V}_{4})=\vec{V}_{1}=\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{U}_{0})
on
\partial B(0,1)
. Therefore, the function
\vec{V}_{0}+\vec{V}_{4}
is the unique solution to the system (
3.112
). Furthermore, by the estimate (
3.113
),
S
is a contraction, so we get a unique fixed point
\vec{U}_{1}
of
S
, which satisfies
\nabla_{z}\vec{U}_{1}=0
and
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{U}_{1})=\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{U}_{0}\right)
on
\partial B(0,1)
. Therefore, the function
\vec{U}=\vec{U}_{0}-\vec{U}_{1}
is the unique solution to the system of the theorem.
∎

Recall the expansion

\displaystyle\vec{L}_{k}=\vec{V}_{k}-\vec{W}_{k}-\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}}\ast f_{k}(\vec{U}_{k}),

where
f_{k}(\vec{U}_{k})\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}}(\mathbb{C})
,
\vec{W}_{k}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))
. Moreover,

\displaystyle e^{\lambda_{k}(z)}|\vec{V}_{k}(z)|\leq\frac{C}{|z|}\,,\quad\text{for all $z\in\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0})$}\,.

From
[
38
, Lemma A.
1
]
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi\overline{z}}\ast f_{k}(\vec{U}_{k})\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(\mathbb{C}),

which finally implies that

\displaystyle\vec{L}_{k}=\vec{V}_{k}+\widetilde{\vec{W}}_{k},\quad\text{with $\widetilde{\vec{W}}_{k}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))$}\,.

For simplicity, we shall rename
\widetilde{\vec{W}}_{k}
as
\vec{W}_{k}
. Summarising, we proved the following:

Theorem 3.16
.

Under the hypothesis of Theorem
3.2
, there exists
C_{1}(m,h,\Lambda)
,
\alpha_{0}>0
and a measurable function
\vec{L}_{k}:B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{m}
satisfying the equation

\displaystyle\nabla_{z}\vec{L}_{k}=\vec{Y}_{k}\qquad\text{on}\;\,\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}).

Moreover, the following decomposition holds:
\vec{L}_{k}=\vec{V}_{k}+\vec{W}_{k}
, where

\displaystyle e^{\lambda_{k}(z)}|\vec{V}_{k}(z)|\leq\frac{C_{1}}{|z|},\quad\text{for all $z\in\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0})$},

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{L}_{k})\;\,\text{and}\;\,\vec{W}_{k}\;\,\text{are bounded in $\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))$ uniformly in $k\in\mathbb{N}$.}

3.4
Conclusion of the proof of Theorem
3.2

By Lemma
3.15
, we deduce that there exists
S_{k}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}),\mathbb{C})
such that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{z}S_{k}&=\left\langle\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k},\overline{\vec{L}_{k}}\right\rangle\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k})\\
\mathrm{Im}\,(S_{k})&=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k})\end{aligned}\right.

(3.114)

Furthermore, by
[
38
, (
6.7
)]
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}\left(\langle\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k},\overline{\vec{L}_{k}}\rangle\right)\right)=-\frac{1}{2}e^{2\lambda_{k}}\left\langle\vec{H}_{k},\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{L}_{k}\right)\right\rangle.

Since
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{L}_{k})\in\mathrm{L}^{q}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))
for all
q<\infty
,
e^{\lambda_{k}}\vec{H}_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))
and
e^{\lambda_{k}}\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}))
for all
p<\frac{2}{1-\varepsilon}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle e^{2\lambda_{k}}\left\langle\vec{H}_{k},\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{L}_{k}\right)\right\rangle\qquad\text{is bounded in}\;\,\mathrm{L}^{p}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))\;\,\text{for all}\;\,q<\frac{2}{2-\varepsilon}.

Therefore, since

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta\mathrm{Im}\,(S_{k})&=4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}\left(\langle\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k},\overline{\vec{L}_{k}}\rangle\right)\right)\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k})\\
\mathrm{Im}\,(S_{k})&=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}),\end{aligned}\right.

the classical Calderón-Zygmund estimates give that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\,(S_{k})\;\,\text{is bounded in}\;\,\mathrm{W}^{2,q}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))\;\,\text{for all}\;\,q<\frac{2}{2-\varepsilon}.

By Sobolev embedding, we deduce that

\displaystyle\nabla\mathrm{Im}\,(S_{k})\;\,\text{is bounded in}\;\ \mathrm{L}^{p}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))\qquad\text{for all}\;\,p<\frac{2}{1-\varepsilon}.

Therefore, for all
z\in\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0})
it holds

\displaystyle|\nabla\mathrm{Re}\,(S_{k})|

\displaystyle=2|\partial_{z}\mathrm{Re}\,(S_{k})|=2\left|-\partial_{z}\mathrm{Im}\,(S_{k})+\langle\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k},\overline{\vec{L}_{k}}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq|\nabla\mathrm{Im}\,(S_{k})|+2e^{\lambda_{k}}|\vec{L}_{k}|\leq\frac{C}{|z|}+e^{\lambda_{k}}|g_{k}(z)|,

(3.115)

where
g_{k}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))
.

Now, using Lemma
3.15
, we deduce that there exists
\vec{R}_{k}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}),\Lambda^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})
such that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \nabla_{z}\vec{R}_{k}&=\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\wedge\vec{L}_{k}-2i\,\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,\alpha R_{k})\\
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{R}_{k})&=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,\alpha R_{k}).\end{aligned}\right.

(3.116)

Furthermore, by
[
38
, (
6.8
)]
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\nabla_{\overline{z}}\left(\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\wedge\vec{L}_{k}-2i\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\right)\right)=-\frac{1}{2}e^{2\lambda_{k}}\vec{H}_{k}\wedge\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{L}_{k})\in\mathrm{L}^{q}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}))\qquad\text{for all}\;\,q<\frac{2}{2-\varepsilon}.

This give in turn

\displaystyle\Delta\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{R}_{k})

\displaystyle=4\,\partial_{\overline{z}}\partial_{z}\vec{R}_{k}=4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}\left(\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\wedge\vec{L}_{k}-2i\,\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\right)\right)-4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}\left(F_{k}(\vec{R}_{k})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\nabla_{\overline{z}}\left(\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\wedge\vec{L}_{k}-2i\,\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\right)\right)-4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\overline{F_{k}}\left(\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\wedge\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{L}_{k})-2i\,\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\right)\right)

\displaystyle-4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}\left(F_{k}(\vec{R}_{k})\right)\right).

Notice that

\displaystyle\left|\overline{F}_{k}\left(\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\wedge\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{L}_{k})-2i\,\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\right)\right|\leq Ce^{2\lambda_{k}}\left(|\vec{L}_{k}|+|\vec{H}_{k}|\right)\in\mathrm{L}^{q}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))\quad\text{for all}\;\,q<\dfrac{2}{2-\varepsilon}.

Recalling the Sobolev embedding

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,1))\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\bigcap_{q<\infty}\mathrm{L}^{q}(B(0,1)),

we have
\vec{R}_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{q}
for all
q<\infty
. Since
e^{\lambda_{k}}\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}))
for all
p<\frac{2}{1-\varepsilon}
, we deduce that
a fortiori
it holds

\displaystyle|F_{k}(\vec{R}_{k})|\leq Ce^{\lambda_{k}}|\vec{R}_{k}|\in\bigcap_{p<\frac{2}{1-\varepsilon}}\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}))\,.

Thus
\Delta\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{R}_{k})\in\mathrm{W}^{-1,p}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))
for all
p<\dfrac{2}{1-\varepsilon}
and, by Calderón-Zgymund estimates, we obtain that
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{R}_{k})\in\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))
for all
p<\dfrac{2}{1-\varepsilon}
.

Next, we sharpen the last estimate. To this aim, we first prove a pointwise bound for
\mathrm{Re}\,(\vec{R}_{k})
.
Writing

\displaystyle\vec{R}_{k}=\vec{R}_{k}^{1}\wedge\vec{R}_{k}^{2}\,,

we deduce that

\displaystyle\nabla_{z}\vec{R}_{k}

\displaystyle=\partial_{z}\vec{R}_{k}+f_{k}(\vec{R}_{k}^{1})\wedge\vec{R}_{k}^{2}+\vec{R}_{k}^{1}\wedge f_{k}(\vec{R}_{k}^{2})

\displaystyle=\partial_{z}\vec{R}_{k}+\sum_{i,j,l=1}^{n}c_{i,j,l}\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k,l}\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\,e_{i}\wedge e_{j}\,,

where
(\vec{e}_{1},\cdots,\vec{e}_{m})
is a basis of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and
c_{i,j,l}\in\mathbb{R}
are finite linear combinations of Christoffel symbols with integer weights. We compute:

\displaystyle\partial_{\overline{z}}F_{k}(\vec{R}_{k})=\sum_{i,j,l=1}^{m}\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}c_{i,j,l}\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k,l}\,\vec{R}_{k,i,j}+\frac{1}{2}c_{i,j,l}e^{\lambda_{k}}\vec{H}_{k}\cdot e^{\lambda_{k}}\vec{R}_{k,i,j}+c_{i,j,l}\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k,l}\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)\,e_{i}\wedge e_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,l=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{1}{4}\left(\partial_{x}c_{i,j,l}+i\,\partial_{y}c_{i,j,l}\right)\left(\partial_{x}\vec{\Phi}_{k,l}-i\,\partial_{y}\vec{\Phi}_{k,l}\right)\left(\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)+i\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}c_{i,j,l}e^{\lambda_{k}}\vec{H}_{k}\cdot\left(\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)+i\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{4}c_{i,j,l}\left(\partial_{x}\vec{\Phi}_{k,l}-i\,\partial_{y}\vec{\Phi}_{k,l}\right)\left(\partial_{x}+i\,\partial_{y}\right)\left(\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)+i\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)\right)\bigg)\,e_{i}\wedge e_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,l=1}^{m}\bigg(\frac{1}{4}\left(\langle\nabla c_{i,j,l},\nabla\vec{\Phi}_{k,l}\rangle+i\,\langle\nabla c_{i,j,l},\nabla^{\perp}\vec{\Phi}_{k,l}\rangle\right)\left(\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)+i\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}c_{i,j,l}e^{\lambda_{k}}\vec{H}_{k}\cdot\left(\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)+i\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{4}c_{i,j,l}\left(\partial_{x}\vec{\Phi}_{k,l}-i\,\partial_{y}\vec{\Phi}_{k,l}\right)\left(\partial_{x}\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)-\partial_{y}\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+i\left(\partial_{y}\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)+\partial_{x}\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)\right)\right)\bigg)\,e_{i}\wedge e_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,l=1}^{m}\bigg(\frac{1}{4}\left(\langle\nabla c_{i,j,l},\nabla\vec{\Phi}_{k,l}\rangle\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)-\langle\nabla c_{i,j,l},\nabla^{\perp}\vec{\Phi}_{k,l}\rangle\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{i}{4}\left(\langle\nabla c_{i,j,l},\nabla^{\perp}\vec{\Phi}_{k,l}\rangle\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)+\langle\nabla c_{i,j,l},\nabla\vec{\Phi}_{k,l}\rangle\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}c_{i,j,l}e^{\lambda_{k}}\vec{H}_{k}\cdot e^{\lambda_{k}}\left(\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)+i\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4}c_{i,j,l}\left(\langle\nabla\vec{\Phi}_{k},\nabla\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)\rangle+\langle\nabla\vec{\Phi}_{k},\nabla^{\perp}\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{i}{4}c_{i,j,l}\left(-\langle\nabla\vec{\Phi}_{k,l},\nabla^{\perp}\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)\rangle+\langle\nabla\vec{\Phi}_{k,l},\nabla\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)\rangle\right)\bigg)\,e_{i}\wedge e_{j}\,.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}F_{k}(\vec{R}_{k})\right)=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i,j,l=1}^{n}\bigg(\langle\nabla c_{i,j,l},\nabla^{\perp}\vec{\Phi}_{k,l}\rangle\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)+\langle\nabla c_{i,j,l},\nabla\vec{\Phi}_{k,l}\rangle\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\vec{R}_{k,i,j}\right)
