\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta\mathrm{Im}\,(S_{k})&=-2e^{2\lambda_{k}}\langle\vec{H}_{k},\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{L}_{k})\rangle\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0})\\
\Delta\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{R}_{k})&=4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\nabla_{\overline{z}}\left(\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\wedge\vec{L}_{k}-2i\,\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\right)\right)-4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}\left(F_{k}(\vec{R}_{k})\right)\right)\\
&\qquad-4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\overline{F_{k}}\left(\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\wedge\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{L}_{k})-2i\,\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}\wedge\vec{H}_{k}\right)\right)\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}).\end{aligned}\right.

(5.4)

Define the function
\vec{\Psi}_{k}:\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}|z_{k}|^{-1})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

\displaystyle\vec{\Psi}_{k}(w)=e^{-\lambda_{k}(z_{k})-\log|z_{k}|}\left(\vec{\Phi}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)-\vec{\Phi}_{k}(z_{k})\right).

A direct computation shows that

\displaystyle\partial_{w}\vec{\Psi}_{k}(w)=|z_{k}|e^{-\lambda_{k}(z_{k})-\log|z_{k}|}\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)=e^{-\lambda_{k}(z_{k})}\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}(|z_{k}|w).

(5.5)

Therefore, the conformal parameter
\mu_{k}
of
\vec{\Psi}_{k}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mu_{k}(w)=\lambda_{k}(|z_{k}|w)-\lambda_{k}(z_{k})\,.

By the uniform Harnack inequality on the conformal parameters (see Theorem
3.3
), there exists
C>0
independent of
k\geq N
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\left(\lambda_{k}-d_{k}\log|z|\right)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}))}+\left\|\lambda_{k}-d_{k}\log|z|\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}))}\leq C,

where
d_{k}\to d\in(-1,\infty)
, as
k\to\infty
.
In particular, it holds

\displaystyle|\mu_{k}(w)|\leq\left|\lambda_{k}(|z_{k}|w)-d_{k}\log|z_{k}w|\right|+\left|d_{k}\log|z_{k}|-\lambda_{k}(z_{k})\right|+|d_{k}\log|w||\leq(|d|+1)|\log|w||+2C,

which is uniformly bounded on any compact
subset
K\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
. Now, by (
5.5
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\vec{n}}_{k}(w)=\vec{n}_{\vec{\Psi}_{k}}(w)=\vec{n}_{k}(|z_{k}|w).

(5.6)

Then, we compute

\displaystyle\partial_{w\vec{w}}^{2}\vec{\Psi}(w)=|z_{k}|e^{-\lambda_{k}(z_{k})}\partial_{z\overline{z}}^{2}\vec{\Phi}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)=|z_{k}|e^{-\lambda_{k}(z_{k})}\times 2e^{2\lambda_{k}(|z_{k}|w)}\vec{H}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)=2|z_{k}|e^{2\lambda_{k}(|z_{k}|w)-\lambda_{k}(z_{k})}\vec{H}_{k}(|z_{k}|w),

which implies that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\vec{H}}_{k}(w)=\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\mu_{k}(w)}\partial_{w\vec{w}}^{2}\vec{\Psi}_{k}(w)=|z_{k}|e^{\lambda_{k}(z_{k})}\vec{H}_{k}(|z_{k}|w).

(5.7)

We deduce that

\displaystyle e^{\mu_{k}}\widetilde{\vec{H}}_{k}(w)=|z_{k}|e^{\lambda_{k}(z_{k})}\vec{H}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)\,,

which shows that, after the linear change of variable
z=|z_{k}|w
, it holds

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}|z_{k}|^{-1})}e^{2\mu_{k}(w)}|\widetilde{\vec{H}}_{k}(w)|^{2}|dw|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}|z_{k}|^{-1})}|z_{k}|^{2}e^{2\lambda_{k}(|z_{k}|w)}|\vec{H}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)|^{2}|dw|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0})}e^{2\lambda_{k}(z)}|\vec{H}_{k}(z)|^{2}|dz|^{2}=\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0})}|\vec{H}_{k}|^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}.

We deduce that

\displaystyle\partial_{w}\widetilde{\vec{H}}_{k}(w)=|z_{k}|^{2}e^{\lambda_{k}(z_{k})}\partial_{z}\vec{H}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)

(5.8)

and that

\displaystyle|z_{k}|^{2}e^{\lambda_{k}(z_{k})}\Gamma_{l,q}^{j}(\vec{\Phi}_{k}(|z_{k}|w))\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)^{q}\vec{H}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)^{l}=\Gamma_{l,q}^{j}(\vec{\Phi}_{k}(|z_{k}|w))|z_{k}|\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)^{q}\widetilde{\vec{H}}_{k}(w)^{l}.

(5.9)

Since
\left\{\mu_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{0\right\})
, we deduce by the
\varepsilon
-regularity that
\left\{\widetilde{\vec{H}}_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{0\right\})
. Furthermore, by the Harnack inequality (
3.21
), we deduce that

\displaystyle e^{-A}|z_{k}|^{d_{k}+1}|w|^{d_{k}}\leq|z_{k}||\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k,q}(|z_{k}|w)|\leq e^{A}|z_{k}|^{d_{k}+1}|w|^{d_{k}}\,.

Since
d_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}d>-1
, and
z_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
, while
\Gamma_{l,q}^{j}
are bounded, we conclude that for all compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
, it holds

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left\||z_{k}|^{2}e^{\lambda_{k}(z_{k})}\Gamma_{l,q}^{j}(\vec{\Phi}_{k}(|z_{k}|w))\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)^{q}\vec{H}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)^{l}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(K)}=0.

(5.10)

Now, the previous scaling considerations prompt us to introduce
\widetilde{\vec{L}}_{k}:\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}|z_{k}|^{-1})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
defined by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\vec{L}}_{k}(w)=|z_{k}|e^{\lambda_{k}(z_{k})}\vec{L}_{k}(|z_{k}|w).

It is immediate to check that

\displaystyle e^{\mu_{k}}\widetilde{\vec{L}}_{k}(w)=|z_{k}|e^{\lambda_{k}(|z_{k}|w)}\vec{L}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)=|z_{k}|e^{\lambda_{k}(|z_{k}|w)}\vec{V}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)+|z_{k}|e^{\lambda_{k}(|z_{k}|w)}\vec{W}_{k}(|z_{k}|w),

where
\vec{W}_{k}\in\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))
and
\vec{V}_{k}
satisfy for all
z\in\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0})
the estimate

\displaystyle e^{\lambda_{k}(z)}|\vec{V}_{k}(z)|\leq\frac{C}{|z|}

for some constant
C>0
independent of
k
. We deduce that

\displaystyle|z_{k}|e^{\lambda_{k}(|z_{k}|w)}\left|\vec{V}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)\right|\leq|z_{k}|\times\frac{C}{||z_{k}|w|}=\frac{C}{|w|}\,.

Also, by defining
\widetilde{\vec{W}}_{k}:B(0,\alpha_{0}|z_{k}|^{-1}R_{k})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\vec{W}}_{k}(w)=\vec{W}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)\,,

we have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\widetilde{\vec{W}}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,\alpha_{0}|z_{k}|^{-1}R_{k}))}=\left\|\nabla\vec{W}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))}\leq C\,,

giving that
\vec{W}_{k}
is bounded in
\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}(B(0,\alpha_{0}|z_{k}|^{-1}R_{k}))
. Furthermore, by the Harnack inequality (
3.21
), we deduce that

\displaystyle|z_{k}|e^{\lambda_{k}(|z_{k}|w)}\leq e^{A}|z_{k}|^{d_{k}+1}|w|^{d_{k}}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0\qquad\text{in}\;\,\mathrm{L}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{0\right\}).

Therefore, we get that

\displaystyle\left\||z_{k}|e^{\lambda_{k}(|z_{k}|\,\cdot\,)}\widetilde{W}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}|z_{k}|^{-1}))}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

\displaystyle|z_{k}|e^{\lambda_{k}(|z_{k}|\,\cdot\,)}\widetilde{W}_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0\qquad\text{in}\;\,\mathrm{L}^{p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{0\right\})\;\,\text{for all}\;\,p<\infty.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\nabla\mathrm{Im}\,(\widetilde{\vec{L}}_{k})(w)=|z_{k}|^{2}e^{\lambda_{k}(z_{k})}\nabla\vec{L}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)

which implies that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\mathrm{Im}\,(\widetilde{\vec{L}}_{k})\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}))}

\displaystyle=|z_{k}|e^{\lambda_{k}(z_{k})}\left\|\nabla\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{L}_{k})\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}))}

\displaystyle\leq e^{A}|z_{k}|^{d_{k}+1}\left\|\nabla\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{L}_{k})\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}))}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0,

where we used
|z_{k}|\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
and
d_{k}+1\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}d+1>0
. Finally, we can manipulate the equation (
5.3
) by using (
5.8
) and (
5.9
) to obtain:

\displaystyle\partial_{w}\widetilde{\vec{L}}_{k}-i\left(\partial_{w}\widetilde{\vec{H}}_{k}-3\,\pi_{\widetilde{\vec{n}}_{k}}(\partial_{w}\vec{H}_{k})-i\star_{h}\left(\partial_{z}\widetilde{\vec{n}}_{k}\wedge\widetilde{\vec{H}}_{k}\right)\right)=\vec{Z}_{k}

(5.11)

where

\displaystyle\left(\vec{Z}_{k}\right)_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l,q=1}^{n}|z_{k}|^{2}e^{\lambda_{k}(z_{k})}\Gamma_{j,q}^{l}(\vec{\Phi}_{k}(|z_{k}|w))\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k,q}(|z_{k}|w)\bigg(\vec{L}_{k,l}(|z_{k}|w)

\displaystyle-i\,\vec{H}_{k,l}(|z_{k}|w)+3i\,\pi_{\vec{n}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)}(\vec{H}_{k}(|z_{k}|w))_{l}-\star_{h}\left(\vec{n}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)\wedge\vec{H}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)\right)_{l}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l,q=1}^{n}\Gamma_{j,q}^{l}(\vec{\Phi}_{k}(|z_{k}|w))|z_{k}|\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k,q}(|z_{k}|w)\left(\widetilde{\vec{L}}_{k,l}(w)-i\,\widetilde{H}_{k,l}(w)+3i\,\pi_{\widetilde{n}_{k}(w)}(\widetilde{\vec{H}}_{k}(w))_{l}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\star\left(\widetilde{\vec{n}_{k}}(w)\wedge\widetilde{\vec{H}}_{k}(w)\right)_{l}\right).

By the
\varepsilon
-regularity Theorem
4.9
, as in (
5.10
), we get that for any compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
, it holds

\displaystyle\left\|\Gamma_{l,q}^{j}(\vec{\Phi}_{k}(|z_{k}|w))|z_{k}|\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)^{q}\bigg(-i\,\widetilde{H}_{k}(w)^{l}+3i\,\pi_{\widetilde{n}_{k}(w)}(\widetilde{\vec{H}}_{k}(w))^{l}-\star\left(\widetilde{\vec{n}_{k}}(w)\wedge\widetilde{\vec{H}}_{k}(w)\right)^{l}\bigg)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(K)}=0.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle|\vec{L}_{k}(w)|\leq\frac{C}{|w|^{d_{k}+1}}+f_{k}(w)\,,

where
f_{k}
is bounded in
\mathrm{W}^{1,(2,\infty)}
and in particular in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
for all
p<\infty
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle|z_{k}|\,|\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)|\,|\vec{L}_{k}(w)|\leq\frac{|z_{k}|^{d_{k}+1}}{|w|}+|z_{k}|^{d_{k}+1}|w|^{d_{k}}f_{k}(w)

which implies that for any compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
and any
p<\infty
, it holds

\displaystyle\left\|\Gamma_{l,q}^{j}(\vec{\Phi}_{k}(|z_{k}|w))|z_{k}|\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{k}(|z_{k}|w)^{q}\widetilde{\vec{L}}_{k}(w)^{l}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(K)}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

Now define
\widetilde{S}_{k}:\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}|z_{k}|^{-1})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
and
\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{k}:\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}|z_{k}|^{-1})\rightarrow\Lambda^{2}\mathbb{C}^{n}
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{k}(w)=S_{k}(|z_{k}|w),\qquad\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{k}(w)=\vec{R}_{k}(|z_{k}|w).

By scaling invariance, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}|z_{k}|^{-1})}|\nabla\vec{n}_{\vec{\Psi}_{k}}|^{2}dx=\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0})}|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}dx\leq C

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\widetilde{S}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}|z_{k}|^{-1}))}=\left\|\nabla S_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}))}\leq C

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}|z_{k}|^{-1}))}=\left\|\nabla\vec{R}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}))}\leq C.

Therefore, by the
\varepsilon
-regularity of Theorem
4.9
to deduce that for any compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
, and for any
l\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
C_{l}(K)<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{l}\vec{n}_{\vec{\Psi}_{k}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(K)}\leq C_{l}(K).

Therefore, up to a subsequence,
\vec{\Psi}_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\vec{\Psi}_{\infty}
in
C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{0\right\})
and by lower semi-continuity and conformal invariance of the Dirichlet energy (or
\mathrm{L}^{2,1}
norm of the gradient), we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|e^{\mu_{\infty}}\widetilde{\vec{L}}_{\infty}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}+\left\|\nabla\widetilde{S}_{\infty}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\mathbb{C})}+\left\|\nabla\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\mathbb{C})}\leq C.

Furthermore, recalling that
\mathrm{Im}\,(S_{k}),\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{R}_{k})\in\mathrm{W}^{2,q}(B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k}))
for all
q<\frac{2}{2-\varepsilon}
, and using that
\mathrm{Im}\,(\widetilde{S}_{k})(w)=\mathrm{Im}\,(\widetilde{S}_{k})(|z|_{k}w)
, we deduce that for all
q<\frac{2}{1-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,\alpha_{0}|z_{k}|^{-1}R_{k})}\left(|\nabla^{2}\mathrm{Im}\,(\widetilde{S}_{k})|^{q}+|\nabla^{2}\mathrm{Im}\,(\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{k})|^{q}\right)dx

\displaystyle=|z_{k}|^{2(q-1)}\int_{B(0,\alpha_{0}R_{k})}\left(|\nabla^{2}\mathrm{Im}\,({S}_{k})|^{q}+|\nabla^{2}\mathrm{Im}\,({R}_{k})|^{q}\right)dx\leq 2(C_{q})^{q}|z_{k}|^{2(q-1)}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0,

It follows that
\nabla^{2}\mathrm{Im}\,(\widetilde{S}_{\infty})=0
and
\nabla^{2}\mathrm{Im}\,(\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty})=0
and, since
\nabla\mathrm{Im}\,(\widetilde{S}_{\infty})\in\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\mathbb{C})
and
\nabla\mathrm{Im}\,(\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty})\in\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\mathbb{C})
, this implies that
\mathrm{Im}\,(\widetilde{S}_{\infty})
and
\mathrm{Im}\,(\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty})
are constant.
Recalling that (
5.3
) can be rewritten (see
[
38
, Lemma
6.2
]
) as

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \nabla_{z}\vec{R}_{k}&=(-1)^{m+1}\star_{h}\left(\vec{n}_{k}\mathbin{\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt}i\,\nabla_{z}\vec{R}\right)+i\,\partial_{z}S_{k}\,\star_{h}\vec{n}_{k}\\
\partial_{z}\vec{S}_{k}&=-i\langle\nabla_{z}\vec{R}_{k},\star_{h}\vec{n}_{k}\rangle,\end{aligned}\right.

an expansion similar to the one made in (
5.11
) shows that the system passes to the limit and yields

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{z}\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty}&=(-1)^{m+1}\star_{h}\left(\widetilde{\vec{n}}_{\infty}\mathbin{\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt}i\,\partial_{z}\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty}\right)+i\,\partial_{z}\widetilde{S}_{\infty}\,\star_{h}\vec{n}_{k}\\
\partial_{z}\widetilde{S}_{\infty}&=-i\langle\partial_{z}\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty},\star_{h}\widetilde{\vec{n}}_{\infty}\rangle\,.\end{aligned}\right.

Since both
\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty}
and
\widetilde{S}_{\infty}
are real, this system can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \nabla\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty}&=(-1)^{m}\star_{h}\left(\widetilde{\vec{n}}_{\infty}\mathbin{\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt}\nabla^{\perp}\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty}\right)-\nabla^{\perp}\widetilde{S}_{\infty}\,\star_{h}\widetilde{\vec{n}}_{\infty}\\
\nabla\widetilde{S}_{\infty}&=\langle\nabla^{\perp}\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty},\star_{h}\widetilde{\vec{n}}_{\infty}\rangle.\end{aligned}\right.

We deduce that the following Jacobian system holds:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty}&=(-1)^{m}\star_{h}\left(\nabla\widetilde{\vec{n}}_{\infty}\mathbin{\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt}\nabla^{\perp}\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty}\right)-\nabla^{\perp}\widetilde{S}_{\infty}\cdot\nabla\left(\star\widetilde{\vec{n}}_{\infty}\right)\\
\Delta\widetilde{S}_{\infty}&=\langle\nabla^{\perp}\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty},\nabla\left(\star_{h}\widetilde{\vec{n}}_{\infty}\right)\rangle.\end{aligned}\right.

Using an improved Wente estimate as in
[
4
]
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\mathbb{C})}+\left\|\nabla\widetilde{S}_{\infty}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\mathbb{C})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\mathbb{C})}+\left\|\nabla\widetilde{S}_{\infty}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\mathbb{C})}\right)\left\|\nabla\widetilde{n}_{\infty}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{C})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon_{0}\left(\left\|\nabla\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\mathbb{C})}+\left\|\nabla\widetilde{S}_{\infty}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\mathbb{C})}\right)\,.

Therefore, taking
\varepsilon_{0}=\frac{1}{2C}
, we get that
\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty}
and
\widetilde{S}_{\infty}
are constant.
Since
\mathrm{Im}\,(\vec{L}_{\infty})=0
, we have the identity

\displaystyle e^{2\mu_{\infty}}\widetilde{\vec{H}}_{\infty}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\partial_{z}\widetilde{\vec{R}}_{\infty}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{\Psi}_{\infty}\right)-\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\partial_{z}\widetilde{S}_{\infty}(i\,\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{\Psi}_{\infty})\right)+\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\langle\partial_{z}\vec{\Psi}_{\infty},\mathrm{Im}\,(\widetilde{L}_{\infty})\rangle\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{\Psi}_{\infty}\right)=0.

We deduce that
\widetilde{\vec{H}}_{\infty}=0
which, by the exact same proof as in
[
4
]
, yields that
\nabla\widetilde{\vec{n}}_{\infty}=0
, contradicting the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2}\setminus\overline{B}_{1}(0)}|\nabla\widetilde{n}_{\infty}|^{2}dx\geq C_{0}^{-1}\varepsilon_{1}^{2}

that passed to the limit thanks to the strong convergence on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
.
∎

6
Proof of the main Theorem
A

We are finally in position to prove the main result of the paper (Theorem
A
), namely the quantization of the Willmore energy for Willmore spheres in Riemannian manifolds. The proof will combine all the main technical results proved in the paper: the
\mathrm{L}^{2,1}
uniform bounds on the Willmore integrand in neck regions (Theorem
3.2
), the
\varepsilon
-regularity Theorem
4.9
, and the
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}
quantization of energy (Theorem
5.1
).

Proof of Theorem
A
.

Let us first consider the case when
\displaystyle\inf_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\textrm{Area}(\vec{\Phi}_{k}(S^{2}))>0
.

Thanks to the pre-compactness Theorem
2.1
, we know that there exist a sequence of Lipschitz diffeomorphisms
\{f_{k}\}
of
S^{2}
and a weak immersion
\vec{\xi}_{\infty}
of
(S^{2}\setminus\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}\})
, possibly branched at the finitely many points
a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}
, into
(M^{m},h)
such that

\vec{\xi}_{k}=\vec{\Phi}_{k}\circ f_{k}\rightharpoonup\vec{\xi}_{\infty}\quad\text{weakly in $\mathrm{W}^{2,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{2}\setminus\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}\})$ ,}

(6.1)

Thanks to the
\varepsilon
-regularity Theorem
4.9
, we can improve the weak
\mathrm{W}^{2,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence to local smooth convergence:

\displaystyle\vec{\xi}_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\vec{\xi}_{\infty}\qquad\text{in}\;\,C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{2}\setminus\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}\right\})\,\;\text{for all}\;\,l\in\mathbb{N}.

(6.2)

Following verbatim the arguments at
[
4
, pp.129–130]
, one can extend the map
\vec{\xi}_{\infty}
to the whole
S^{2}
, so that the extension
\ \vec{\xi}_{\infty}:S^{2}\rightarrow(M^{m},h)
realises a Willmore immersion of
S^{2}
into
(M^{m},h)
, possibly branched at the points
a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}
.

Now, in a neck-region conformally equivalent to
\Omega_{k}(\alpha)=B_{\alpha R_{k}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\alpha^{-1}r_{k}}(0)
, we can apply Theorem
3.2
and Theorem
5.1
to deduce that there exists
\alpha_{0}>0
and
C>0
independent of
k
such that

\displaystyle\left\|e^{\lambda_{k}}\vec{H}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha_{0}))}\leq C\quad\text{and}\quad\lim\limits_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left\|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}=0\,.

By the
\mathrm{L}^{2,1}/\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}
duality, we deduce that for all
0<\alpha<\alpha_{0}

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}e^{2\lambda_{k}}|\vec{H}_{k}|^{2}dx\leq\left\|e^{\lambda_{k}}\vec{H}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\left\|e^{\lambda_{k}}\vec{H}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\leq C\left\|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))},

which implies that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}|\vec{H}_{k}|^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g_{k}}=0\,.

(6.3)

By
[
4
, Lemma V.
1
]
(which does not use the Willmore equation and is valid for any weak immersion), we also have

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left|\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}K_{g_{k}}d\mathrm{vol}_{g_{k}}\right|=0.

(6.4)

Moreover, by the proof of Theorem
3.2

(see (
3.20
)), it holds

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Area}(\vec{\xi}_{k}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha)))=0\,.

(6.5)

Using the point-wise identity

\displaystyle|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}=4|\vec{H}_{k}|^{2}-2K_{g_{k}}+2K(\vec{\xi}_{k,\ast}(TS^{2}))\,,

together with (
6.3
), (
6.4
) and (
6.5
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}dx=0\,.

(6.6)

This is the
no-neck energy
which will give below the desired quantization result.

Using that
(M^{m},h)
is isometrically embedded into
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and the conformal invariance of the Willmore energy to obtain a suitable convergence result for the energy of bubbles. Indeed, seeing
\vec{\Phi}_{k}:\Sigma\rightarrow(M^{m},h)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
as an immersion of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, since by assumption
h=\iota^{\ast}g_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, where
\iota:M^{m}\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the inclusion map, we deduce that for any open subset
\Omega\subset\Sigma
it holds

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(|\vec{H}_{k}|^{2}+K(\vec{\Phi}_{k,\ast}(T\Sigma))\right)d\mathrm{vol}_{g_{k}}=W_{(M^{m},h)}(\vec{\Phi}_{k}|\Omega)=W_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\iota\circ\vec{\Phi}_{k}|\Omega)=\int_{\Omega}|\vec{H}_{\iota\circ\vec{\Phi}_{k}}|^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g_{k}},

Let

B(i,j,\alpha,k)=B_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k}^{i,j}}(x_{k}^{i,j})\setminus\bigcup_{j^{\prime}\in I^{i,j}}B_{\alpha\rho_{k}^{i,j}}(x_{k}^{i,j^{\prime}})

be a bubble domain (for more details, see
[
4
]
or the discussion after (
3.16
)). Recall that
\rho^{i,j}_{k}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
and that the indices
i,j
vary within a finite set. The Harnack inequality (
3.17
) (proved in
[
4
, Display (VIII.
10
)]
) gives that for all
0<\alpha<1
, there exists
A_{\alpha}=A(i,j,\alpha)\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in B(i,j,\alpha,k)}e^{\lambda_{k}(z)}\leq e^{A_{\alpha}}\inf_{z\in B(i,j,\alpha,k)}e^{\lambda_{k}(z)}.

(6.7)

Choose an arbitrary point
z_{k}^{i,j}\in B(i,j,\alpha,k)
and set
\lambda(i,j,\alpha,k)=\lambda_{k}(z_{k}^{i,j})
.
The uniform area bound implies that

\limsup_{k\to\infty}e^{2\lambda(i,j,\alpha,k)}(\rho_{k}^{i,j})^{2}<\infty\,,\quad\text{for all $\alpha\in(0,1)$}.

(6.8)

Thus we have two cases.

Case 1
.
\limsup_{k\to\infty}e^{2\lambda(i,j,\alpha,k)}(\rho_{k}^{i,j})^{2}>0\,,
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

This case corresponds to a macroscopic bubbles forming in the region
B(i,j,\alpha,k)
. By performing a good gauge extraction procedure along the lines of
[
38
, Lemma 4.1]
, we can find positive Möbius transformations
\widetilde{f}_{k}
of
S^{2}
such that the reparametrised immersions (up to a subsequence)

\widetilde{\vec{\xi}}_{k}=\xi_{k}\circ\widetilde{f}_{k}:S^{2}\rightarrow(M^{m},h)

converge weakly in
\mathrm{W}^{2,2}
(and then smoothly, by the
\varepsilon
-regularity Theorem) outside finitely many points
\{a^{i,j}_{1},\ldots,a^{i,j}_{N_{i,j}}\}
to a Willmore immersion
\vec{\Psi}_{i,j}:S^{2}\setminus\big\{a_{1}^{i,j},\cdots,a_{N_{i,j}}^{i,j}\big\}\rightarrow(M^{m},h)
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\vec{\xi}}_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\vec{\Psi}_{i,j}\qquad\text{in}\;\,C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{2}\setminus\big\{a^{i,j}_{1},\cdots,a^{i,j}_{N_{i,j}}\big\})\,\;\text{for all}\;\,l\in\mathbb{N}\,.

(6.9)

Following verbatim the arguments at
[
4
, pp. 129–130]
, one can extend the map to the whole
S^{2}
, so that the extension
\vec{\Psi}_{i,j}:S^{2}\rightarrow(M^{m},h)
realises a Willmore immersion of
S^{2}
into
(M^{m},h)
, possibly branched at the finitely many points
a^{i,j}_{1},\cdots,a^{i,j}_{N_{i,j}}
.
Moreover, the no neck energy identity (
6.6
) ensures that

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}W_{(M,h)}\left(\vec{\xi}_{k}|B(i,j,k,\alpha)\right)=W_{(M,h)}(\vec{\Psi}_{i,j}).

(6.10)

Such a branched Willmore immersion corresponds to a Riemannian bubble
\vec{\Psi}_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,u\in\mathbb{N}
in the statement of Theorem
A
.

Case 2
.
\lim_{k\to\infty}e^{2\lambda(i,j,\alpha,k)}(\rho_{k}^{i,j})^{2}=0\,,
for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

In this case, there exists a point
\overline{x}_{i,j}\in M
such that (again, up to a subsequence in
k
)

\vec{\xi}_{k}(B(i,j,\alpha,k))\to\overline{x}_{i,j}\,\quad\text{in Hausdorff distance sense, as $k\to\infty$, for all $\alpha\in(0,1)$.}

Let
{\rm Exp}_{\overline{x}_{i,j}}:B_{\varepsilon}^{\mathbb{R}^{m}}(0)\to M
denote the exponential map of
(M,h)
based at the point
\overline{x}_{i,j}
.
Consider the rescaled immersions (with values in
T_{p}M\simeq\mathbb{R}^{m}
)

\displaystyle\vec{\xi}_{k}^{i,j}(w)=e^{-\lambda(i,j,\alpha,k)-\log\,\rho_{k}^{i,j}}\,{\rm Exp}_{\overline{x}_{i,j}}^{-1}\left(\vec{\xi}_{k}(\rho_{k}^{i,j}w+z_{k}^{i,j})-\vec{\xi}_{k}(z_{k}^{i,j})\right),\;\forall w\in(\rho_{k}^{i,j})^{-1}\big(B(i,j,\alpha,k)-z^{k}_{i,j}\big)\subset\mathbb{C}\,.

It is easily seen that (
6.7
) implies

\displaystyle e^{-A_{\alpha}-1}\leq|\partial_{w}\vec{\xi}_{k}^{i,j}|\leq e^{A_{\alpha}+1}\,,\quad\text{for $k\in\mathbb{N}$ sufficiently large},

and that the assumption of case 2 yields
e^{-\lambda(i,j,\alpha,k)-\log\,\rho_{k}^{i,j}}\to+\infty
.

Notice that
\vec{\xi}_{k}^{i,j}
are Willmore immersions in
(\mathbb{R}^{m},g(k,i,j))
, where the Riemannian metrics
g(k,i,j)
converge to the Euclidean metric as
k\to\infty
, in
C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
topology, for every
l\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover, the scaling invariance of the Willmore functional implies that

W_{(M,h)}\left(\vec{\xi}_{k}|B(i,j,k,\alpha)\right)=W_{(\mathbb{R}^{m},g(k,i,j))}\left(\vec{\xi}_{k}^{i,j}|(\rho_{k}^{i,j})^{-1}\big(B(i,j,\alpha,k)-z^{k}_{i,j}\big)\right).

(6.11)

Using the aforementioned
C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
convergence of the ambient Riemannian metrics, one can immediately adapt the proof of the
\varepsilon
-regularity Theorem
4.9
to deduce that there exists a finite set of points
\big\{a_{1}^{i,j},\cdots,a_{N_{i,j}}^{i,j}\big\}\subset\mathbb{C}
with
N_{i,j}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\vec{\xi}_{k}^{i,j}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\vec{\xi}_{\infty}^{i,j}\quad\text{in}\;\,C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathbb{C}\setminus\big\{a_{1}^{i,j},\cdots,a_{N_{i,j}}^{i,j}\big\}\right),\;\text{for all}\;\,l\in\mathbb{N},

where
\vec{\xi}_{\infty}^{i,j}
is a smooth Willmore immersion of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\big\{a_{1}^{i,j},\cdots,a_{N_{i,j}}^{i,j}\big\}
in the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

We can now follow verbatim the arguments in
[
4
, pp. 130–132]
and deduce that:

(1)

In case
\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\nabla\vec{\xi}_{\infty}^{i,j}|^{2}dx<\infty
, using the stereographic projection of
S^{2}
to
\mathbb{C}
, the limit map
\vec{\xi}_{\infty}^{i,j}
extends to a smooth Willmore immersion of
S^{2}
into
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, possibly branched at
\big\{a_{1}^{i,j},\cdots,a_{N_{i,j}}^{i,j}\big\}
. Moreover (see also (
6.11
)):

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}W_{(M,h)}\left(\vec{\xi}_{k}|B(i,j,k,\alpha)\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{(\rho_{k}^{i,j})^{-1}\big(B(i,j,\alpha,k)-z^{k}_{i,j}\big)}|\vec{H}_{\vec{\xi}_{k}^{i,j}}|^{2}e^{2\lambda_{\vec{\xi}_{k}^{i,j}}}dx

\displaystyle=W_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}(\vec{\xi}_{\infty}^{i,j}).

(6.12)

Such a branched Willmore immersion corresponds to a Euclidean bubble
\vec{\eta}_{s}
,
s=1,\ldots,p\in\mathbb{N}
in the statement of Theorem
A
.

(2)

In case
\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\nabla\vec{\xi}_{\infty}^{i,j}|^{2}dx=\infty
, one finds suitable inversions
{\mathcal{I}}_{k,i,j},{\mathcal{I}}_{\infty,i,j}
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that

{\mathcal{I}}_{k,i,j}\circ\vec{\xi}_{k}^{i,j}\to{\mathcal{I}}_{\infty,i,j}\circ\vec{\xi}_{\infty}^{i,j},\qquad\text{in}\;\,C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathbb{C}\setminus\big\{a_{1}^{i,j},\cdots,a_{N_{i,j}}^{i,j}\big\}\right),\;\,\text{for all}\;\,l\in\mathbb{N}.

Furthermore, one obtains that (pre-composing with the stereographic projection)
{\mathcal{I}}_{\infty,i,j}\circ\vec{\xi}_{\infty}^{i,j}
extends to a smooth Willmore immersion of
S^{2}
into
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, possibly branched at
\big\{a_{1}^{i,j},\cdots,a_{N_{i,j}}^{i,j}\big\}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}W_{(M,h)}\left(\vec{\xi}_{k}|B(i,j,k,\alpha)\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{(\rho_{k}^{i,j})^{-1}\big(B(i,j,\alpha,k)-z^{k}_{i,j}\big)}|\vec{H}_{\vec{\xi}_{k}^{i,j}}|^{2}e^{2\lambda_{\vec{\xi}_{k}^{i,j}}}dx

\displaystyle=W_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\infty,i,j}\circ\vec{\xi}_{\infty}^{i,j})-4\pi\theta_{i,j}\,,

(6.13)

where
\theta_{i,j}
is the integer density of
{\mathcal{I}}_{\infty,i,j}\circ\vec{\xi}_{\infty}^{i,j}
at the image point
0\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Such an inverted (compact) branched Willmore immersion of
S^{2}
corresponds to a Euclidean bubble
\vec{\zeta}_{t}
,
t=1,\ldots,q\in\mathbb{N}
in the statement of Theorem
A
.

The combination of (
6.9
), (
6.10
), (
(1)
) and (
(2)
) gives the desired energy identity (up to a subsequence in
k
):

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}W_{(M^{m},h)}(\vec{\Phi}_{k})

\displaystyle=W_{(M^{m},h)}({\vec{\xi}_{\infty}})+\sum_{j=1}^{u}W_{(M^{m},h)}(\vec{\Psi}_{j})+\sum_{s=1}^{p}W_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}(\vec{\eta}_{s})+\sum_{t=1}^{q}\left(W_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}(\vec{\zeta}_{t})-4\pi\theta_{0,t}\right)\,.

∎

Arguing along the lines of the proof of Theorem
A
, one can prove the following quantization result for surfaces of arbitrary genus, under the assumption of weak convergence to a limit surface and a bound on the conformal structures.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface,
(M^{m},h)
be a smooth compact Riemannian manifold of dimension
m\geq 3
, and let
\{\vec{\Phi}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathrm{Imm}(\Sigma,M^{m})
be a sequence of conformally parametrised Willmore immersions.
Assume that the conformal classes of
\left\{\vec{\Phi}_{k}^{\ast}h\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
remain within a compact region of the moduli space of
\Sigma
and that there exists a weak, possibly branched, immersion
\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}:\Sigma\to(M^{m},h)
such that

\vec{\Phi}_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}\quad\text{weakly in }\mathrm{W}^{2,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Sigma\setminus\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}\right\})\text{ and weakly${}^{*}$ in }\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Sigma\setminus\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}\right\})

(6.14)

where
\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}\right\}\subset\Sigma
is a finite set.
Then the following identity holds:

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}W_{(M^{m},h)}(\vec{\Phi}_{k})

\displaystyle=W_{(M^{m},h)}(\vec{\Phi}_{\infty})+\sum_{j=1}^{u}W_{(M^{m},h)}(\vec{\Psi}_{j})+\sum_{s=1}^{p}W_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}(\vec{\eta}_{s})+\sum_{t=1}^{q}\left(W_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}(\vec{\zeta}_{t})-4\pi\theta_{0,t}\right)\,,

where:

(1)

The map
\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}
is a smooth, possibly branched, Willmore immersion of
\Sigma
into
(M^{m},h)
and

\vec{\Phi}_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}\quad\text{ in }C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Sigma\setminus\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}\right\}),\quad\forall l\in\mathbb{N}.

Furthermore, it holds

\lim_{k\to\infty}W_{(M^{m},h)}(\vec{\Phi}_{k})=W_{(M^{m},h)}(\vec{\Phi}_{\infty})\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\vec{\Phi}_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}\text{ in }C^{l}(\Sigma),\quad\forall l\in\mathbb{N}.

(2)

The maps
\vec{\Psi}_{j}:S^{2}\rightarrow(M^{m},h)
are smooth, possibly branched, Willmore immersions.

(3)

The maps
\vec{\eta}_{s}:S^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\vec{\zeta}_{t}:S^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
are smooth, possibly branched, Willmore immersions and
\theta_{0,t}=\theta_{0}(\vec{\zeta}_{t},x_{t})\in\mathbb{N}
is the multiplicity of
\vec{\zeta}_{t}
at some some point
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

(4)

The Riemannian Willmore bubbles
\vec{\Psi}_{j}:S^{2}\to M^{m}
are obtained as follows: there exist a sequence of unit area and constant curvature metrics
h_{k}
on
\Sigma
conformally equivalent to
\vec{\xi}_{k}^{*}h
and strongly converging in
C^{l}(\Sigma)
such that for any
j\in\{1,\ldots,u\}
, there exists a sequence of points
x^{u}_{k}\in\Sigma
converging to one of
\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}\right\}
, a sequence of radii
\rho^{j}_{k}
converging to zero such that (in converging
h_{k}
conformal coordinates
\varphi_{k}
around the given point in
\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}\right\}
):

\vec{\xi}_{k}\circ\varphi_{k}(\rho^{j}_{k}y+\varphi_{k}^{-1}(x^{j}_{k}))\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\vec{\Psi}_{j}\circ\pi^{-1}(y)\quad\text{ in }C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\big\{a_{1}^{j},\cdots,a^{j}_{N_{j}}\big\}),\quad\forall l\in\mathbb{N},

where
\pi
denotes the stereographic projection from
S^{2}
into
\mathbb{C}
, and
\big\{a_{1}^{j},\cdots,a^{j}_{N_{j}}\big\}
is a finite set of points in the complex plane.

(5)

The Euclidean Willmore bubbles
\vec{\eta}_{s},\vec{\zeta}_{t}:S^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
are obtained by the following blow up procedure: there exist a sequence of unit area and constant curvature metrics
h_{k}
on
\Sigma
conformally equivalent to
\vec{\xi}_{k}^{*}h
and strongly converging in
C^{l}(\Sigma)
such that for any
s\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
(resp. for any
t\in\{1,\ldots,q\}
), there exists a point
\overline{x}^{s}\in M
(resp.
\overline{x}^{t}\in M
), there exists a sequence of points
x^{s}_{k}\in\Sigma
(resp.
x^{t}_{k}\in\Sigma
) converging to one of
\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}\right\}
, a sequence of radii
\rho^{s}_{k}
(resp.
\rho^{t}_{k}
) converging to zero, a sequence of rescalings
\lambda^{s}_{k}\to\infty
(resp.
\lambda^{t}_{k}\to\infty
) and inversions
\Xi^{t}_{k}
of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that (in converging
h_{k}
conformal coordinates
\varphi_{k}
around the given point in
\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}\right\}
):

\lambda^{s}_{k}\cdot{\rm Exp}_{\overline{x}^{s}}^{-1}\circ\vec{\xi}_{k}\circ\varphi_{k}(\rho^{s}_{k}y+\varphi_{k}^{-1}(x^{s}_{k}))\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\vec{\eta}_{s}\circ\pi^{-1}(y)\quad\text{ in }C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\big\{a_{1}^{s},\cdots,a^{s}_{N_{s}}\big\}),\quad\forall l\in\mathbb{N},

and, respectively,

\Xi^{t}_{k}\circ\lambda^{t}_{k}\cdot{\rm Exp}_{\overline{x}^{t}}^{-1}\circ\vec{\xi}_{k}\circ\varphi_{k}(\rho^{t}_{k}y+\varphi_{k}^{-1}(x^{t}_{k}))\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\vec{\zeta}_{t}\circ\pi^{-1}(y)\quad\text{ in }C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{a_{1}^{t},\cdots,a^{t}_{N_{t}}\right\}),\quad\forall l\in\mathbb{N},

where
\pi
denotes the stereographic projection from
S^{2}
into
\mathbb{C}
, and
\big\{a_{1}^{s},\cdots,a^{s}_{N_{s}}\big\},\big\{a_{1}^{t},\cdots,a^{t}_{N_{t}}\big\}
are finite sets of points in the complex plane.

7
Appendix: Generalised Lorentz Spaces

7.1
General discussion and first example

Let
(X,\mu)
be a measured space. For any
\mu
-measurable function
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{X}|f|^{p}d\mu=p\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{p-1}\lambda_{f}(t)dt\,,

where, for all
t>0
,
we denote

\displaystyle\lambda_{f}(t)=\mu\left(X\cap\left\{x:|f(x)|>t\right\}\right)\,.

Define the decreasing rearrangement
f_{\ast}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
of
f
by

\displaystyle f_{\ast}(t)=\inf\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\cap\left\{s:\lambda_{f}(s)\leq t\right\}\right)\,.

It is clear from the definitions that for all
t>0

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\cap\left\{s:f_{\ast}(s)>t\right\}\right)=\lambda_{f}(t)\,,\quad\text{ for all $t>0$ .}

Applying twice the slicing formula, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{X}|f|^{p}d\mu=p\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{p-1}\lambda_{f}(t)dt=p\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{p-1}\mathscr{L}^{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\cap\left\{s:f_{\ast}(s)>t\right\}\right)dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{\ast}^{p}(s)ds.

More generally, for all real-valued differentiable functions
\varphi,\psi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we have the integration by parts formula

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi(\lambda_{f}(t))\psi^{\prime}(t)dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi^{\prime}(t)\psi\left(f_{\ast}(t)\right)dt.

(7.1)

Since this formula is not completely standard, we give a proof of it. Let
f
be a non-negative step function, then there exists
0<a_{1}<a_{2}<\cdots<a_{n}<\infty
and pair-wise disjoint measurable sets
A_{1},\cdots,A_{n}
such that

\displaystyle f=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\mathbf{1}_{A_{i}}.

Following
[
55
]
, defining
\displaystyle B_{i}=\bigcup_{j=i}^{n}A_{j}
, we have

\displaystyle f=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(a_{i}-a_{i-1})\mathbf{1}_{B_{i}},

where
a_{0}=0
. Then it holds

\displaystyle\lambda_{f}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu(B_{i})\mathbf{1}_{[a_{i-1},a_{i}]}\quad\text{and}\quad f_{\ast}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\mathbf{1}_{[\mu(B_{i+1}),\mu(B_{i})]}.

Thanks to a discrete integration by parts with
B_{n+1}=\emptyset
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi(\lambda_{f}(t))\psi^{\prime}(t)dt=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{a_{i-1}}^{a_{i}}\varphi(\mu(B_{i}))\psi^{\prime}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi(\mu(B_{i}))\left(\psi(a_{i})-\psi(a_{i-1})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi(a_{i})\left(\varphi(\mu(B_{i}))-\varphi(\mu(B_{i}+1))\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi(a_{i})\int_{\mu(B_{i+1})}^{\mu(B_{i})}\varphi^{\prime}(t)dt=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mu(B_{i+1})}^{\mu(B_{i})}\varphi^{\prime}(t)\psi(f_{\ast}(t))dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi^{\prime}(t)\psi(f_{\ast}(t))dt.

The rest of the proof is the same as
[
55
]
if
\varphi
is unbounded or if
(X,\mu)
is
\sigma
-finite, and given in
[
54
]
in the general case. Notice that the proof would hold unchanged only assuming that
\varphi
and
\psi
are absolutely continuous.

We will now define a class of Lorentz spaces (which can also be seen as generalisation of Orlicz spaces
[
43
]
) of interest in this paper (see
[
55
]
and
[
54
]
). Let
\mathscr{C}
be the set of non-negative concave functions
\varphi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
\varphi
is continuous at
0
,

\displaystyle\varphi(0)=\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow 0}\varphi(t)=0

and
\varphi(t)>0
for all
t>0
. For all measurable
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
(of
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{\pm\infty\right\}
), define the norm

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{N(\varphi)}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi(\lambda_{f}(t))dt.

Now fix some integer
n\geq 1
and let
\mathscr{M}(X)
be the class of measurable
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued function on
X
. Define

\displaystyle N(\varphi)=\mathscr{M}(X)\cap\left\{f:\left\|f\right\|_{N(\varphi)}<\infty\right\}.

Then we have the following result:

Theorem 7.1

(Steigerwalt-White
[
55
]
, Steigerwalt
[
54
]
)
.

The functional
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{N(\varphi)}
is a norm and
N(\varphi)
and
(N(\varphi),\|\,\cdot\,\|_{N(\varphi)})
is a Banach space.

By the integration by parts formula (
7.1
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{N(\varphi)}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi^{\prime}(t)f_{\ast}(t)\,dt\,.

Now let
1<p<\infty
and
1\leq q<\infty
. Define for all
t>0

\displaystyle f_{\ast\ast}(t)={\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.57222pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.91281pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.30664pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.13547pt}}\!\int}_{\mkern-10.0mu0}^{t}f_{\ast}(s)ds=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}f_{\ast}(s)ds.

Then, the Lorentz space
\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)\cap\left\{f:\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}<\infty\right\},\quad\text{where}\quad\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}=\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{q}{p}-1}f_{\ast\ast}^{q}(t)dt\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}.

It is a Banach space and the following semi-norm
|\,\cdot\,|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}

\displaystyle|f|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}=\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{q}{p}-1}f_{\ast}^{q}(t)dt\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

is equivalent to
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}
. In the case
q=1
, we have by Fubini’s theorem

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,1}(X)}=\frac{p}{p-1}|f|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,1}(X)}.

Using the integration by parts formula (
7.1
), with
\varphi(t)=\frac{p}{q}t^{\frac{p}{q}}
and
\psi(t)=t^{q}
we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{p}{q}-1}f_{\ast}^{q}(t)dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}qt^{q-1}\frac{p}{q}\lambda_{f}(t)^{\frac{q}{p}}dt=p\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{q-1}\lambda_{f}(t)^{\frac{q}{p}}dt.

This gives the well known fact that
\mathrm{L}^{p,p}(X)=\mathrm{L}^{p}(X)
with equivalent norms. Taking instead
\varphi(t)=t^{\frac{1}{p}}
, we get that

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,1}(X)}=\frac{p}{p-1}|f|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,1}(X)}=\frac{p}{p-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{1}{p}-1}f_{\ast}(t)dt=\frac{p^{2}}{p-1}\left\|f\right\|_{N(\varphi)}.

Therefore, the spaces
N(\varphi)
are generalisations of
\mathrm{L}^{p,1}
spaces, but
\mathrm{L}^{p,q}
spaces with

1<q<\infty
are not
N(\varphi)
-spaces.

Now, we will define generalisations of the weak
\mathrm{L}^{p}
spaces or Marcinkiewicz spaces. Fix a
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{A}\subset X(\mu)
and assume the following property: for all
A\in\mathscr{A}
, if
\mu(A)=\infty
, there exists
B\subset A
such that
0<\mu(B)<\infty
. For
\varphi\in\mathscr{C}
, set

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{M(\varphi)}=\sup_{t>0}\left\{\frac{1}{\varphi(t)}\int_{0}^{t}f_{\ast}(s)ds\right\},\quad\text{for all $f\in\mathscr{M}(X)$.}

(7.2)

If
1\leq p<\infty
, define
\mathrm{L}^{p,\infty}(X)=M(t^{1-\frac{1}{p}})
. It is known that
\mathrm{L}^{p,\infty}(X)
is a Banach space equipped with this norm for
1<p<\infty
(and such a norm is denoted by
\left\|\,\cdot\,\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,\infty}(X)}
). Furthermore, the following result holds.

Theorem 7.2

(Steigerwalt-White
[
55
]
, Steigerwalt
[
54
]
)
.

Assume that
\varphi(t)=o(t)
as
t\rightarrow\infty
. Then
M(\varphi)
is a norm and
(M(\varphi),\|\,\cdot\,\|)
is a Banach space.

Remark 7.3
.

In
[
55
]
, the authors first define the norm

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{M(\varphi)}=\sup\left\{\frac{1}{\varphi(\mu(A))}\int_{A}|f|d\mu:A\in\mathscr{A}_{1}\right\}

(7.3)

where

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{1}=\mathscr{A}\cap\left\{A:0<\mu(A)<\infty\right\}.

Then Theorem
7.2
holds with this norm without any restrictions on
\varphi
, and the authors show (Theorem
3.3
) that (
7.2
) and (
7.3
) coincide if either
(X,\mathscr{A},\mu)
is
\sigma
-finite or if
\varphi(t)=o(t)
as
t\rightarrow\infty
. Then they quote Mary Steingerwalt’s PhD thesis
[
54
]
where the result is proven without any hypothesis on
X
or
\varphi
. Notice that this result does not contradict the fact that
\mathrm{L}^{1,\infty}
is not a Banach space (even with
X=\mathbb{R}
and
\mu=\mathscr{L}^{1}
). Indeed, since
\mathrm{L}^{p,\infty}(X)=M(t^{1-\frac{1}{p}})
, we would have
\mathrm{L}^{1,\infty}(X)=M(1)
but the function
\varphi(t)=1
is not admissible (does not belong to
\mathscr{C}
) since it does not satisfy
\varphi(0)=0
. However, taking
p=\infty
, we formally get
\mathrm{L}^{\infty,\infty}(X)=M(t)=N(t)^{\ast}=\mathrm{L}^{1}(X)^{\ast}=\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(X)
, so there is no new Lorentz space corresponding to
p=q=\infty
.

Now, it is known that for all
1<p<\infty
and
1\leq q\leq\infty
, the dual space of
\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)
is
\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(X)
with

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1\,,\qquad\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}=1\,.

Moreover, for all measurable
f,g:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{\pm\infty\right\}
such that
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)
and
g\in\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(X)
, it holds

\displaystyle\left|\int_{X}fg\,d\mu\right|\leq\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}\left\|g\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(X)}.

For the generalised Lorentz space
N(\varphi)
, we have the following duality result.

Theorem 7.4

(Steigerwalt-White
[
55
]
, Steigerwalt
[
54
]
)
.

For all
(f,g)\in N(\varphi)\times M(\varphi)
, it holds
fg\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(X,\mu)
and

\displaystyle\left|\int_{X}fg\,d\mu\right|\leq\left\|f\right\|_{N(\varphi)}\left\|g\right\|_{M(\varphi)}.

In particular,
N(\varphi)^{\ast}=M(\varphi)
.

Notice that those results are consistent with the
\mathrm{L}^{p,1}-\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime},\infty}
duality: indeed
\mathrm{L}^{q,\infty}(X)=M(t^{\frac{1}{q}})
, so that
\mathrm{L}^{p,1}(X)^{\ast}=N(t^{\frac{1}{p}})^{\ast}=M(t^{\frac{1}{p}})=\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime},\infty}(X)
.

Finally, generalising both the Lorentz spaces and the Orlicz spaces, one can add a positive weight in the definition of
N(\varphi)
which gives a new norm
N(\varphi,\psi)
defined by

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{N(\varphi,\psi)}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi(\lambda_{f}(t))\psi(t)\,dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi^{\prime}(t)\Psi(f_{\ast}(t))\,dt\,,\quad\text{where }\Psi(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\psi(s)\,ds\,.

where the second identity follows from (
7.1
). Here, to make sure that
f
is a normal, we must assume that
\psi(t)>0
for all
t>0
and
\varphi\in\mathscr{C}
. However, even this generalisation does not permit to recover the Lorentz spaces
\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)
for
1<q<\infty
.

7.2
Another Generalised Lorentz Space

This appendix is linked to the content of the paper but not strictly needed; we include it for future reference.
Let
d>0
, and define the function
f=f_{d}:B(0,R)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
by

\displaystyle f(r)=\frac{1}{r}\left(1+\frac{1}{d}\left(\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{d}-1\right)\right).

We want to study a Lorentz space based on this function and obtain estimates reminiscent of Lemma
3.10
and uniform as
d\rightarrow 0
. Notice that when
d\rightarrow 0
, we have

\displaystyle f_{d}(r)=\frac{1}{r}\left(1+\frac{1}{d}\left(\exp\left(d\log\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)\right)-1\right)\right)\underset{d\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{r}\left(1+\log\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)\right),

which will correspond to the standard function in
M(\Lambda_{1})
, where
\Lambda_{1}
is defined in (
3.68
).
We have

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(r)=-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{d}\left(\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{d}-1\right)\right)-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{d}<0\,,

which shows that
f
is strictly decreasing. Therefore, we have for all
t>0

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{2}\left(B(0,R)\cap\left\{x:f(|x|)\right\}>t\right)=\mathscr{L}^{2}\left(B(0,R)\cap\left\{x:|x|<f^{-1}(t)\right\}\right)=\pi\left(f^{-1}(t)\right)^{2}.

If
u=u_{d}=f_{d}(|\,\cdot\,|)
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{2}(B(0,R)\cap\left\{x:|u(x)|>s\right\})\leq t\Longleftrightarrow\pi(f^{-1}(s))^{2}\leq t\Longleftrightarrow f^{-1}(s)\leq\sqrt{\frac{t}{\pi}}\Longleftrightarrow s\geq f\left(\sqrt{\frac{t}{\pi}}\right).

For
t\leq\pi R^{2}
, we infer that

\displaystyle u_{\ast}(t)=u_{d,\ast}=f\left(\sqrt{\frac{t}{\pi}}\right)=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}\left(1+\frac{1}{d}\left(\left(\frac{\pi R^{2}}{t}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}-1\right)\right),

while
u_{\ast}(t)=0
for
t\geq\pi R^{2}
.
Therefore, we have for all
t\leq\pi R^{2}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}u_{\ast}(s)ds

\displaystyle=\left[2\sqrt{\pi s}\left(1+\frac{1}{d}\left(\left(\frac{\pi R^{2}}{s}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}-1\right)\right)\right]_{0}^{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{s}}\left(\frac{\pi R^{2}}{s}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}ds

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\pi t}\left(1+\frac{1}{\beta}\left(\left(\frac{\pi R^{2}}{t}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}-1\right)\right)+\frac{2}{1-d}\sqrt{\pi}(\pi R^{2})^{\frac{d}{2}}t^{\frac{1-d}{2}},

(7.4)

while for all
t\geq\pi R^{2}
we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}u_{\ast}(s)ds=2\pi R+\frac{2\pi R}{1-d}\underset{d\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}4\pi R<\infty\,.

(7.5)

Now, let
\alpha<\dfrac{1}{2}
and define
\varphi:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
by

\displaystyle\varphi(t)=\sqrt{t}\left(\frac{1}{t^{\alpha}}-1\right)=t^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime\prime}(t)=\left(-\frac{1}{4}+\alpha^{2}\right)t^{-\frac{3}{2}-\alpha}+\frac{1}{4}t^{-\frac{3}{2}}<0\Longleftrightarrow t^{\alpha}<(1-(2\alpha)^{2})\Longleftrightarrow t<\left(1-(2\alpha)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}},

which shows that
\varphi
is concave in
[0,(1-(2\alpha)^{2})^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}]
. Notice that as
\alpha\rightarrow 0
, we have

\displaystyle(1-(2\alpha)^{2})^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}=e^{\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(1-(2\alpha)^{2}\right)}=e^{-4\alpha+O(\alpha^{2})}\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}1.

Therefore, for all
0<\beta<\dfrac{1}{4}
, define the concave function
K_{\beta}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
by

\displaystyle K_{\beta}(t)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{t}\left(1+\frac{1}{2\beta}\left(\left(\frac{\pi R^{2}}{t}\right)^{\beta}-1\right)\mathbf{1}_{[0,(1-(2\beta)^{2})^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\pi R^{2}]}(t)\right)+\frac{(\pi R^{2}t)^{\frac{1}{2}-\beta}}{1-2\beta}\,.

It is continuous at
0
, satisfies
K_{\beta}(0)=0
, and
K_{\beta}(t)>0
for all
t>0
. It is continuous everywhere except in
(1-(2\beta)^{2})^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\pi R^{2}
, but the function used in the definition of Lorentz spaces only needs to be continuous at
0
. Furthermore, the function
K_{\beta}
could be replaced by its continuous counter-part

\displaystyle K_{\beta}^{\ast}(t)=\sqrt{t}\left(1+\frac{1}{2\beta}\left(\left(\frac{\pi R^{2}}{t}\right)^{\beta}-1\right)\mathbf{1}_{[0,(1-(2\beta)^{2})^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\pi R^{2}]}(t)+\frac{1}{2\beta}\left(\left(\frac{1}{1-(2\beta)^{2}}-1\right)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{t>(1-(2\beta)^{2})^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\right\}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{(\pi R^{2}t)^{\frac{1}{2}-\beta}}{1-2\beta}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{t}\left(1+\frac{1}{2\beta}\left(\left(\frac{\pi R^{2}}{t}\right)^{\beta}-1\right)\mathbf{1}_{[0,(1-(2\beta)^{2})^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\pi R^{2}]}(t)+\left(\frac{2\beta}{1-(2\beta)^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{t>(1-(2\beta)^{2})^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\pi R^{2}\right\}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{(\pi R^{2}t)^{\frac{1}{2}-\beta}}{1-2\beta}

and the results stated below would be unchanged.
Furthermore, by (
7.2
), we deduce that for all
0<t\leq(1-d^{2})^{\frac{2}{d}}\pi R^{2}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K_{\frac{d}{2}}(t)}\int_{0}^{t}u_{\ast}(s)ds=2\sqrt{\pi},

and for all
(1-d^{2})^{\frac{2}{d}}\pi R^{2}<t\leq\pi R^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K_{\frac{d}{2}}(t)}\int_{0}^{t}u_{\ast}(s)ds=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}+\frac{(\pi R^{2}t)^{\frac{1-d}{2}}}{1-d}}\left(2\sqrt{\pi t}\left(1+\frac{1}{d}\left(\left(\frac{\pi R^{2}}{t}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}-1\right)\right)+\frac{2\sqrt{\pi}}{1-d}(\pi R^{2})^{\frac{d}{2}}t^{\frac{1-d}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(1-d^{2})^{\frac{1}{d}}\sqrt{\pi}R+\frac{(\pi R^{2})^{1-d}}{(1-d)^{2}(1+d)}(1-d^{2})^{\frac{1}{d}}}\left(2\pi R\left(1+\frac{1}{d}\left(\frac{1}{(1-d^{2})}-1\right)\right)+\frac{2\pi R}{1-d}\right).

We have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\left(\frac{1}{(1-d^{2})}-1\right)=d+O(d^{3})\underset{d\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0\,.

As above we have

\displaystyle(1-d^{2})^{d}=e^{d\log(1-d^{2})}=1-d^{3}+O(d^{5})\underset{d\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}1,

which implies that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1-d^{2})^{\frac{1}{d}}\sqrt{\pi}R+\frac{(\pi R^{2})^{1-d}}{(1-d)^{2}(1+d)}(1-d^{2})^{\frac{1}{d}}}\left(2\pi R\left(1+\frac{1}{d}\left(\frac{1}{(1-d^{2})}-1\right)\right)+\frac{2\pi R}{1-d}\right)

\displaystyle\underset{d\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}\frac{4\pi R}{\sqrt{\pi}R+\pi R^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\sqrt{\pi}}{1+\sqrt{\pi}R}<\infty.

Thus, for all
t>\pi R^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K_{\frac{d}{2}}(t)}\int_{0}^{t}u_{\ast}(s)ds

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi R}{\sqrt{t}+\frac{(\pi R^{2}t)^{\frac{1-d}{2}}}{1-d}}2\pi R\left(1+\frac{1}{1-d}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\pi R}{\sqrt{\pi}R+\frac{(\pi R^{2})^{{1-d}}}{1-d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{1-d}\right)\underset{d\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}\frac{4\pi R}{\sqrt{\pi}R+\pi R^{2}}=\frac{4\sqrt{\pi}}{1+\sqrt{\pi}R}<\infty.

Thanks to (
7.5
), we have

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{K_{\frac{d}{2}}(t)}\int_{0}^{t}u_{\ast}(s)ds=0.

We deduce that

\displaystyle\limsup_{d\rightarrow 0}\sup_{t>0}\frac{1}{K_{\frac{d}{2}}(t)}\int_{0}^{t}u_{\ast}(s)ds\leq 4\sqrt{\pi}<\infty,

showing that
u=u_{d}
is uniformly bounded in the Lorentz space
M(K_{\frac{d}{2}})
as
d\rightarrow 0
,
where for all
0<\beta<\frac{1}{4}
, we have

\displaystyle M(K_{\beta})=\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(B(0,R))\cap\left\{f:\|f\|_{M(K_{\beta})}=\sup_{t>0}\left(\frac{1}{K_{\beta}(t)}\int_{0}^{t}f_{\ast}(s)ds\right)<\infty\right\}.

Notice that when
\beta\rightarrow 0
, for all
t>0
, we have

\displaystyle K_{\beta}(t)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{t}\left(1+\frac{1}{2\beta}\left(e^{\beta\log\left(\frac{\pi R^{2}}{t}\right)}-1\right)\mathbf{1}_{[0,(1-(2\beta)^{2})^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\pi R^{2}]}(t)\right)+\frac{(\pi R^{2}t)^{\frac{1}{2}-\beta}}{1-2\beta}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{t}\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{\pi R^{2}}{t}\right)\mathbf{1}_{[0,(1-(2\beta)^{2})^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\pi R^{2}]}(t)+O\left(\beta\log^{2}\left(\frac{\pi R^{2}}{t}\right)\right)\right)+\frac{(\pi R^{2}t)^{\frac{1}{2}-\beta}}{1-2\beta}

\displaystyle\underset{\beta\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}\sqrt{t}\left(1+\sqrt{\pi}R+\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{\pi R^{2}}{t}\right)_{+}\right)=\sqrt{t}\left(1+\log_{+}\left(R\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}\right)\right)+\sqrt{\pi}R\sqrt{t}=\Lambda_{1}(t)+\sqrt{\pi}R\sqrt{t},

showing that
M(K_{0})=M(\Lambda_{1})
, where
\Lambda_{1}
is defined in (
3.68
). Therefore, we introduce the notation

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\log,\beta}(B(0,R))=M(K_{\beta}).

Likewise, we define the space
\mathrm{L}^{2,1}_{\log,\beta}(B(0,R))=N(K_{\beta})
by

\displaystyle N(K_{\beta})=\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(B(0,R))\cap\left\{f:\|f\|_{N(K_{\beta})}=\int_{0}^{\infty}K_{\beta}\left(\mathscr{L}^{2}\left(B(0,R)\cap\left\{x:|f(x)|>t\right\}\right)\right)dt<\infty\right\}.

Now, we will show a result similar to Lemma
3.10
(with a uniform estimate as
\beta\rightarrow 0
).
Fix
0<\beta<\dfrac{1}{4}
, let
x\in B(0,\frac{R}{2})
and
0<r<\frac{R}{4}
. By the co-area formula, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x)}|u(x)|dx=\int_{r}^{2r}\left(\rho\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(x)}|u|d\mathscr{H}^{1}\right)\frac{d\rho}{\rho}\leq\log(2)\inf_{r<\rho<2r}\left(\rho\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(x)}|u|d\mathscr{H}^{1}\right).

Therefore, there exists
\rho\in(r,2r)
such that

\displaystyle\rho\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(x)}|u|d\mathscr{H}^{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\log(2)}\int_{B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x)}|u(x)|dx.

We will show that if
u
is a holomorphic function on
B(0,R)
, then a
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\log^{\beta}}
control implies a
\mathrm{W}^{1,1}
control on
B(0,\alpha R)
for all
\alpha<1
. Since it seems not standard to us, we give a full proof of this claim (in fact, we know not whether such spaces where ever used in the past).

Using the
\mathrm{L}^{2,1}_{\log,\beta}/\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\log,\beta}
duality (Theorem
4.4

[
55
]
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x)}|u(x)|dx\leq\|1\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{log},{\beta}}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0))}\|u\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\mathrm{log},{\beta}}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0))}.

Notice that we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(t)=\mathscr{L}^{2}\left(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x)\cap\left\{x:1>t\right\}\right)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &3\pi r^{2}\qquad&&\text{if}\;\,t<1\\
&0\qquad&&\text{if}\;\,t\geq 1.\end{aligned}\right.

We have by definition

\displaystyle\|1\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{log}}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x))}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left((\lambda_{1}(t))^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{2\beta}\left(\left(\frac{\pi R^{2}}{\lambda_{1}(t)}\right)^{\beta}-1\right)\mathbf{1}_{[0,(1-(2\beta)^{2})^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\pi R^{2}]}(\lambda_{1}(t))\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{(\pi R^{2}\lambda_{1}(t))^{\frac{1}{2}-\beta}}{1-2\beta}\right)dt

\displaystyle=\sqrt{3\pi}r\left(1+\frac{1}{2\beta}\left(\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{R}{r}\right)^{2\beta}\right)-1\right)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\sqrt{3}r<(1-(2\beta)^{2})^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}R\right\}}+\frac{\pi(\sqrt{3}rR)^{1-2\beta}}{1-2\beta}.

Finally, we deduce that

\displaystyle\rho\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(x)}|u|d\mathscr{H}^{1}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{3\pi}}{\log(2)}r\left(1+\frac{1}{2\beta}\left(\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{R}{r}\right)^{2\beta}-1\right)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\sqrt{3}r<(1-(2\beta)^{2})^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}R\right\}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\pi}{\log(2)}\frac{(\sqrt{3}rR)^{1-2\beta}}{1-2\beta}\bigg)\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log,\beta}^{2,\infty}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x))},

and
a fortiori
that for all
x\in B(0,\frac{R}{2})
and
0<r\leq\frac{R}{2}
such that
B(x,2r)\subset B(0,R)
, there exists
\rho\in[r,2r]
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(x)}|u|d\mathscr{H}^{1}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{3\pi}}{\log(2)}\left(1+\frac{1}{2\beta}\left(\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{R}{r}\right)^{2\beta}-1\right)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\sqrt{3}r<(1-(2\beta)^{2})^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}R\right\}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\pi}{\log(2)}\frac{1}{r^{2\beta}}\frac{(\sqrt{3}R)^{1-2\beta}}{1-2\beta}\bigg)\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log,\beta}^{2,\infty}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x))}.

(7.6)

Thanks to this result, we will now be able to show a variant of a lemma presented first in
[
26
]
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
u:B(0,R)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a holomorphic function and fix some
0\leq\alpha<1
and
0\leq\beta<\dfrac{1}{4}
. Assume that
u\in\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\log,{\beta}}(B(0,R))
. Then
u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,\alpha R))
and there exists a universal constant
\Gamma_{0}

(
independent of
\alpha
and
\beta
)
such that

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,\alpha R))}\leq\Gamma_{1}\frac{\alpha}{(1-\alpha)^{1+2\beta}}\left(1+R^{1-4\beta}+\frac{1}{2\beta}\left(\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)^{2\beta}-1\right)\right)\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log,{\beta}}^{2,\infty}(B(0,R))}.

Proof.

Write

\displaystyle u(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}z^{n}.

Notice that
u=\partial_{z}v
, where

\displaystyle v(z)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}b_{n}z^{n}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{a_{n-1}}{n}z^{n}.

(7.7)

First, using
the estimate (
3.4
) applied to
\nabla v
at a point
z\in\partial B(0,\alpha R)
with
r=\frac{1}{2}(1-\alpha)R
, we deduce by the mean-value formula that for some
\rho\in[\frac{1}{2}(1-\alpha)R,(1-\alpha)R]
, we have

\displaystyle|\nabla v(z)|=|2\,\partial_{z}v(z)|=\left|\frac{1}{\pi\rho}\int_{\partial B(z,\rho)}\partial_{\zeta}v(\zeta)d\zeta\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{\log(2)}\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\frac{1}{\rho}\left(1+\frac{1}{2\beta}\left(\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)^{2\beta}-1\right)\right)+\frac{1}{\log(2)}\frac{1}{\rho\,r^{2\beta}}\frac{(\sqrt{3}R)^{1-2\beta}}{1-2\beta}\right)\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log,\beta}^{2,\infty}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{\log(2)}\left(\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\frac{1}{(1-\alpha)R}\left(1+\frac{1}{2\beta}\left(\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)^{2\beta}-1\right)\right)+\frac{1}{((1-\alpha)R)^{1+2\beta}}\frac{(\sqrt{3}R)^{1-2\beta}}{1-2\beta}\right)\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log,\beta}^{2,\infty}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x))},

(7.8)

where we used
|\nabla v|^{2}=4|\partial_{z}v|^{2}
by the holomorphy of
v
. Therefore, by integrating by parts, we deduce since
u
and
\overline{u}
are harmonic that

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,\alpha R)}|u(z)|^{2}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{B(0,\alpha R)}|\nabla v|^{2}dx=\frac{1}{2}\int_{B(0,\alpha R)}\dive\left(\overline{v}\nabla v\right)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial B(0,\alpha R)}\overline{v}\,{\partial_{\nu}{v}}\,d\mathscr{H}^{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial B(0,\alpha R)}\overline{(v-v_{\alpha R})}\partial_{\nu}v\,d\mathscr{H}^{1},

(7.9)

where for all
0<\rho<R
, we have

\displaystyle v_{\rho}={\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.57222pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.91281pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.30664pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.13547pt}}\!\int}_{\mkern-10.0mu\partial B_{\rho}(0)}v\,d\mathscr{H}^{1}.

Thanks to the
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
bound (
3.3
) and the Sobolev embedding
\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}(S^{1})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{1}(B(0,1))
, there exists a uniform constant
C_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,\alpha R))}^{2}=\left|\int_{\alpha R}\overline{(v-{v}_{\partial B_{\alpha R}})}\partial_{\nu}v\,d\mathscr{H}^{1}\right|\leq\left\|v-v_{\alpha R}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(\partial B_{\alpha R}(0))}\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\partial B_{\alpha R(0)})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\alpha R\left\|v\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial B_{\alpha R}(0))}\times\frac{8}{\log(2)}\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\frac{1}{(1-\alpha)R}\left(1+\frac{1}{2\beta}\left(\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)^{2\beta}-1\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{((1-\alpha)R)^{1+2\beta}}\frac{(\sqrt{3}R)^{1-2\beta}}{1-2\beta}\bigg)\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log,\beta}^{2,\infty}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8C_{0}}{\log(2)}\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\frac{\alpha}{(1-\alpha)}\left(1+\frac{1}{2\beta}\left(\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)^{2\beta}-1\right)\right)+\frac{\alpha}{(1-\alpha)^{1+2\beta}}\frac{3^{\frac{1}{2}-\beta}R^{1-4\beta}}{1-2\beta}\bigg)

\displaystyle\times\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,\alpha R))}\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log,\beta}^{2,\infty}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x))}.

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,\alpha R))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8C_{0}}{\log(2)}\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\frac{\alpha}{(1-\alpha)}\left(1+\frac{1}{2\beta}\left(\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)^{2\beta}-1\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha}{(1-\alpha)^{1+2\beta}}\frac{3^{\frac{1}{2}-\beta}R^{1-4\beta}}{1-2\beta}\bigg)\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log,\beta}^{2,\infty}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(x))}

which concludes the proof of the lemma since
\beta<\dfrac{1}{4}
.
∎

Corollary 7.6
.

Let
\left\{\beta_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,\frac{1}{4})
such that
\beta_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
.
Assume that
\left\{u_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}:B(0,R)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a sequence of holomorphic functions bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}_{\log,\beta_{k}}(B(0,R))
,
i.e.
such that

\displaystyle\Lambda=\limsup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left\|u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{\log,\beta_{k}}^{2,\infty}(B(0,R))}<\infty.

Then
\left\{u_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,\alpha R))
for all
0<\alpha<1
and for all
0<\alpha<1
, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left\|u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,\alpha R))}\leq\Gamma_{1}\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}\left(1+\log(2)+R+\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)\right)\Lambda<\infty.

(7.10)

Proof.

For all fixed
0<\alpha<1
, as
\beta\rightarrow 0
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\beta}\left(\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)^{2\beta}-1\right)=\frac{1}{2\beta}\left(e^{2\beta\log\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)}-1\right)\underset{\beta\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}\log\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)\leq\log(2)+\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)

which concludes the proof using Lemma
7.5
.
∎
